On the well-posedness of a Hele-Shaw-like system resulting from an inverse geometry problem formulated through a shape optimization setting Journal: a journal.
Julius Fergy Tiongson Rabago, Masato Kimura

Abstract
The purpose of this study is twofold. First, we revisit a shape optimization reformulation of a prototypical shape inverse problem and briefly propose a simple yet efficient numerical approach for solving the corresponding minimization problem. Second, we examine the existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence of a classical solution to a Hele-Shaw-like system, which is derived from the continuous setting of a numerical discretization of the shape optimization reformulation for the shape inverse problem. The analysis is based on the methods developed by G. I. Bizhanova and V. A. Solonnikov in “On Free Boundary Problems for Second Order Parabolic Equations” (Algebra Anal. 12 (6) (2000) 98–139), and by V. A. Solonnikov in “Lectures on Evolution Free Boundary Problems: Classical Solutions” (Lect. Notes Math., Springer, 2003, pp. 123–175).



1
Introduction

1.1
A Hele-Shaw-like system

In this study, we examine a Hele-Shaw-like system that arises from a shape optimization reformulation of an inverse geometry problem.
We prove the existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence of a classical solution to the quasi-stationary moving boundary problem derived from the discretized version of the optimization algorithm used to solve the shape optimization problem.
The inverse problem occurs in contexts such as non-destructive testing to identify inclusions or voids in solids.
The available information or data are collected from a part of the solid’s surface (geometrical boundary), with the goal of determining the inclusion within the interior of the solid.

Let
T
be a positive number and
t\in[0,T]
.
Let
\Omega(t)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d\in{2,3}
, be a bounded annular domain with a sufficiently smooth boundary
\partial\Omega(t)=\Gamma(t)\cup\Sigma
, consisting of two non-intersecting parts: a fixed boundary
\Sigma
and a free boundary
\Gamma(t)
.
We assume that
\Sigma
lies outside the surface
\Gamma(t)
, and denote by
\nu{(t)}
the unit normal vector to
\Gamma(t)
directed inward the domain
\Omega(t)
; that is, it goes in the same direction with the outward unit normal vector to
\Sigma
.
The main problem we consider here is the following: given a pair of
positive-valued
functions
f=f(x,t)
and
g=g(x,t)
defined on
\Sigma
, for
t>0
, and an initial geometry
\Gamma_{\circ}
of the moving boundary, we seek to find a surface
\Gamma(t)
and two functions
u_{\text{D}}=u_{\text{D}}(x,t)
and
u_{\text{N}}=u_{\text{N}}(x,t)
satisfying the following system of partial differential equations (PDEs):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\Delta u_{\text{D}}(x,t)&=&0,&\quad x\in\Omega(t),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
u_{\text{D}}(x,t)&=&f(x,t),&\quad x\in\Sigma,\quad t\in[0,T],\\
u_{\text{D}}(x,t)&=&0,&\quad x\in\Gamma(t),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
-\Delta u_{\text{N}}(x,t)&=&0,&\quad x\in\Omega(t),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial\nu}u_{\text{N}}(x,t)&=&g(x,t),&\quad x\in\Sigma,\quad t\in[0,T],\\
u_{\text{N}}(x,t)&=&0,&\quad x\in\Gamma(t),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
V_{n}(x,t)&=&-\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial\nu}\left(u_{\text{D}}(x,t)-u_{\text{N}}(x,t)\right),&\quad x\in\Gamma(t),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
\Gamma(0)&=&\Gamma_{\circ},&\end{array}\right.

(1)

where
\partial/\partial{\nu}=\partial/\partial{\nu(t)}
is the inward normal derivative operator on
\Gamma(t)
while
V_{n}(x,t)
represents the velocity of movement of
\Gamma(t)
in the direction of the normal
\nu{(t)}
for all
t>0
.
As alluded above, the problem originates from a reformulation of a shape inverse problem into a shape optimization setting.
Shape optimization is a well-established technique extensively applied in engineering sciences for addressing shape identification problems.
The modern mathematical theory of shape optimization was laid in monographs
[
20
,
38
,
63
]
.

Motivation and review of related work.
As far as we are concerned, shape inverse problems are typically solved numerically from an optimization perspective through shape optimization settings; see, for example,
[
21
,
57
]
and the references therein.
The model problem from which the Hele-Shaw-like system (
1
) was derived is a specific case of the more general conductivity reconstruction problem.
This type of problem is known to be severely ill-posed in the sense of Hadamard
[
21
]
.
Nevertheless, it has been widely studied in literature, both theoretically and numerically; see, e.g.,
[
21
,
2
,
5
,
4
,
7
,
9
,
12
,
39
,
40
]
and references therein.
For instance, the existence and uniqueness of the solution to the problem based on boundary measurement data have been studied by several authors.; see, e.g.,
[
4
,
7
,
9
,
40
]
.
Aside from these aforementioned investigations, shape optimization reformulations of shape inverse problems, in general, are hardly examined from a different theoretical and numerical point of view.
The main purpose of this investigation, therefore, is to carry out a rigorous analysis of the existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence on the data of the classical solution of (
1
) in short-time horizon.
We assert that, to our knowledge, little to no work has been done on the well-posedness of the shape optimization problem from which (
1
) is derived, especially in the direction of our current study.
We believe that the system (
1
) itself, originating from a shape optimization context, which in turn was derived from a shape inverse problem, is novel.
Therefore, the analysis conducted in this work, inspired by the work of Bizhanova and Solonnikov
[
10
]
and Solonnikov
[
61
]
, offers a new perspective.
We emphasize, however, that Escher and Simonett demonstrated the existence and uniqueness of classical solutions for the multidimensional expanding Hele-Shaw problem in
[
25
]
.
In their study, the problem is formulated over the exterior boundary component, while the interior component remains fixed.
It is also worth mentioning that geometric flows related to shape optimization problems similar to Bernoulli-type free boundary problems are explored by Cardaliaguet and Ley in
[
14
]
.
The evolution process examined in their work involves a combination of curvature and Hele-Shaw type nonlocal terms.
They introduce the concept of generalized set solutions, drawing heavily from viscosity solutions (see, e.g.,
[
15
]
or
[
30
, Chap. 2, p. 69]
), which diverges from our current focus.
The main result in
[
14
]
is the inclusion preservation principle for generalized solutions, which guarantees the existence, uniqueness, and stability of the flow.
In addition, the study examines the asymptotic behavior, establishing that the solutions converge to a generalized Bernoulli exterior free-boundary problem.
Meanwhile, from geometric analysis perspective, Plotnikov and Sokołowski
[
51
]
recently provided a comprehensive review of existing results in shape optimization emphasizing the theory of gradient flows for objective functions and their regularizations.
For the sake of simplicity, their mathematical exposition is confined to two spatial dimensions, and they illustrate their findings through a model problem, leading to an initial result concerning convergence in shape optimization.
In an earlier work
[
52
]
, Plotnikov and Sokołowski rigorously established the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem arising from a
penalized
gradient flow and conducted an in-depth examination of the case involving the Kohn-Vogelius cost functional.

Remark 1.1
.

The analysis conducted in this investigation can be applied to a class of shape optimization problems.
Specifically, the same methodology presented in the subsequent sections can be utilized to prove the well-posedness of (
1
), where the normal velocity of the boundary of the evolving domain is given by
V_{n}(x,t)=-G(x,u(x))
for
x\in\Gamma(t)
and
t>0
.
Here,
G
can be the exact shape gradient of a cost functional such as (
3
) and is assumed to be positive for all
x\in\Omega(t)
, for all
t>0
.
Additionally, this approach is applicable to other quasi-stationary moving boundary problems that arise from the discretization of numerical shape optimization schemes used to solve the shape optimal control reformulation of the inverse geometry problem, such as
L^{2}
tracking of boundary data, under appropriate assumptions on the kernel of the shape gradient of the associated cost functional.

In the next subsection, we will explore the derivation of system (
1
) and its connection to a shape optimization problem to provide further context and motivation.

1.2
Derivation of the system

Consider a bounded (simply connected) domain
D
with boundary
\Sigma:=\partial{D}
and assume the existence of a simply connected open set
{{\omega}}
such that
\overline{{\omega}}\subset D
, composed of a material with a constant conductivity that is essentially different from the constant conductivity of the material in the annular subregion
\Omega:=D\setminus\overline{{\omega}}
.
We are interested in determining the shape and location of the inclusion from the knowledge of the Cauchy data of the electrical potential
u
on the boundary
\Sigma
.
In other words, through the concept of non-destructive testing and evaluation, we want to identify the inclusion given that the pair of boundary data
f=u\big|_{\Sigma}
and
g=(\nabla u\cdot\nu)\big|_{\Sigma}
is known, where
\nu
is the outward unit normal to
\Sigma
.
As alluded in the previous section, the problem under consideration is a special case of the general conductivity reconstruction problem and has been extensively studied in the literature, particularly in the context of inverse problems.

Various numerical techniques can solve the inverse problem, including applying shape optimization methods as demonstrated by Roche and Sokołowski in
[
57
]
, and by Eppler and Harbrecht in
[
21
]
.
The former study utilized first-order shape optimization algorithms to analyze and numerically address the proposed shape optimization formulation.
On the other hand,
[
21
]
investigated second-order methods related to the previous study, developed and applied by the same authors in their prior work.
Here, we introduce an alternative numerical approach closely aligned with the shape optimization techniques employed in these references. Notably, our method does not directly require knowledge of the exact shape gradient typically used in shape optimization procedures.
Nonetheless, our approach can be seen as stemming from the specific shape optimization formulation in
[
21
,
57
]
.
In essence, our method simplifies the gradient-based optimization procedure outlined in
[
57
]
.
In fact, we will choose a suitable descent direction that improves descent speed for the relevant shape optimization problem.
It is important to highlight in advance that our method operates as a Lagrangian-type numerical scheme, utilizing finite element methods.
This is in contrast to the approaches used in
[
21
,
57
]
which rely on the concepts of boundary integral equations.

\Sigma
\omega
\boldsymbol{D}
\Omega
\Gamma
\nu

Figure 1:
Conceptual model

To further explain the idea about our method, we first review the shape optimization formulation proposed in
[
57
]
and
[
21
]
for the model problem introduced previously; see Figure
1
for a conceptual model.
To determine the inclusion
{{\omega}}
bounded by
\Gamma
, we apply the concept of nondestructive testing and evaluation, a well-known technique used in engineering and related sciences to evaluate the properties of a material without causing damage.
That is, we identify
{{\omega}}
by measuring, for a given current distribution
g\in H^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
, the voltage distribution
f\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma)
at the boundary
\Sigma
.
This means that we are seeking to find a connected domain
\Omega:=D\setminus\overline{{\omega}}
and an associated harmonic function
u
which satisfies the overdetermined boundary value problem:

-\Delta u=0\ \text{in $\Omega$},\qquad u=0\ \text{on $\Gamma$},\qquad u=f\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla u\cdot{\nu}=g\ \text{on $\Sigma$}.

(2)

Equation (
2
) admits a solution only when the true inclusion (cavity or void)
1
1

1

While the terms inclusion, cavity, and void are related and commonly used in various contexts, such as materials science, geology, and engineering, we have used them interchangeably in this discussion.

{{\omega}}
is considered.

We recall in the following theorem a key result about identifiability for this inverse problem.
It guarantees the uniqueness of the inclusion
{{\omega}}
and consequently, the potential
u
from a pair of boundary data
(f,g)\neq(0,0)
.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
9
]
)
.

The Cauchy pair
(f,g)\neq(0,0)
uniquely determine
\Gamma
and
u
satisfying (
2
).

In the context of thermal imaging, the Dirichlet data
f
represent a prescribed surface heat, and correspondingly,
g
stands for the surface heat flux of the material.
This perspective reinterprets the state variable
u
as the internal thermal distribution within the material.
It is important to note that the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition applied to the unknown shape
\Gamma
models a perfectly conducting inclusion.
Note that when the Dirichlet data on the surface
\Sigma
is positive, the solution
u
to (
2
) is also positive due to the maximum principle and unique continuation property
[
33
]
.
This implies that the outgoing flux
\nabla u\cdot\nu
on
\Sigma
is positive, while on
\Gamma
, the outgoing flux is negative.
Moreover, because
u
takes homogeneous Dirichlet data on the surface
\Sigma
of the unknown inclusion, we have the identity
\left|{\nabla u}\right|\equiv-\nabla u\cdot\nu
on
\Sigma
.
For a non-conducting inclusion, a Neumann boundary condition governs the problem.
Additionally, if the conductivity of the inclusion differs from that of the surrounding material or object
\Omega
, an inverse transmission problem arises.

Roche and Sokołowski in
[
57
]
proposed a shape optimization reformulation of (
2
) as follows:

J(\Omega)=\int_{\Omega}{\left|{\nabla(u_{\text{D}}-u_{\text{N}})}\right|^{2}}\,{{d}}x=\int_{\Sigma}{\left(g-\frac{\partial{u_{\text{D}}}}{\partial{\nu}}\right)\left(u_{\text{N}}-f\right)}\,{{d}}s\to\inf,

(3)

subject to the underlying well-posed state problems

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{\text{D}}=0\quad\text{in $\Omega$},\qquad u_{\text{D}}=f\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad u_{\text{D}}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$},\\
-\Delta u_{\text{N}}=0\quad\text{in $\Omega$},\qquad\nabla u_{\text{N}}\cdot\nu=g\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad u_{\text{N}}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}.\end{aligned}\right.

(4)

In (
3
), the infimum has to be taken over all domains containing an inclusion with sufficiently regular boundary.
The existence of optimal solutions with respect to the shape optimization problem (
3
) was established in
[
57
]
.

Remark 1.3
.

1.

The proof of the existence of an optimal solution to (
3
) over some set of admissible domains is based on properties of harmonic functions in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and uses the same arguments as those given by Šverák
[
66
]
for a slightly different shape optimization problem.In particular, the method cannot be directly applied in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d\geqslant 3
.

2.

To establish an existence result in dimension
d=3
, we need to assume that the family of admissible domains, say
U_{\text{ad}}
, meets the following compactness criterion (see, e.g.,
[
38
, Chap. 2]
).

Given any sequence
\{\Omega_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset U_{\text{ad}}
, there exists a subsequence
\{\Omega_{n_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}=\{D\setminus\overline{\omega}_{n_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that the sequence of compacts
\{\omega_{n_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in the Hausdorff metric to the compact
\omega
.
Moreover, the associated sequence of metric projections
P_{n}:H_{0}^{1}(D)\to H_{0}^{1}(\Omega_{n})
converges strongly to the metric projection
P:H_{0}^{1}(D)\to H_{0}^{1}(D\setminus\overline{\omega})
.
For such a family of admissible domains, there is a solution to the shape identification problem in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
The hypothesis generally requires some form of uniform regularity for the boundaries
\Gamma
, such as the cone condition or more intricate conditions related to capacity (refer to
[
20
,
38
]
).
For further details, see Henrot, Horn, and Sokołowski
[
37
]
for an overview of stability results for the Dirichlet problem, and Bucur and Zolésio
[
11
]
for an approach to existence problems. Additionally,
[
38
]
provides more discussion on the existence of optimal shapes in shape optimization problems.
For a recent related result on the existence issue related to Problem (
3
), see
[
6
]
.

3.

For technical purposes, in order to establish the existence optimal solution, we require that any admissible inclusion or obstacle
\omega\Subset D
satisfies the condition that there exists a constant
d_{0}>0
such that
\operatorname{d}(x,\partial D)>d_{0}
, for all
x\in\omega
.
This condition is assumed without further notice throughout the paper.
We emphasize that such a requirement is also needed for computing the shape derivatives of the states
u
and the cost function
J
with respect to
\omega
(see, e.g.,
[
6
]
).

The equivalence between (
2
) and (
3
) issues from the first-order necessary condition of a minimizer of the shape functional
J(\Omega)
; that is,
2
2

2

Here, the representation of the shape gradient is non-conventional. We must note that
\nu
denotes the inward unit normal to
\Gamma
and
\partial/\partial\nu
is the inward normal derivative on
\Gamma
.

\displaystyle{d}J(\Omega)[\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}]=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{J(\Omega_{\varepsilon})-J(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}=\left.\frac{{d}}{{d}\varepsilon}J(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\right|_{\varepsilon=0}=\int_{\Gamma}{G\nu\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}}\,{{d}}s,

where

G=\left(\frac{\partial u_{\text{D}}}{\partial\nu}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{\partial u_{\text{N}}}{\partial\nu}\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{\partial u_{\text{D}}}{\partial\nu}+\frac{\partial u_{\text{N}}}{\partial\nu}\right)\left(\frac{\partial u_{\text{D}}}{\partial\nu}-\frac{\partial u_{\text{N}}}{\partial\nu}\right)=:G^{+}G^{-},

has to hold for all sufficiently smooth perturbation fields
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}
.
Here,
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
stands for a perturbation of
\Omega
along
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}
that vanishes on the known surface
\Sigma
.
Obviously, by the calculus of variations, the above statement implies the necessary condition
\nabla u_{\text{N}}\cdot\nu\equiv\nabla u_{\text{D}}\cdot\nu
on
\Gamma
.
For details on how to compute
{d}J(\Omega)[\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}]
, and for more discussion on shape optimization methods in general, we refer readers to
[
20
]
and
[
63
]
.

To resolve the minimization problem (
3
), a typical approach is to utilize the kernel of the shape derivative
{d}J(\Omega)[\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}]
, the so-called shape gradient (see, e.g.,
[
20
, Thm. 3.6, p. 479–480]
), in a gradient-based descent algorithm.
For example, for a smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
and sufficiently regular states
u_{\text{D}}
and
u_{\text{N}}
, one can take
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{0}}\not\equiv\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}=-G{\nu}\in L^{2}(\Gamma)^{d}
.
This implies that, by formal expansion, and
t>0
sufficiently small, the following inequality holds

\displaystyle J(\Omega_{t})=J(\Omega)+t\left.\frac{{d}}{{d}\varepsilon}J(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\right|_{\varepsilon=0}+O(t^{2})

\displaystyle=J(\Omega)+t\int_{\Gamma}{G\nu\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}}\,{{d}}s+O(t^{2})

\displaystyle=J(\Omega)-t\int_{\Gamma}{\left|{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}}\right|^{2}}\,{{d}}s+O(t^{2})<J(\Omega).

Hence, in practice, opting for the descent direction
\mathbf{V}=-G\nu
is straightforward.
However, there are cases where one can deviate from this choice.
For instance, if
f>0
, we can choose
\mathbf{V}=-G^{-}\nu
, which still provides a descent direction for
J
.
Indeed, in such a case,
G^{+}<0
on
\Gamma
.
Hence, it is evident that when
\mathbf{V}=-G^{-}\nu
, the following holds:

\displaystyle J(\Omega_{t})

\displaystyle=J(\Omega)+t\int_{\Gamma}{{G^{+}}{G^{-}}\nu\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}}\,{{d}}s+O(t^{2})

\displaystyle=J(\Omega)-t\int_{\Gamma}\underbrace{{G^{+}}}_{>\ 0}\left|{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}}\right|^{2}\,{d}s+O(t^{2})<J(\Omega).

(5)

A naive idea to numerically solve (
2
) then involves an iterative procedure that minimizes the cost functional
J
.
For simplicity, let
{\Delta{t}}>0
be a fixed number.
Given an initial shape
\Gamma_{\circ}
, for all
k=1,2,\ldots
, denote the free boundary at iteration
k
as
\Gamma_{k}
.
Then, the update at iteration
k+1
is given by
\Gamma_{k+1}=\{x+\Delta{t}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{k}(x)\mid x\in\Gamma_{k}\}
, for
k=0,1,\ldots
. Accordingly, the main steps for computing the
k
th free boundary
\Gamma_{k}
using
J
via a naive gradient-based descent method is given in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1
.

Boundary variation algorithm
:

–
Initialization

Fix a number
\Delta{t}>0
and choose an initial shape
\Gamma_{\circ}
.

–
Iteration

For
k=0,1,\ldots

1.

Solve the corresponding variational formulations of (
4
) on
\Omega_{k}
.

2.

Define
V_{n,k}:=\nabla u_{\text{D},k}\cdot\nu_{k}-\nabla u_{\text{N},k}\cdot\nu_{k}
on
\Gamma^{k}
.

3.

Set
\Gamma_{k+1}=\{x+\Delta{t}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{k}(x)\mid x\in\Gamma_{k}\}
.

–
Stop Test

Repeat
Iteration
until convergence.

The identification of the unknown boundary
\Gamma
in (
2
) according to Algorithm
1
describes a similar evolutionary equation for the Hele-Shaw-like system (
1
) (see, e.g.,
[
16
,
23
,
56
]
for the classical Hele-Shaw flow problem) in discrete setting.
Indeed, let
T>0
be a given final time of interest (this can be interpreted as the condition for
Stop Test
in Algorithm
1
),
N_{T}
be a fixed positive integer, and set the time discretization step-size as
\Delta{t}:=T/N_{T}
.
For each time-step index
k=0,1,\cdots,N_{T}
, we let
t_{k}=k\Delta{t}
and denote the time-discretized domain and free boundary by
\Omega_{k}\approx\Omega(k\Delta{t})
and
\Gamma_{k}\approx\Gamma(k\Delta{t})
, respectively, and the associated time-discretized functions as
u_{\text{D},k}\approx u_{\text{D}}(\cdot,k\Delta{t})
and
u_{\text{N},k}\approx u_{\text{N}}(\cdot,k\Delta{t})
.
Then, given an initial free boundary
\Gamma_{\circ}
, Algorithm
1
reduces to solving the moving boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\Delta u_{\text{D},k}&=&0&\quad\text{in}\ \Omega_{k},\\
u_{\text{D},k}&=&f&\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
u_{\text{D},k}&=&0&\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}_{k},\\
-\Delta u_{\text{N},k}&=&0&\quad\text{in}\ \Omega_{k},\\
\nabla u_{\text{N},k}\cdot\nu_{k}&=&g&\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
u_{\text{N},k}&=&0&\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}_{k},\\
V_{n,k}&=&-\left(\nabla{u_{\text{D},k}}\cdot\nu_{k}-\nabla{u_{\text{N},k}}\cdot\nu_{k}\right)&\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}_{k},\\
\Gamma(0)&=&\Gamma_{\circ}.&\end{array}\right.

(6)

Notice that the discrete system (
6
) is clearly the time-discretized version of system (
1
) with
f=f(x)
and
g=g(x)
,
x\in\Sigma
.

1.3
Extended-regularized normal flows for shape identifications

In various studies, it has been observed that directly using
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}=-G\nu
can lead to unwanted oscillations on the boundary, which may cause numerical instabilities, especially in finite element methods.
To address this issue, one typically employs a Sobolev gradient method in numerical optimization (see
[
50
, Chap. 11]
), combined with perimeter or surface area penalization.
A typical approach includes the traction method
[
8
]
or the
H^{1}
gradient method
[
17
]
, both widely used smoothing techniques in shape optimization problems
[
48
]
.
On the other hand, it is worth noting that treating inverse geometry problems as shape optimization problems presents significant challenges due to their inherent ill-posed nature
[
21
]
.
These problems are prone to instability and are frequently stabilized through numerical regularization techniques
[
1
]
.
For example, in a related work
[
53
]
, the authors stabilized their shape optimization algorithm, similar to (
2
), by employing perimeter regularization.

Following the discussed idea, Algorithm
1
can be modified to formulate a more stable numerical shape optimization algorithm to solve (
2
).
Given an initial shape
\Omega_{0}
and denoting the domain shape at iteration
k
as
\Omega_{k}
, the update at iteration
k+1
is
\Omega_{k+1}=\{x+\Delta{t}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{k}(x)\mid x\in\Omega_{k}\}
, where
{\Delta{t}}>0
is a sufficiently small step size and
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{k}
is the descent deformation field at iteration
k
.
The main steps for computing
\Omega_{k}
through formulation (
3
) via a gradient-based descent method in Hilbert space can then be summarized in the following optimization algorithm.

Algorithm 2
.

Domain variation algorithm
:

–
Initialization

Fix a number
\Delta{t}>0
and choose an initial shape
\Omega_{\circ}
.

–
Iteration

For
k=0,1,\ldots

1.

Solve the corresponding variational formulations of (
4
) on
\Omega_{k}
.

2.

Compute
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{k}\in V(\Omega_{k})^{d}
by solving the variational equation

a(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{k},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\varphi}})=\int_{\Gamma_{k}}{\tilde{G}_{k}\nu_{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\varphi}}\,ds},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\varphi}}\in V(\Omega_{k})^{d},

where
V(\Omega_{k}):=\{\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{k})\mid\varphi=0\ \text{on $\Sigma$}\}
and
a
is a bounded and coercive bilinear form on
V(\Omega_{k})^{d}
.

3.

Set
\Omega_{k+1}=\{x+\Delta{t}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{k}(x)\mid x\in\Omega_{k}\}
.

–
Stop Test

Repeat
Iteration
until convergence.

In Step 2 of
Iteration
, we can take
\tilde{G}
as either exactly
G
or
{G^{-}}
, depending on the sign of
f
and
g
.
Meanwhile, for the
Stop Test
, we simply terminate the algorithm after a finite number of iterations.

As emphasized in previous subsections, our main intent in this exposition is to prove the existence and uniqueness of classical solution to (
1
).
Before delving with this problem, however, we first exhibit a numerical example in two spatial dimensions illustrating the feasibility of the proposed optimization algorithm with the choice of preconditioned descent vector field
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}
obtained through the vector
{G^{-}}{\nu}
.
Here, we define
a(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\varphi}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\psi}}):=(\nabla{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\varphi}}},\nabla{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\psi}}})_{\Omega}
, for
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\varphi}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\psi}}\in V(\Omega)
.
Hereafter, we shall refer to our algorithm as quasi-stationary Stefan type-scheme or QSSTS as it also provides a kind of a comoving mesh method
[
59
,
62
]
for (
1
).

Example 1.4
.

Using Algorithm
2
3
3

3

Here, we used a non-uniform time step size to clearly highlight the potential of taking
\tilde{G}=G^{-}
. In fact, we determine the step size using a backtracking line search procedure, calculated as
{\Delta{t}_{k}}=cJ(\Omega_{k})/|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{k}|^{2}_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega_{k})}
at each time step, where
c>0
is a scaling factor. This factor is adjusted to prevent reversed triangles within the mesh after updating (see
[
54
, p. 281]
).
, we reconstruct various cavity shapes under the following setup.
The specimen under investigation is a circular disk with a unit radius, centered at the origin.
For the input data, we set
f\equiv 1
on
\Sigma
, while synthetic data is used for extra boundary measurements on the outer part of
\Sigma
.
To avoid “inverse crimes" (see
[
13
, p. 179]
), the synthetic data is generated using a different numerical scheme, employing a larger number of discretization points and
{P2}
finite element basis functions in the FreeFem++ code
[
36
]
.
In the inversion procedure, all variational problems are solved using
{P1}
finite elements with coarse meshes.

We contrast the traditional shape optimization approach (utilizing
\tilde{G}=G
) with the proposed method (employing
\tilde{G}=G^{-}
).
The geometry of the initial free boundary
\Gamma_{\circ}
is a circle centered at the origin with a radius of
0.9
.
The experimental findings, outlined in Figure
2
, indicate a clear advantage of the proposed method (QSSTS) over the conventional shape optimization method (SO).
The QSSTS method not only achieves faster identification of the shape and location of the unknown inclusion, but also does so with greater accuracy in general.
From these numerical results, one can expect that the proposed method will exhibit the same advantages over traditional shape optimization approaches, even when extended to more complex geometries and higher dimensions.

Figure 2:
Identification results for various cavity shapes (top row), histories of Hausdorff distances (middle row), and residuums (bottom row). In the history plots, the red lines represent the conventional shape optimization approach (SO), while the blue lines indicate the values obtained using the QSSTS approach.

Example 1.5
.

We also evaluate the performance of the proposed QSSTS approach compared to the classical SO method in three dimensions. The results, shown in Figure
3
, illustrate the identification of cubic and dumbbell-shaped cavities inside a unit ball using these methods, where in all cases we choose a ball of radius
0.8
as the initial guess for the reconstruction procedure. The main observation is that both methods produce almost identical reconstructions of the exact cavities. However, a clear difference is seen in their convergence speeds: the QSSTS method converges significantly faster than the SO method, as expected and consistent with the results observed in the two-dimensional case.
It should be noted, however, that the reconstruction in the concave regions of the cavities is less accurate, likely because these regions are farther from the measurement surface. In relation to this, a recent numerical study has proposed improving the identification of such concavities by coupling the standard SO method with the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM); see
[
55
]
. We expect that combining QSSTS with ADMM could lead to a more robust and accurate reconstruction scheme, which we leave as a subject for future work.

Figure 3:
Identifications of a cube and a dumbbell-shaped cavity, along with the histories of cost values and gradient norms.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we establish several auxiliary results related to a diffeomorphic mapping between a reference domain and its perturbation.
In Section
3
, we present the main result of the paper (see Theorem
3.1
), which addresses the local-in-time solvability of system (
1
) by employing techniques developed by Solonnikov in
[
61
]
.
The remaining sections sequentially outline the key steps in proving the main result.
Section
4
details the transformation of the original problem onto a fixed domain.
In Section
5
, we establish the regularity of solutions on the fixed domain.
Section
6
addresses the nonlinear problem with respect to a parameter.
This is followed by an analysis of the linearized problem in Section
7
.
The proof of the main result, as stated in Theorem
3.1
, is completed in Section
8
.
Finally, a summary of the work and some final remarks are given in Section
9
.

This work also includes several appendices that provide preliminary analyses of the well-posedness of (
1
) in the case of axisymmetric domains (see Appendices
A
and
B
), demonstrations of transformations and computations of key equations and identities (Appendices
C
and
D
) utilized in the paper, and detailed proofs of auxiliary lemmas used in the proof of the main result (Appendix
E
).

2
Auxiliary Results

To state clearly our main result, we start by fixing some notations and prove some preliminary results in this section.
Throughout the paper, for
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
{{C}}^{k}(\overline{\Omega})
denotes the usual space of all functions having continuous and bounded derivatives in
\overline{\Omega}
up to
k
th order while, with
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
{{C}}^{k+\alpha}(\Omega)
is the Banach space of all functions
u\in{{C}}^{k}(\Omega)
for which the norm
4
4

4

See also the norms issued at the beginning of Section
3
(cf. notations in
[
44
,
46
]
)
.

\left|{u}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Omega}=\left|{u}\right|^{(k)}_{\Omega}+[u]^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Omega},

is finite, where

\left|{u}\right|^{(k)}_{\Omega}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}[u]^{(j)}_{\Omega},\qquad[u]^{(j)}_{\Omega}=\max_{\left|{\beta}\right|=j}\left|{D^{\beta}{u}}\right|^{(0)}_{\Omega},\qquad\left|{u}\right|^{(0)}_{\Omega}=[u]^{(0)}_{\Omega}=\sup_{\Omega}\left|{u}\right|,

and

[u]^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Omega}=\max_{\left|{\beta}\right|=k}[D^{\beta}{u}]^{(\alpha)}_{\Omega},\qquad[u]^{(\alpha)}_{\Omega}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{=}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega\\
x\neq x^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|{u(x)-u(y)}\right|}{\left|{x-y}\right|^{\alpha}}.

Given a bounded simply connected domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with boundary
\partial{D}=\Sigma
, we define the
set of admissible geometries
(the boundary of
inclusions
) as follows:

\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}:=\left\{\Gamma=\partial{\omega}\mid\overline{\Omega}\subset D,\ \text{$\omega$ is a simply connected bounded domain and $\partial{\omega}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}$}\right\}.

For
\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
, we introduce the notation
\Omega(\Gamma)
to denote an annular domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with boundary
\partial\Omega(\Gamma)=\Gamma\cup\Sigma
.
Accordingly, given
f\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma)
and
\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
, we denote by
u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma)
the unique solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}u_{\text{D}}&\in&{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega(\Gamma)}),\\
-\Delta u_{\text{D}}&=&0&\quad\text{in $\Omega(\Gamma)$},\\
u_{\text{D}}&=&f&\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
u_{\text{D}}&=&0&\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}.\end{array}\right.

(7)

Similarly, given
g\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
and
\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
, we denote by
u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma)
the unique solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}u_{\text{N}}&\in&{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega(\Gamma)}),\\
-\Delta u_{\text{N}}&=&0&\quad\text{in $\Omega(\Gamma)$},\\
\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}}}{\partial\nu}&=&g&\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
u_{\text{N}}&=&0&\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}.\end{array}\right.

(8)

Hereinafter, unless otherwise stated, we assume
\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
.

For technical purposes and for economy of space, we need to introduce a notion of
quasi-normal
vector on
\Gamma
.

Definition 2.1
.

We say that a vector field
\mathbf{N}
is
quasi-normal
on
\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
, inheriting the regularity of
\Gamma
, if

\left\{\ \begin{aligned} &\text{$\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R}^{d})$, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{$\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}=0$ near $\Sigma$}}, and}\\
&\text{it is such that $\left|{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)}\right|=1$ and $\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\cdot\nu(\xi;\Gamma)>0$, for all $\xi\in\Gamma$.}\end{aligned}\right.

(9)

The assumption that
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
vanishes near
\Sigma
is necessary to keep the exterior boundary
\Sigma
fixed.

We let
{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}}:=[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]
and fix a constant
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})>0
such that the map

X:\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Gamma^{\varepsilon_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad X(\xi,\rho)\ \longmapsto\ \xi+\rho\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\subset{D},

is a
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
-diffeomorphism, where

\Gamma^{{\varepsilon}}:=\{X(\xi,r):=\xi+r\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\mid(\xi,r)\in\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon}\},

for
{\varepsilon}>0
.
In fact, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 2.2
.

There exists a constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

X\in\operatorname{Diffeo}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma\times\overline{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}};{\Gamma^{\varepsilon_{0}}}),\qquad{\Gamma^{\varepsilon_{0}}}:=X(\Gamma\times\overline{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}}).

Proof.

Let
\varrho\in B_{1}:=B_{1}{(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}
(i.e.,
B_{1}
denotes the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
).
For all
\xi_{0}\in\Gamma
, there exists an open set
B(\xi_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
containing
\xi_{0}
and a mapping
\varphi\in\operatorname{Diffeo}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{B_{1}},\overline{\Gamma\cap B(\xi_{0})})
such that

\Upsilon:\overline{B}_{1}\times{I}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad\Upsilon(\varrho,r):=X(\varphi(\varrho),r)=\varphi(\varrho)+r\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\varphi(\varrho)).

We shall first prove that the determinant of the Jacobian of the map
\Upsilon
is non-zero for all
(\varrho,r)\in\overline{B}_{1}\times{I}_{\varepsilon}
.

We let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}
be a smooth extension of
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
in a tubular neighborhood of
\Gamma
and define

\textsf{N}(\varrho,r)=\textsf{I}+r\nabla_{\Gamma}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}(\varphi(\varrho)).

By straightforward computations, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\ni\nabla_{(\varrho,r)}^{\top}\Upsilon{(\varrho,r)}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\varrho}^{\top}\Upsilon{(\varrho,r)}&\partial_{r}\Upsilon{(\varrho,r)}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\varrho}^{\top}\varphi(\varrho)+r\left(\nabla_{x}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}(x)\big|_{x=\varphi(\varrho)}\right)\nabla_{\varrho}^{\top}\varphi(\varrho)&\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\varphi(\varrho))\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\varrho}^{\top}\varphi(\varrho)+r\nabla_{\Gamma}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}(\varphi(\varrho))\nabla_{\varrho}^{\top}\varphi(\varrho)&\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\varphi(\varrho))\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\left(\textsf{I}+r\nabla_{\Gamma}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}(\varphi(\varrho))\right)\nabla_{\varrho}^{\top}\varphi(\varrho)&\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\varphi(\varrho))\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\varrho}^{\top}\varphi(\varrho)&\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\varphi(\varrho))\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\textsf{N}(\varrho,r)\begin{pmatrix}\textsf{N}^{-1}(\varrho,r)\nabla_{\varrho}^{\top}\varphi(\varrho)&\textsf{N}^{-1}(\varrho,r)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\varphi(\varrho))\end{pmatrix}.

For
\xi:=\varphi(\varrho)
, we claim that

\left[\textsf{N}^{-1}(\varrho,r)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\right]\cdot\nu(\xi)>0.

Indeed, since
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
is a quasi-normal vector on
\Gamma
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\cdot\nu(\xi)\geqslant{c}>0
, for all
\xi\in\Gamma
.
Noting that

\textsf{N}^{-1}-\textsf{I}=\textsf{N}^{-1}(\textsf{I}-\textsf{N})=r\textsf{N}^{-1}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}},

we can choose
\varepsilon_{0}\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}
where

\varepsilon_{1}={c}\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{2\left\|{\textsf{N}^{-1}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}\right\|}\right\}

so that

\displaystyle\left[\textsf{N}^{-1}(\varrho,r)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\right]\cdot\nu(\xi)

\displaystyle=\left[\textsf{N}^{-1}(\textsf{I}-\textsf{N})(\varrho,r)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\right]\cdot\nu(\xi)+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\cdot\nu(\xi)

\displaystyle\geqslant{c}-\left\|{\textsf{N}^{-1}-\textsf{I}}\right\|\geqslant\frac{c}{2},

where
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
is the usual entry-wise matrix norm.

We next prove that the map
X
is injective.
For this purpose, we show that

if
{\xi},{\tilde{\xi}}\in\Gamma
and
X({\xi},{\rho})=X({\tilde{\xi}},{\tilde{\rho}})
, then
({\xi},{\rho})=({\tilde{\xi}},{\tilde{\rho}})
.

Again, we note that for each
\xi\in\Gamma
, there exists an open set
B({\xi})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
containing
\xi
.
Meanwhile, since
\Gamma
is compact, there exists a finite collection of open covers
\{O_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\Gamma=\bigcup_{j=1}^{M}O_{j}
.
For each
j=1,\ldots,M
, we let
\xi_{j}\in\Gamma
and define, for each open set
B(\xi_{j})\ni\xi_{j}
, the set
O_{j}=\Gamma\cap B(\xi_{j})\ni\xi_{j}
.
Clearly, for
\xi_{i}\neq\xi_{j}
, there exists
r_{0}>0
such that

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\}\not\subset O_{j}\\
\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\in\Gamma\end{subarray}}\left|{\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}}\right|=r_{0}>0.

(10)

By the previous argument, it suffices to prove that

if
{\xi},{\tilde{\xi}}\in\Gamma
and
X({\xi},{\rho})=X({\tilde{\xi}},{\tilde{\rho}})
, then
{\xi}={\tilde{\xi}}
.

Let us assume that
{\xi},{\tilde{\xi}}\in\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon}
,
X({\xi},{\rho})=X({\tilde{\xi}},{\tilde{\rho}})
, and
{\xi}\neq{\tilde{\xi}}
.
The latter condition implies that there exist
i,j\in\{1,\dots,M\}
, with
i\neq j
, such that
{\xi}\in{O}_{i}\setminus{O}_{j}
and
\tilde{\xi}\in O_{j}\setminus O_{i}
.
Now, we let

{r}_{0}:=\inf\{{{\xi}-{\tilde{\xi}}}\mid{\xi},{\tilde{\xi}}\in\Gamma\ \text{and}\ \{\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\}\not\subset O_{j},\ \text{for all $j=1,\ldots,M$}\}.

Meanwhile, we have

0\neq{\xi}-{\tilde{\xi}}={\rho}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}({\xi})-{\tilde{\rho}}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}({\tilde{\xi}})=({\rho}-{\tilde{\rho}})\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}({\xi})+{\tilde{\rho}}({\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}({\xi})-{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}({\tilde{\xi}})).

Hence,

{{\xi}-{\tilde{\xi}}}\leqslant\left|{{\rho}-{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|+L_{0}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|{{\xi}-{\tilde{\xi}}},\qquad\text{where}\ L_{0}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Gamma\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(x)-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)}\right|}{\left|{x-y}\right|}.

Since
\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
, we get

(1-\varepsilon_{0}L_{0}){{\xi}-{\tilde{\xi}}}\leqslant\left|{{\rho}-{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|\leqslant 2\varepsilon_{0}.

From (
10
), we deduce that

r_{0}=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\}\not\subset O_{j}\\
\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\in\Gamma\end{subarray}}\left|{\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}}\right|\leqslant{{\xi}-{\tilde{\xi}}}\leqslant\frac{2\varepsilon_{0}}{1-\varepsilon_{0}L_{0}}.

Taking
\varepsilon_{0}<\dfrac{r_{0}}{2+r_{0}L_{0}}
, we arrive at a contradiction unless
r_{0}=0
.
Thus,
{\xi}={\tilde{\xi}}
.

For the sake of being specific, we choose

\varepsilon_{0}=c\min\left\{\dfrac{r_{0}}{3+r_{0}L_{0}},\varepsilon_{1}\right\},\qquad\varepsilon_{1}={c}\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{2\left\|{\textsf{N}^{-1}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}\right\|}\right\}

to conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 2.3

(Extensions of the normal vector)
.

In the previous proof, it suffices to assume that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}
is
{C}^{1+\alpha}
.
This is guaranteed because
\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
implies that
\nu\in C^{1+\alpha}
, and therefore, we can create a
{C}^{1+\alpha}
extension of
\nu
in
D\supset\Gamma
.
More specifically, there exists an
\varepsilon_{\star}
-neighborhood of
\Gamma
(
\varepsilon_{\star}\geqslant\varepsilon_{0}
), say
\mathscr{N}^{\varepsilon_{\star}}(\Gamma)\supset X(\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}})
, such that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}\in{C}^{1+\alpha}(\mathscr{N}^{\varepsilon_{\star}}(\Gamma);\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Additionally, we see that the distance function
\operatorname{d}(\cdot,\Gamma)\in{C}^{2+\alpha}(\mathscr{N}^{\varepsilon_{\star}}(\Gamma))
, where
\operatorname{d}(x,\Gamma):=\pm\operatorname{dist}(x,\Gamma)
.
Consequently, for any
x\in\mathscr{N}^{\varepsilon_{\star}}(\Gamma)
, we can define
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}(x):=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi(x))
, where
\xi(x)=x-\operatorname{d}(x,\Gamma)\nabla\operatorname{d}(x,\Gamma)
.
Furthermore, for later use, we emphasize that we can create another extension of
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
which is
{C}^{2+\alpha}
. That is, for
(\xi,\rho)\in\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}}
, we can define
{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}$}}}}(X(\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}})):=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)
such that
{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}$}}}}\big|_{\Gamma}=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
.
These extensions are assumed to vanish near
\Sigma
.

For fixed real numbers
a
and
b
, where
b>a
, the scalar-valued function
\rho
belongs to the Banach space

{{R}}_{[a,b]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}):=\left\{\rho:\Gamma\times[a,b]\to{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})}\mid\rho\in{{C}}^{0}([a,b];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma))\cap{{C}}^{1}([a,b];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma))\right\}.

(11)

We introduce the set

\displaystyle{{R}}_{0}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\rho\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma)\mid\left|{\rho(\xi)}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}),\ \text{$\forall\xi\in\Gamma$}\right\}.

For
\rho\in{{R}}_{0}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})
, it can be shown that

{\mathscr{S}}(\rho):=\{X(\xi,\rho)\mid\xi\in\Gamma\}
is a
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
boundary.

In fact,
{\mathscr{S}}(\rho)\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
, as claimed in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.4
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{1}:=\varepsilon_{1}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})]
such that
{\mathscr{S}}(\rho)\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
holds for
\rho\in{{R}}_{1}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})
, where

{{R}}_{1}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}):=\left\{\rho\in{{R}}_{0}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})\mid\text{$\left|{\rho(\xi)}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}$, $\left|{\nabla_{\Gamma}\rho(\xi)}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}$, for all $\xi\in\Gamma$}\right\}.

To prove the proposition, we need the next lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in[0,1)
, and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open bounded set with
{{C}}^{k+\alpha}
boundary.
Let
\phi\in{{C}}_{0}^{k+\alpha}(\Omega)
and consider
\varphi(x)=x+\phi(x)
,
x\in\Omega
.
Assume that
\max_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}\left\|{\nabla^{\top}\phi(x)}\right\|<1
.
Then,
\operatorname{{det}}(\nabla^{\top}\varphi)>0
and
\varphi\in\operatorname{Diffeo}^{k+\alpha}(\Omega,\Omega)
; i.e., the map
\varphi:\Omega\to\Omega
is a
{{C}}^{k+\alpha}
-diffeomorphism.

See Appendix
E.1
for the proof.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}:=\varepsilon_{0}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})
.
The map
X:\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\to\Gamma^{\varepsilon_{0}}
is a
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
-diffeomorphism. We choose and fix
\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{0}
, with
\left|{\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{0}}\right|
sufficiently small, such that the map
X:\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon}\to\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\subset{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
-diffeomorphism.
Consequently,
X^{-1}:\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\to\Gamma\times{I}_{\varepsilon}
is also a
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
-diffeomorphism;
i.e.,
X^{-1}=(\hat{\xi}(x),\hat{r}(x))
where
\hat{\xi}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon},\Gamma)
and
\hat{r}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon},{I}_{\varepsilon})
.
We consider
\eta\in{{C}}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
0\leqslant\eta(s)\leqslant 1
, such that
\eta(s)=1
for
\left|{s}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
and
\operatorname{supp}\eta\subset(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

Let us define
\Phi
as follows:

\Phi(x)=\begin{cases}x&\text{if }x\in\overline{D}\setminus\Gamma^{\varepsilon},\\
x+\eta(\hat{r}(x))\rho(\hat{\xi}(x))\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\hat{\xi}(x))&\text{if }x\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

Clearly,
\Phi\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We claim that
\Phi\in\operatorname{Diffeo}^{2+\alpha}(\Omega,\Omega)
.
Indeed, this follows from Lemma
2.5
.
We only need to verify that the norm of the Jacobian of its perturbation – given that
\left|{\rho(\xi)}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}
,
\left|{\nabla_{\Gamma}\rho(\xi)}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}
, for all
\xi\in\Gamma
– has magnitude less than one.
To facilitate the proof, let us write
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{G}}(x):=\eta(\hat{r}(x))\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\hat{\xi}(x))
and define
\Phi(x)=x+\phi(x)
, where

\phi(x)=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{0}}&\text{if }x\in\overline{D}\setminus\Gamma^{\varepsilon},\\
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{G}}(x)\rho(\hat{\xi}(x))&\text{if }x\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

From the definition of
\phi
, we have

\nabla^{\top}\phi(x)=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{0}}&\text{if }x\in\overline{D}\setminus\Gamma^{\varepsilon},\\
\nabla^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{G}}(x)\rho(\hat{\xi}(x))+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{G}}(x)\nabla^{\top}_{\Gamma}\rho(\hat{\xi}(x))\nabla^{\top}_{x}\hat{\xi}(x)&\text{if }x\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

Computing its norm, while noting that
\left|{\rho(\xi)}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}
and
\left|{\nabla_{\Gamma}\rho(\xi)}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}
, for all
\xi\in\Gamma
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla^{\top}\phi(x)}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|{\nabla^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{G}}}\right\|+\left|{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{G}}(x)}\right|\left|{\nabla^{\top}_{\Gamma}\rho(\hat{\xi}(x))}\right|\left\|{\nabla^{\top}_{x}\hat{\xi}(x)}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}\left[\max_{x\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}\left\|{\nabla^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{G}}(x)}\right\|+\max_{x\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{G}}(x)}\right|\left\|{\nabla^{\top}_{x}\hat{\xi}(x)}\right\|\right)\right]<1.

This proves the proposition.
∎

For
\rho\in{{R}}_{[a,b]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})
, we define the moving boundary

\mathscr{M}(\rho,[a,b]):=\bigcup_{t\in[a,b]}{\mathscr{S}}(\rho(t))\times\{t\},

(12)

with normal velocity
V_{n}(t)=V_{n}(\cdot,t)\in{{C}}^{0}({\mathscr{S}}(\rho(t)))
where

V_{n}(x,t):=\rho_{t}(\xi,t)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\cdot\nu(x;{\mathscr{S}}(\rho(t))),\qquad x=\xi+\rho(\xi,t)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\in{\mathscr{S}}(\rho(t)),\quad\xi\in\Gamma.

We define the set of moving boundaries as

{{M}}_{[a,b]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}):=\left\{\mathscr{M}(\rho,[a,b])\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\mid\text{$\exists\rho\in{{R}}_{[a,b]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})$ such that \eqref{eq:M_rho_ab} is satisfied}\right\}.

Now, we consider the quasi-stationary moving boundary problem stated as follows:

Problem 2.6
.

For given
\Gamma_{\circ}\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
,
f\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma)
, and
g\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
,
find
T>0
and
\mathscr{M}=\bigcup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\}
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} V_{n}(t)&=-\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial\nu}\left[u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma(t))-u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma(t))\right]\quad\text{on $\Gamma(t)$, \quad$(0\leqslant t\leqslant T)$},\\
\Gamma(0)&=\Gamma_{\circ},\end{aligned}\right.

(13)

where
u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma(t))
and
u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma(t))
are defined by (
7
) and (
8
), respectively.

We formally define the solution of Problem
2.6
as follows.

Definition 2.7
.

We say
\mathscr{M}=\bigcup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
a solution of Problem
2.6
,
if for
\Gamma(0)=\Gamma_{\circ}
, there exists a collection of closed intervals
\{I_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
such that
\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}{I_{k}}=[0,T]
, and for each
k
, there exists
t_{k}\in I_{k}
,
\Gamma_{k}\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
, and quasi-normal
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}_{k}
on
\Gamma_{k}
such that

\mathscr{M}\big|_{I_{k}}\in{{M}}_{I_{k}}(\Gamma_{k},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}_{k})\quad\text{where}\quad\mathscr{M}\big|_{I_{k}}=\bigcup_{t\in I_{k}}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\},

is a solution of

\text{$V_{n}(t)=-\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial\nu}\left[u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma(t))-u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma(t))\right]$ on $\Gamma(t)$, \quad for $t\in I_{k}$, for each $k=1,\ldots,n$},

where
u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma(t))
and
u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma(t))
are defined by (
7
) and (
8
), respectively.

We note that the definition of
V_{n}
does not depends on the choice of
\Gamma_{k}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}_{k}
.

Before we proceed further, we ask the following question in view of Definition
2.7
:
suppose
\mathscr{M}_{[0,T]}
is a solution to (
1
), then is it true that
\mathscr{M}_{[{t}_{\ast},T]}
is also a solution to (
1
) for
{t}_{\ast}\in[0,T]
?

The next lemma answers this question affirmatively.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
be a quasi-normal vector on
\Gamma
,

\mathscr{M}=\bigcup_{a\leqslant t\leqslant b}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\}\in{{M}}_{[a,b]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}),

{t}_{\ast}\in[a,b]
, and
{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}_{\ast}
be quasi-normal on
\Gamma({t}_{\ast})
.
Then, there exists a
{\tau}>0
such that, with
I_{\ast}:=[a,b]\cap[{t}_{\ast}-{\tau},{t}_{\ast}+{\tau}]
, we have

\mathscr{M}\big|_{I_{\ast}}\in{{M}}_{I_{\ast}}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast}),{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}_{\ast}).

(14)

Proof.

We start by noting that there exist
\varepsilon_{\ast}>0
such that

{{X}_{\ast}}\in\operatorname{Diffeo}^{2+\alpha}\left(\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast},{X}_{\ast}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast})\right),\qquad{{X}_{\ast}}(x,r):=x+r{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}_{\ast}(x),

where

(x,r)\in\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast},\qquad I_{\ast}:={I}_{\varepsilon_{\ast}}\cap[a,b],\qquad{I}_{\varepsilon_{\ast}}=[-\varepsilon_{\ast},\varepsilon_{\ast}].

We claim the following:

There exists a
{\tau}>0
such that

\Gamma(t)\subset{X}_{\ast}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast\ast})
, for
t\in{I}_{\ast\ast}:={I}_{\ast}\cap[{t}_{\ast}-{\tau},{t}_{\ast}+{\tau}]
.

For
(y,t)\in\Gamma\times{I}_{\ast\ast}
, we define

({x}_{\ast}(y,t),{\rho}_{\ast}(y,t)):={X}_{\ast}^{-1}(X(y,\rho(y,t))),

and for
(x,t)\in\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast\ast}
, we set

\widetilde{\rho}(x,t):={\rho}_{\ast}({x}_{\ast}(\cdot,t)^{-1}(x),t),

where
{x}_{\ast}(\cdot,t):\Gamma\to\Gamma({t}_{\ast})
.
Hence, by construction

X(y,\rho(y,t))={X}_{\ast}(x,\tilde{\rho}(x,t))\Big|_{x={x}_{\ast}(y,t)}.

Now, to validate (
14
), we need to show that

\tilde{\rho}\in{{C}}^{0}({I}_{\ast\ast};{C}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast}))\cap{C}^{1}({I}_{\ast\ast};{C}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast})).

(15)

This follows directly from the following assumptions:

\displaystyle(y,t)

\displaystyle\in\Gamma\times{I}_{\ast\ast}\subset\Gamma\times{I}_{\ast}\subset\Gamma\times[a,b],

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\in{{C}}^{0}({I}_{\ast};{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma))\cap{{C}}^{1}({I}_{\ast};{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma)),

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\in{C}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma\times I_{\varepsilon_{0}}),

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\in\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast\ast}\subset\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast},

as well as from the regularities of the following mappings:

\displaystyle[(y,t)\mapsto X(y,\rho(y,t))]

\displaystyle\in{{C}}^{0}({I}_{\ast\ast};{C}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap{C}^{1}({I}_{\ast\ast};{C}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R}^{d})),

\displaystyle{X}_{\ast}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{Diffeo}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast\ast},{X}_{\ast}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast})\times{I}_{\ast\ast})),

\displaystyle{\rho}_{\ast}

\displaystyle\in{{C}}^{0}({I}_{\ast\ast};{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma))\cap{{C}}^{1}({I}_{\ast\ast};{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma)),

\displaystyle{x}_{\ast}

\displaystyle\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma;\Gamma({t}_{\ast})),

\displaystyle{x}_{\ast}^{-1}

\displaystyle\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast});\Gamma).

In view of Definition
2.7
, the composition of these mappings leads to (
15
).
∎

By Lemma
2.8
, we can state an equivalent definition of the solution to Problem
2.6
, given as follows:

Definition 2.9
.

We say
\mathscr{M}=\bigcup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
is a solution to Problem
2.6
if, for all
{t}_{\ast}\in[0,T]
, there exist
a<b
and
{\tau}>0
such that
[{t}_{\ast}-{\tau},{t}_{\ast}+{\tau}]\cap[0,T]\subset[a,b]\subset[0,T]
, and there exists a quasi-normal vector
{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}_{\ast}
on
\Gamma({t}_{\ast})
such that

\mathscr{M}_{[a,b]}\in{{M}}_{[a,b]}(\Gamma({t}_{\ast}),{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}_{\ast}),\quad\text{where $\mathscr{M}_{[a,b]}:=\bigcup_{a\leqslant t\leqslant b}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\}$},

and
u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma(t))
and
u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma(t))
, defined by (
7
) and (
8
), solve (
13
).

Remark 2.10
.

The definition of the solution to Problem
2.6
given in Definition
2.9
is indeed equivalent to Definition
2.7
.
To see this, we note that, for all
t\in[0,T]
, there exists
{\tau}(t)>0
such that
[t-{\tau}(t),t+{\tau}(t)]\cap[0,T]\subset[a,b]\subset[0,T]
, and there exists a quasi-normal vector
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
on
\Gamma(t)
such that

\mathscr{M}_{[a,b]}\in{{M}}_{[a,b]}(\Gamma(t),\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}),

and
u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma(t))
and
u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma(t))
, defined by (
7
) and (
8
), solve (
13
).
Now, we observe that

\emptyset\neq\mathscr{O}(t):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(t-{\tau}(t),t+{\tau}(t))\cap(0,T)&\text{for }t&\in(0,T),\\
&[0,{\tau}(0))&\text{for }t&=0,\\
&(t-{\tau}(t),T]&\text{for }t&=T.\end{aligned}\right.

Note that
\mathscr{O}
is open in
[0,T]
and
\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathscr{O}(t)=[0,T]
.

3
Main Results

Our main objective is to demonstrate the (local-in-time) solvability of system (
1
) by applying the techniques outlined by Solonnikov in
[
61
]
. Specifically, we refer to the proofs of
[
61
, Thm. 1.1 and Thm. 3.1]
, and also to
[
29
]
for a related application of this method.

Notations.
Let
l
be a nonnegative real number.
We denote by
{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{l}(\overline{\Omega}))
the space of continuous functions with respect to

(x,t)\in\left\{(x,t)\mid t\in[0,T],x\in\overline{\Omega}\right\}

with the finite norm

\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}|u(\cdot,t)|^{(l)}_{\overline{\Omega}},

where

\displaystyle\left|{u}\right|^{(l)}_{\overline{\Omega}}

\displaystyle:=\left\|{u}\right\|_{{{C}}^{[l],l-[l]}(\overline{\Omega})}=\left|{u}\right|_{[l],l-[l];\,\overline{\Omega}}=\sum_{\left|{j}\right|<l}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}|{{D}}^{j}u(x)|+[u]^{(l)}_{\overline{\Omega}},

\displaystyle[u]^{(l)}_{\overline{\Omega}}

\displaystyle:=[u]_{[l],l-[l];\,\overline{\Omega}}=\sum_{\left|{j}\right|=[l]}\max_{x,{{\hat{x}}}\in\overline{\Omega}}\frac{|{{D}}^{j}u(x)-{{D}}^{j}u({{\hat{x}}})|}{|x-{{\hat{x}}}|^{l-[l]}}.

For example,
\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}
is the maximum norm of
u(x)
, i.e.,
\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}=\max_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}|u(x)|
.
Also, in a more familiar notation,
\left|{u}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega}}:=\left\|{u}\right\|_{{{C}}^{k,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}
where
l=k+\alpha
,
k=[l]\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
\alpha=[l]-l\in(0,1)
; see
[
31
, Eqs. (4.5)–(4.6), p. 53]
.
The spaces
{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{l}(\Sigma))
and
{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{l}(\Gamma))
are introduced in a similar manner.
Here,
{{C}}^{[l],l-[l]}:={{C}}^{k}
for integer
l=k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Throughout the paper, we use
c
as a general positive constant, meaning it can vary in value depending on the context.

Let
a
and
b
be fixed real numbers such that
b>a
,
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
For functions
\varphi=\varphi(\xi,t)\in{{C}}^{0}([a,b];{{C}}^{k+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
, the norm

{\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\left|{\varphi(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega}},

will appear many times in the paper (especially from Section
7
onwards and in Appendix
E.3
). So, for economy of space, we introduce the following notation

\left|{\varphi}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi;\,[a,b]}:={\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\left|{\varphi(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi},\qquad\Xi\in\{\Omega,\overline{\Omega},\Gamma,\Sigma\}.

For nested maximum norms, we write

\left|{\varphi}\right|^{\infty}_{\Xi;\,[a,b]}:={\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\,{\max_{\Xi}}\left|{\varphi(\cdot,\tau)}\right|,\qquad\Xi\in\{\overline{\Omega},\Gamma,\Sigma\}.

Additionally, for functions
u=u(\xi,t)
,
v=v(\xi,t)\in{{C}}^{0}([a,b];{{C}}^{k+\alpha}(\Xi))
,
\Xi\in\{\overline{\Omega},\Gamma,\Sigma\}
, we introduce the following specially defined norms

\left\|{(u,v)}\right\|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi;\,[a,b]}:=\left|{u}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi;\,[a,b]}+\left|{v}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi;\,[a,b]}={\max_{a\leqslant t\leqslant b}}\left|{u(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi}+{\max_{a\leqslant t\leqslant b}}\left|{v(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi}.

For a pair of functions with subscripts
{\cdot}_{\text{D}}
and
{\cdot}_{\text{N}}
, we simply write

\left\|{\varphi_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}\right\|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi;\,[a,b]}:=\left\|{(\varphi_{\text{D}},\varphi_{\text{N}})}\right\|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi;\,[a,b]},

especially when such a norm appears many times in a sequence of arguments.
Moreover, and specifically for the boundary data
f\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma))
and
g\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma))
,
[a,b]\subseteq[0,T]
, we simply write

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|(f,g)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\Sigma;\,[a,b]}:={\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\left|{f(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{\Sigma}+{\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\left|{g(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{\Sigma},\qquad{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|(f,g)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}:=\left\|{(f,g)}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{\Sigma,\,[0,T]}.

Additionally, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho\in{{C}}^{0}([a,b];{{C}}^{k+\alpha}(\Xi))\cap{{C}}^{1}([a,b];{{C}}^{k-1+\alpha}(\Xi))
and
\Xi\in\{\overline{\Omega},\Gamma\}
, we introduce the norm notation

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi;\,[a,b]}:={\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\left|{\rho(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\Xi}+{\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\left|{\frac{d}{d\tau}\rho(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(k-1+\alpha)}_{\Xi},

and another special notation

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(k+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[a,b]}:={\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\left|{\rho(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(k+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega}}+{\max_{a\leqslant\tau\leqslant b}}\left|{\frac{d}{d\tau}\rho(\cdot,\tau)}\right|^{(k-1+\alpha)}_{\Gamma}.

Finally, for any pair of well-defined functions
\varphi_{\text{D}}
and
\varphi_{\text{N}}
, we will extensively use the following special notations for convenience:

{\varphi}_{\text{D}\text{N}}:=\varphi_{\text{D}}-\varphi_{\text{N}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\varphi}_{\text{N}\text{D}}:=\varphi_{\text{N}}-\varphi_{\text{D}}.

For example, we write
{u}_{\text{D}\text{N}}=u_{\text{D}}-u_{\text{N}}
and
{u}_{\text{D}\text{N}}(\Gamma)=u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma)-u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma)
.

To prepare for later discussions involving the differentiability of certain linear and bounded operators (see Lemma
4.3
), we introduce the following function spaces. The space
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
denotes the set of linear and continuous (bounded) operators between two normed vector spaces
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
.
We also consider the space
C^{\omega}(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V})
, consisting of real analytic functions from a Banach space
\mathscr{U}
into
\mathscr{V}
, understood in the sense of Fréchet or Gâteaux differentiability.

Prior to stating the primary result we seek to establish in this study, we observe that, in the original inverse geometry problem setup,
f
and
g
are solely space-dependent.
However,
for the remainder of the discussion, otherwise specified, we shall assume that these functions are also (pseudo-)time-dependent.

Specifically, we let
f=f(x,t)
and
g=g(x,t)
, where
x\in\Omega(t)
and
t\in[0,T]
, in (
1
), and assume that they are both positive-valued.

We consider the following conditions, which are essential for the analytical framework underlying the proof of the well-posedness of (
1
):

Assumption A
.

•

For some
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\Sigma,\,\Gamma=\Gamma_{\circ}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha},\quad f\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma)),\ f>0,\quad g\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)),\ g>0,

such that

\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial\nu}\left({u}_{\text{D}\text{N}}(\Gamma_{\circ})\right)>0,

(A1)

where
u_{\text{D}}
and
u_{\text{N}}
respectively solves (
7
) and (
8
) in
\Omega(\Gamma_{\circ})
.

•

There exists a
t^{\star\star}\in(0,T]
such that

\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial\nu}\left({u}_{\text{N}\text{D}}(\Gamma(t))\right)>0,\quad\text{for $t\in[0,t^{\star\star}]$}.

It is necessary to comment on the key assumptions above, taking into account both its technical and practical implications.
The positivity of the difference between the normal derivatives, as expressed in (
A1
), is not only an essential requirement for proving the well-posedness of system (
1
) (see comment at the bottom of page 584 in
[
42
]
), but also plays a crucial role in the reconstruction procedure, and can, in fact, be ensured in practical computations.
This condition is satisfied when the initial guess in the algorithm is chosen sufficiently large to fully enclose the exact cavity – a choice that is both natural and effective in numerical implementations, at least in simple settings (see, e.g.,
[
55
]
).
Appendix
A
further illustrates this point in the setting of axially symmetric domains through a detailed comparison of normal derivatives.

Our main theorem is formulated as follows.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let Assumption
A
be satisfied.
Then, there exists a unique solution
\Gamma(t)
,
u_{\text{D}}(x,t)
, and
u_{\text{N}}(x,t)
to (
1
) defined on some small time-interval
{{I}^{\star}}=[0,{t}^{\star}]
, where
{{t}^{\star}}<T
.
The free surface
\Gamma(t)
is described by the equation

x=\xi+\rho(\xi,t)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi),\qquad\xi\in\Gamma,

(16)

where
\xi
is the local coordinate on the surface
\Gamma
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
is a smooth vector field on
\Gamma
such that
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nu_{\circ}\geqslant\nu_{\star}>0
, where
\nu_{\circ}
is the unit normal vector to the surface
\Gamma
directed inward the domain
\Omega(\Gamma)
.
The function
\rho\in{{C}}^{0}({{I}^{\star}};{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma))
has extra smoothness with respect to the variable
t
; namely,
\rho_{t}\in{{C}}^{0}({{I}^{\star}};{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma))
.
Meanwhile, the functions
u_{\text{D}}(x,t)
and
u_{\text{N}}(x,t)
are defined in
\Omega(t)
for
t\in{{I}^{\star}}
and both belong to the space
{{C}}^{0}({{I}^{\star}};{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega(t)}))
.
Moreover, the following estimate hold

\left\|{{u_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|(f,g)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Sigma;\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c\,{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|(f,g)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}.

(17)

for some constant
c>0
, for all
t\in{{I}^{\star}}
.

Given the short-time existence of solution to (
1
), we can also prove the uniqueness of short-time solution to the system.

Theorem 3.2
.

A solution of Problem
2.6
is unique.

Proof.

If there exist two solutions
\mathscr{M}_{i}:=\bigcup\Gamma^{i}(t)\times\{t\}
,
i=1,2
, to Problem
2.6
, then we need to show that
\mathscr{M}_{1}=\mathscr{M}_{2}
.
We prove the assertion via a contradiction.
Let us assume that
\mathscr{M}_{1}\neq\mathscr{M}_{2}
.
Then, there exists
{t}_{\ast}\in[0,T)
and a sequence
\{t_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\in({t}_{\ast},T]
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathscr{M}_{1}\big|_{[0,{t}_{\ast}]}=\mathscr{M}_{2}\big|_{[0,{t}_{\ast}]},\\
&T\geqslant t_{1}>t_{2}>\cdots>{t}_{\ast},\quad\text{where \ $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k}={t}_{\ast}$,\ and}\\
&\Gamma^{1}(t_{k})\neq\Gamma^{2}(t_{k}),\quad\text{for $k=1,2,\ldots$}.\end{aligned}\right.

(18)

Since
\Gamma({t}_{\ast}):=\Gamma^{1}({t}_{\ast})=\Gamma^{2}({t}_{\ast})
satisfies the condition in Theorem
3.1
, there exists
{t}_{\ast\ast}\in({t}_{\ast},T]
such that there is a unique
\Gamma(t)
for
t\in[{t}_{\ast},{t}_{\ast\ast}]
.
However, this contradicts the last two lines in (
18
).
Thus,
\mathscr{M}_{1}
and
\mathscr{M}_{2}
have to be the same solution to Problem
2.6
.
∎

Before we proceed, we provide additional comments on condition (
A1
) (cf. (
A3
)).
To this end, we first consider the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, of class
{{C}}^{2}
, be an open bounded connected set with non-intersecting boundaries
\Gamma
and
\Sigma
.
Assume that
v\in{{C}}^{2}(\Omega)\cap{{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega})\cap{{C}}^{1}(\Sigma)
and

-\Delta{v}=0\quad\text{in $\Omega$},\qquad{v}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$},\qquad\dfrac{\partial{v}}{\partial\nu}>0\quad\text{on $\Sigma$}.

Then,

{v}>0\quad\text{in $\Omega$}.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by contradiction and uses the maximum principle
[
26
, Chap. 6.4, p. 344]
.
We start by observing that
v\not\equiv\text{constant}
in
\Omega
; otherwise,
v=0
in
\Omega
because
v=0
on
\Gamma
, and so
\dfrac{\partial{v}}{\partial\nu}=0
on
\Sigma
, which contradicts the assumption.
We suppose that

\inf_{\Omega}{v}<0.

Then, by the maximum principle,

0>\inf_{\Omega}{v}=\min_{\bar{\Omega}}{v}=\min_{\Sigma}{v}.

That is,

\text{there exists }x_{0}\in\Sigma\text{ such that }{v}(x_{0})=\min_{\Sigma}{v}=\min_{\bar{\Omega}}{v}.

Because
v=0
on
\Gamma
, we deduce that

\dfrac{\partial{v}}{\partial\nu}(x_{0})<0,\qquad x_{0}\in\Sigma.

This is a contradiction to our assumption that
\dfrac{\partial{v}}{\partial\nu}>0
on
\Sigma
. Therefore,
\inf_{\Omega}{v}>0
, and thus
v>0
in
\Omega
, proving the lemma.
∎

Using Lemma
3.3
, we will prove in the next proposition that there exists a suitable choice of
\Gamma_{\circ}
such that condition (
A1
) holds.
In fact, we will use the same idea as in Appendices
A.1
and
A.2
, which compares the normal derivatives on the free boundary of concentric circles and spheres.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\Omega=D\setminus\overline{\omega}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, of class
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
, be an open bounded connected set with non-intersecting boundaries
\partial{\omega}=\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
and
\Sigma=\partial{D}
.
Assume that
\partial{\omega^{\star}}=\Gamma^{\star}\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
is the exact interior boundary that satisfies (
2
) and
\omega

strictly contains

\overline{\omega}^{\star}
(i.e.,
\Gamma
lies entirely in the interior of
\overline{\Omega}^{\star}=\overline{D\setminus\overline{\omega}^{\star}}
).
Let
f\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma)
and
g\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
.
Then, the functions
u_{\text{D}}(\Gamma)
and
u_{\text{N}}(\Gamma)
satisfying (
7
) and (
8
), respectively, satisfy the following condition

u_{\text{D}}>u_{\text{N}}\quad\text{in $\Omega$}.

Consequently,

\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial\nu}\left(u_{\text{D}}-u_{\text{N}}\right)>0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}.

Proof.

Let the assumptions of the proposition be satisified.
Let us denote by
(\Omega^{\star},u^{\star}(\Omega^{\star}))
,
\Omega^{\star}=D\setminus\overline{\omega}^{\star}
,
\Omega^{\star}
is of class
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
,
u^{\star}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Omega)\cap{{C}}^{0+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
, the exact solution pair of the free boundary problem (
2
) with the corresponding exact interior boundary
\Gamma^{\star}=\partial{\omega}^{\star}\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
.
We note the following observation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}\Delta u^{\star}=\Delta u_{\text{D}}=\Delta u_{\text{N}}&=&0&\quad\text{in $\Omega$},\\
u^{\star}=u_{\text{D}}&>&0&\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
u_{\text{D}}=u_{\text{N}}&=&0&\quad\text{on $\Gamma$},\\
u^{\star}&>&0&\quad\text{on $\Gamma$},\end{array}\right.

(19)

Let us define

{v}_{\text{D}}:=-u_{\text{D}}+u^{\star}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{v}_{\text{N}}:=-u_{\text{N}}+u^{\star}.

Then, from (
19
), we have the following

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}\Delta{v}_{\text{D}}=\Delta{v}_{\text{D}}&=&0&\quad\text{in $\Omega$},\\
{v}_{\text{D}}&=&0&\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
\dfrac{\partial{{v}_{\text{N}}}}{\partial\nu}&=&0&\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
{v}_{\text{D}}={v}_{\text{N}}=u^{\star}&>&0&\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}.\end{array}\right.

(20)

We deduce from (
20
) that

{v}_{\text{D}}>0\quad\text{in $\Omega$}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\dfrac{\partial{v_{\text{D}}}}{\partial\nu}<0\quad\text{on $\Sigma$}.

(21)

Now, we define

{v}={v}_{\text{N}}-{v}_{\text{D}}=(-u_{\text{N}}+u^{\star})-(-u_{\text{D}}+u^{\star})=u_{\text{D}}-u_{\text{N}}.

Hence, we have

-\Delta{v}=0\quad\text{in $\Omega$},\qquad{v}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$},\qquad\dfrac{\partial{v}}{\partial\nu}>0\quad\text{on $\Sigma$}.

Therefore, by Lemma
3.3
, we get

u_{\text{D}}-u_{\text{N}}={v}>0\quad\text{in $\Omega$}.

Consequently, when
\Gamma\in\mathscr{A}^{2+\alpha}
lies entirely in the interior of
\overline{\Omega}^{\star}=\overline{D\setminus\overline{\omega}^{\star}}
, we get

\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial\nu}\left(u_{\text{D}}-u_{\text{N}}\right)>0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem
3.1
follows from an auxiliary result (see Theorem
4.4
) which we issue in the next section.

4
Problem Transformation onto a Fixed Domain

To carry out our analysis, we first need to transform system (
1
) into a problem over a fixed domain via the change of variables to be described below.
Such technique has been used in many studies (see, e.g.,
[
61
]
) and can be achieved by a special mapping – a modification of the well-known Hanzawa transform – of the pseudo-time dependent domain
\Omega(t)
onto the fixed domain
\Omega
.
The target equation is given by (
28
), and the main result we want to state here is emphasized in Theorem
4.4
.

From this point forward, we assume that Assumption
A
holds and that
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
is a quasi-normal vector on
\Gamma
(see Definition
2.1
).
Also, for some technical purposes, we assume there is a constant
\nu_{\star}>0
such that

\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\cdot\nu_{\circ}(\xi)\geqslant\nu_{\star}>0,\qquad\xi\in\Gamma,

(A2)

where
\nu_{\circ}
denotes the unit inward normal vector to
\Gamma
.
This requirement will be made clear in Section
6
.

Given a constant
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
, it was shown in
[
61
, Sec. 3, p. 134]
that the tubular strip

{\mathscr{S}}_{\varepsilon_{\circ}}=\{\xi+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\lambda\mid\left|{\lambda}\right|<\varepsilon_{\circ},\xi\in\Gamma\}

contains some neighborhood of
\Gamma
where the equation
x=\xi+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\lambda
determines some functions
\xi(x)
and
\lambda(x)
of class
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
(see the proof of Proposition
2.4
).
For
sufficiently small

t>0
, the free boundary
\Gamma(t)
is contained in
{\mathscr{S}}_{\varepsilon_{\circ}}
(cf. Proposition
2.4
) and can be described by the equation
\lambda=\rho(\xi,t)
.
Accordingly, for

\rho\in R_{\circ}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}):=\{\rho\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})\mid\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon_{\circ}\}

we define

{\mathscr{S}}(\rho):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x=\xi+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi)\rho(\xi),\ \xi\in\Gamma\}.

(22)

We introduce the
admissible set of hypersurfaces
consisting of
d-1
dimensional
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
manifold embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as follows

{{H}}_{\varepsilon_{\circ}}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}):=\{{\mathscr{S}}(\rho)\mid\rho\in R_{\circ}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})\}.

The following definition introduces a family of admissible moving surfaces (or moving boundaries).

Definition 4.1
.

We say that a moving surface (or moving boundary)
\mathscr{M}
is
admissible
, and we write
\mathscr{M}\in{{M}}_{[0,T]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})
, if and only if

\left\{\begin{aligned} \ \mathscr{M}&=\bigcup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R},\\
\ \Gamma(t)&={\mathscr{S}}(\rho(t))\in{{H}}_{\varepsilon_{\circ}}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}),\ \text{for all }t\in[0,T],\\
\ \rho&\in{{R}}_{[0,T]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}),\end{aligned}\right.

where
{{R}}_{[0,T]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})
is the set given in (
11
) with
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{\circ}
.

For every function
\rho\in{{R}}_{[0,T]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})
, we put into correspondence its extension

\tilde{\rho}(y,t):={E}\rho(y,t),

(23)

where
E
is a linear and bounded map

{E}:{{R}}_{[0,T]}(\Gamma,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})\to{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))\cap{{C}}^{1}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))

(24)

satisfying

\left\|{{E}{\rho}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}},

for some constant
c>0
.
Such an extension of
\rho
from
\Gamma\times[0,T]
to
\overline{\Omega}\times[0,T]
can be constructed in different ways.
In this investigation, we assume that
\tilde{\rho}={\tilde{\rho}}(y,t):=E\rho(y,t)
satisfies the following boundary value problem:

-\Delta{\tilde{\rho}}=0,\ y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\qquad\left.{\tilde{\rho}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=\rho,\qquad\left.{\tilde{\rho}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,

(25)

where the latter condition is essential because
\Sigma
is fixed.
Using this extension allows for a more straightforward development of later arguments.
Throughout the remainder of our discussion, we assume without further mention that
{\tilde{\rho}}
satisfies (
25
).

To meet certain technical requirements, we will extend the vector field
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
(retaining the same notation) to the domain
{\mathscr{S}}_{\varepsilon_{\circ}}
by defining
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(x)=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi(x))
.
Then, we will further extend it to the domain
\Omega\cup{\mathscr{S}}_{\varepsilon_{\circ}}
, where
\Omega=\Omega(0)
, ensuring that the smoothness class
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Omega\cup{\mathscr{S}}_{\varepsilon_{\circ}})
is maintained (cf. Remark
2.3
).

Let us introduce the change of variables
y=Y(x,t)
under which
\Omega(t)
,
t>0
, is transformed to
\Omega
and define the inverse transform
Y^{-1}:\Omega\to\Omega(t)
as follows:

Y^{-1}(y,t)=x\in\Omega(t),\quad x=y+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y){\tilde{\rho}}(y,t),

(26)

for
y\in\Omega
and
t>0
.
The given map is bijective, as stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
Z(y):=y+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)\tilde{\rho}(y)
,
y\in\overline{\Omega}
, such that
Z\left.{=}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}y
and
Z\big|_{y\in\Gamma}=y+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)\rho(y)
.
Then, the map
Z:\overline{\Omega}\to\overline{\Omega}(\rho)
, where
\overline{\Omega}(\rho)
is the annular domain bounded by
\Sigma
and
{\mathscr{S}}(\rho)
is bijective.

Proof.

See Appendix
E.2
.
∎

To transform the Hele-Shaw-like system (
1
) onto a fixed domain, we make a few additional preparations.
The Jacobi matrix
\textsf{J}=(\textsf{J}_{km})_{km}
(
k,m=1,\ldots,d
) of the transform
Y^{-1}
has entries

\textsf{J}_{km}=(D_{y}Y^{-1}(y,t))_{km}=\delta_{km}+N_{k}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{m}}+\frac{\partial N_{k}}{\partial y_{m}}{\tilde{\rho}}.

Correspondingly, we denote by
\textsf{J}^{km}
the entries of the inverse matrix
\textsf{J}^{-1}
,
i.e.,

\textsf{J}^{-1}=\left(\textsf{J}^{km}\right)_{km}=\left((D_{x}Y(x,t))_{km}\right)_{km}=\left((D_{y}Y^{-1}(y,t))_{km}\right)_{km}^{-1},

which is the Jacobi matrix of the transform
Y(x,t)
.
Denoting
\textsf{J}^{-\top}=(\textsf{J}^{-1})^{\top}
, the operator
\nabla_{x}:=\left(\partial_{x_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x_{d}}\right)
,
\partial_{x_{k}}:=\partial/\partial{x_{k}}
,
k=1,\ldots,d
, takes the form

\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nabla_{y}^{\top}:=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{km}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}\right),\quad m=1,\ldots,d,\qquad\nabla_{y}:=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{d}}\right).

(27)

The following relation between the inward unit normal vectors
\nu_{\circ}
to
\Gamma
and
\nu(t)
to
\Gamma(t)
(cf.
[
61
, p. 135]
or see
[
20
, Thm. 4.4, p. 488]
) holds:
