\nu(t)\circ Y^{-1}=\frac{\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}{|\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}|}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\nu(t)=\frac{\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}{|\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}|}\circ Y.

Applying the above change of variables and identities, we can pass from the
t
-dependent system (
1
) to the problem in the given fixed domain
\Omega
with respect to the three unknown functions
U_{\text{D}}(y,t)=u_{\text{D}}(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)
,
U_{\text{N}}(y,t)=u_{\text{N}}(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)
, and
{\tilde{\rho}}(y,t)
,
y\in\Omega
.
More precisely, given the harmonic function
\tilde{\rho}
satisfying (
25
), we have the following transformation of (
1
) using the map
Y:\Omega(t)\to\Omega
where
Y(x,t)=y\in\Omega
, for
t>0
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\text{D}}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{p}}+\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\frac{\partial\textsf{J}^{pk}}{\partial y_{m}}\frac{\partial U_{\text{D}}}{\partial y_{p}}&=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
\left.{U_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=f(y,t),\qquad\left.{U_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=0,\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{p}}+\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\frac{\partial\textsf{J}^{pk}}{\partial y_{m}}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}&=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
\left.{\dfrac{\partial{U_{\text{N}}}}{\partial\nu}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=g(y,t),\qquad\left.{U_{\text{N}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=0,\\[5.0pt]
\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial{t}}+B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}\dfrac{\partial{{{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=0,\\
{\tilde{\rho}}\big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(28)

where

{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}:=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\textsf{J}^{pk},\qquad m,p=1,\ldots,d,

are the entries of the matrix
{{\textsf{A}}}:=\textsf{J}^{-1}\textsf{J}^{-\top}
, and

B_{\tilde{\rho}}:=\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\right)^{-1}\left(\nu_{\circ}\cdot{{\textsf{A}}}\nu_{\circ}\right).

(29)

We note here that
\dfrac{\partial{{{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}>0
is consistent with Assumption
A
.

The detailed computation of system (
28
) is provided in Appendix
C
.

For convenience of later use, we will write problem (
28
) in compact form.
For this purpose, for
\rho\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma)
, we define the following linear and bounded operators:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}&:{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})\to{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),\quad\qquad\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}=\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{p}},\\
\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}&:{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})\to{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),\qquad\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}=-\sum_{j,k=1}^{d}{N}_{k}\textsf{J}^{jk}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}},\\
\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}&:{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})\to{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),\\
\quad\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}&=-\sum_{p=1}^{d}\left[\sum_{j,k,m,q=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{km}\textsf{J}^{pj}\textsf{J}^{qm}\left(\frac{\partial{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{q}}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{k}}+\frac{\partial{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{k}}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{q}}+\frac{\partial^{2}{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right)\right]\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{p}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(30)

The main equations in system (
28
) can then be written as follows

\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U_{\text{D}}}+\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U_{\text{D}}}\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{\tilde{\rho}}}+\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U_{\text{D}}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U_{\text{N}}}+\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U_{\text{N}}}\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{\tilde{\rho}}}+\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U_{\text{N}}}=0.

(31)

We note here that
\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}\equiv 0
when
{\tilde{\rho}}\equiv 0
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The following regularities hold:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\in{{C}}^{\omega}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma),\mathscr{B}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))),\\
[\rho\mapsto\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}],\ [\rho\mapsto\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}]&\in{{C}}^{\omega}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma),\mathscr{B}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))).\end{aligned}\right.

(32)

Proof.

We will only prove our claim that

[\rho\mapsto\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}]\in{{C}}^{\omega}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma),\mathscr{B}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))).

(33)

The other two results can be proven in a similar fashion.

We let
\rho\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma)
.
In view of (
23
) and (
25
), we define

\tilde{\rho}(y):={E}\rho(y),\quad y\in\Gamma,\qquad\text{where}\ E\in\mathscr{B}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma);{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})),

(34)

and such that

-\Delta{\tilde{\rho}}=0,\ y\in\Omega,\qquad\left.{\tilde{\rho}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=\rho,\qquad\left.{\tilde{\rho}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0.

(35)

We claim that

\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}\in\mathscr{B}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})).

(36)

Let us note that the following regularities hold:

•

\left[\tilde{\rho}\mapsto\dfrac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial{y_{m}}}\right]\in\mathscr{B}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
, for all
m=1,\ldots,d
;

•

D_{mp}:=\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial_{y_{m}}\partial_{y_{p}}}\in\mathscr{B}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
, for all
m,p=1,\ldots,d
;

•

A_{mp}\in{{C}}^{\omega}({{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}));{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})))
, for all
m,p=1,\ldots,d
;

•

for
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
, we have
\Lambda_{mp}\in{{C}}^{\omega}({{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}));{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})))
, for all
m,p=1,\ldots,d
, where
\Lambda_{mp}^{\rho}(x):=A_{mp}(\tilde{\rho})(x)u(x)
,
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.

The latter regularity result follows from the fact that the following map

\mathscr{F}:{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})\times{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})\longrightarrow{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),\qquad(\varphi,\psi)\longmapsto\varphi\psi,

is analytic (cf.
[
31
, p. 53]
); i.e.
\mathscr{F}\in{{C}}^{\omega}({{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})\times{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega});{{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})))
.
Hence, using
\Lambda_{mp}^{\rho}(x)=\mathscr{F}(A_{mp}(\tilde{\rho}),u)
, we establish the final regularity result mentioned above.
Consequently, by composing the given maps, we validate (
36
).

To complete the proof, we use the composition of the map
\rho\mapsto\tilde{\rho}
(defined through the extension operator
E
) and the operator
\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}
, which allows us to confirm our claim (
33
).
∎

Finally, our main result, stated in Theorem
3.1
, follows from the next result, which asserts the local-in-time solvability of (
28
).

Theorem 4.4
.

Let Assumption
A
be satisfied.
Specifically,

\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\left({{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}(y,0)}}\right)>0,\qquad y\in\Gamma.

(A3)

Then, problem (
28
) has a unique solution
({\tilde{\rho}}(y,t),U_{\text{D}}(y,t),U_{\text{N}}(y,t))
,
y\in\Omega
, that is defined for
t\in{{I}^{\star}}
with
{{t}^{\star}}<T
, and such that the following regularities hold:

{\tilde{\rho}}\in{{C}}^{0}({{I}^{\star}};{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})),\quad{\tilde{\rho}}_{t}\big|_{\Gamma}\in{{C}}^{0}({{I}^{\star}};{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma)),\quad U_{\text{D}}(y,t),\ U_{\text{N}}(y,t)\in{{C}}^{0}({{I}^{\star}};{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})).

Moreover, the following estimate holds

\left\|{{U_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\tilde{\rho}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\leqslant c{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|(f,g)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Sigma;\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c\,{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|(f,g)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|},

(37)

for some constant
c>0
, for all
t\in{{I}^{\star}}
.

5
Regularity of Solutions on a Fixed Domain

Our immediate aim is to distinguish between the linear components of the main conditions and the dynamic boundary conditions in the nonlinear problem arising from (
28
). The complete formulation of this nonlinear problem will be formally presented in Section
6
, specifically in equation (
47
). This formulation is derived by introducing two new variables that represent the difference between the solutions of two states: one corresponding to the transformed problem on a varied domain (cf. (
28
)) and the other corresponding to the problem on a fixed domain, which will be introduced in this section.
Part of the analysis requires understanding the unique solvability of a pure Dirichlet problem and a mixed Dirichlet-Neumann problem, as well as obtaining estimates for the solutions of such equations.
In this section, we lay the groundwork for these preparations.

Let
u_{\text{D}}^{0}:=u_{\text{D}}(x,0)
,
f_{0}:=f(\cdot,0)\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma)
, and consider the pure Dirichlet problem

\Delta u_{\text{D}}^{0}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega,\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{u_{\text{D}}^{0}=f_{0}\quad\text{on}\ \Sigma}},\qquad u_{\text{D}}^{0}=0\quad\text{on}\ \Gamma.

(38)

The solvability of (
38
) in the space
{{C}}^{k+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
(
\Omega
a bounded region) is well-known and is given, for instance, in
[
46
, Thm. 1.1, p. 107]
or
[
47
, Chap. V.36, I, p. 166]
(see also Kellogg’s Theorem
[
41
,
58
]
).
In particular, we have the following result.

Lemma 5.1
.

For any
f_{0}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma)
, there exists a unique solution
u_{\text{D}}^{0}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
to (
38
).

Proof.

See, for example,
[
31
, Thm. 6.14, p. 107]
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

It is worth to emphasize here that, for
k=0,1,\ldots
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the space
{{C}}^{k+\alpha}(\Omega)
essentially coincides with
{{C}}^{k+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
for any open (bounded) set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

[
27
, Prop. 1.1.7, p. 6]
.

Now, let
u_{\text{N}}^{0}:=u_{\text{N}}(x,0)
,
g_{0}:=g(\cdot,0)\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
, and consider the following mixed Dirichlet-Neumann boundary value problem

\Delta u_{\text{N}}^{0}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega,\qquad\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}^{0}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}=g_{0}\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad u_{\text{N}}^{0}=0\quad\text{on}\ \Gamma.

(39)

In the next proposition, we aim to prove that
u_{\text{N}}^{0}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
, provided that
g_{0}\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an annular open bounded set with
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
boundary
\partial\Omega=\Sigma\cup\Gamma
and
g_{0}\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
.
Then Equation (
39
) has the unique solution
u_{\text{N}}^{0}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
.

Proof.

Let us choose an annular connected open bounded set
\Omega_{1}
with exterior boundary
\Gamma
and interior boundary
S
of class
{{C}}^{0,\alpha}\cap{{C}}^{\infty}
.
We denote by
\Omega_{2}
the annular open bounded set contained in
\Omega
whose boundary are
S
and
\Sigma
.
Obviously,
\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}=\emptyset
and
\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2}=\Omega
.
We then consider the following pure Dirichlet boundary value problem

\Delta{{V}}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega_{1},\qquad{{V}}=u_{\text{N}}^{0}\quad\text{on}\ S,\qquad{{V}}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}.

(40)

By
[
26
, Thm. 3, p. 316]
, we know that
u_{\text{N}}^{0}\in{{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, in particular,
u_{\text{N}}^{0}\in{{C}}^{2}(\Omega)
.
Hence,
u_{\text{N}}^{0}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}_{1})
by
[
31
, Thm. 4.6, p. 60]
.
This implies that the above problem admits a unique solution
w\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}_{1})
because of
[
31
, Thm. 6.8, p. 100]
(see also
[
46
, Thm. 1.3, p. 107]
).
Since
u_{\text{N}}^{0}\big|_{\overline{\Omega}_{1}}
also solves (
40
), then by uniqueness, we have
w\equiv u_{\text{N}}^{0}\big|_{\overline{\Omega}_{1}}
.

Let us next consider the following boundary value problem:

\Delta v-v=-u_{\text{N}}^{0}\quad\text{in}\ \Omega_{2},\qquad\dfrac{\partial{v}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}=g_{0}\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad\dfrac{\partial{v}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}=\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}^{0}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\quad\text{on}\ S.

(41)

Since
u_{\text{N}}^{0}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}_{2})
,
\nabla u_{\text{N}}^{0}\cdot{\nu}\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(S)
, and
g_{0}\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
, then by
[
49
, Thm. 5.2]
, we see that (
41
) admits a unique solution
v\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}_{2})
.
Because
u_{\text{N}}^{0}\big|_{\overline{\Omega}_{2}}
also solves (
41
), uniqueness then implies that
v\equiv u_{\text{N}}^{0}\big|_{\overline{\Omega}_{2}}
.
This concludes that Equation (
39
) admits a unique solution in
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
.
∎

Let
U^{0}_{\text{D}}:=U^{0}_{\text{D}}(y,t)
,
y\in\Omega
,
t>0
, and
f(\cdot,t)\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma)
, for all
t\geqslant 0
.
Then, it can be checked that the boundary value problem

\Delta U^{0}_{\text{D}}=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\qquad\left.{U^{0}_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=f(y,t),\qquad\left.{U^{0}_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,

(42)

admits a unique solution
U^{0}_{\text{D}}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
, for all
t\geqslant 0
, where
t
is a parameter appearing in the boundary condition on
\Sigma
.
The solution to (
42
) satisfies the inequality condition

{\max_{0\leqslant\tau\leqslant t}}\left|{U^{0}_{\text{D}}(\cdot,\tau)}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\leqslant c(d,\Omega,\alpha){\max_{0\leqslant\tau\leqslant t}}\left|{f(\cdot,\tau)}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(2+\alpha)},

(43)

Similarly, for
U^{0}_{\text{N}}:=U^{0}_{\text{N}}(y,t)
,
y\in\Omega
,
t>0
, and
g(\cdot,t)\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
for all
t\geqslant 0
, the boundary value problem

\Delta U^{0}_{\text{N}}=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\qquad\left.{\dfrac{\partial{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=g(y,t),\qquad\left.{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,

(44)

admits a unique solution
U^{0}_{\text{N}}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
.
Again,
t
is a parameter appearing in the boundary condition on
\Sigma
.

Next, we want to prove an estimate for
\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}
in terms of
\left|{g}\right|{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)}
.
More precisely, we aim to establish the validity of the following proposition.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open, bounded, and connected set with
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
regularity and
g\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma))
be given.
If
U^{0}_{\text{N}}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
is a solution to (
44
), then we have the following estimate

{\max_{0\leqslant\tau\leqslant t}}\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}(\cdot,\tau)}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\leqslant c(d,\Omega,\alpha){\max_{0\leqslant\tau\leqslant t}}\left|{g(\cdot,\tau)}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)},

(45)

where
c(d,\Omega,\alpha)>0
is a constant that depends only on the dimension
d
, the set
\Omega
, and the number
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

Let
g\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma))
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
t\in[0,T]
, and
\tau<t
be fixed.
Note that it is enough to prove that
\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\leqslant c\left|{g}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)}
, for some constant
c>0
.
In the proof, we abuse some notations (i.e., we write
\varphi
instead of
\varphi(\xi,\tau)=:\varphi(\xi)
as
\tau
is fixed for the functions involved here) and assume that
U^{0}_{\text{N}}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
is a solution to (
44
).
We consider a cutoff function
\phi\in{{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\phi=1
near
\Sigma
and
\phi=0
near
\Gamma
.
Next, we define the functions
{v}_{\text{N}}:=\phi U^{0}_{\text{N}}
and
{v}_{\text{D}}:=(1-\phi)U^{0}_{\text{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\Delta{v}_{\text{N}}={h}_{\phi}\quad\text{in}\ \Omega,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial{{v}_{\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}=g\quad\text{on}\in\Sigma,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial{{v}_{\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$};

\displaystyle\ \Delta{v}_{\text{D}}=-{h}_{\phi}\quad\text{in}\ \Omega,

\displaystyle{v}_{\text{D}}=0\quad\text{on}\in\Sigma,

\displaystyle{v}_{\text{D}}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$},

where
{h}_{\phi}:=(\Delta\phi)U^{0}_{\text{N}}+\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla U^{0}_{\text{N}}
.
By these constructions, notice that
U^{0}_{\text{N}}={v}_{\text{N}}+{v}_{\text{D}}
.
Now, by
[
31
, Thm. 6.30, equ. (6.77), p. 127]
, we immediately get the following estimates

\displaystyle\left|{{v}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{\text{0N}}(d,\Omega,\alpha)\left(\left|{{v}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}+\left|{g}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)}+\left|{{h}_{\phi}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0+\alpha)}\right)\leqslant c_{\text{1N}}(d,\Omega,\alpha)\left(\left|{g}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)}+\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(1+\alpha)}\right),

\displaystyle\left|{{v}_{\text{D}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{\text{0D}}(d,\Omega,\alpha)\left|{{h}_{\phi}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0+\alpha)}\leqslant c_{\text{1D}}(d,\Omega,\alpha)\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0+\alpha)},

where
c_{\text{0N}},c_{\text{1N}},c_{\text{0D}},c_{\text{1D}}>0
.
These inequalities clearly implies that

\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\leqslant c_{0}(d,\Omega,\alpha)\left(\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(1+\alpha)}+\left|{g}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)}\right).

The quantity
\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(1+\alpha)}
can be estimated as follows (see, e.g.,
[
31
, Lem. 6.35, p. 135]
):

\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(1+\alpha)}\leqslant c_{1}(\varepsilon,\Omega)\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}+\varepsilon\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)},

for some constant
c_{1}(\varepsilon,\Omega)>0
.
Using the above interpolation inequality, we get

\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\leqslant c_{2}(n,\Omega,\alpha,\varepsilon)\left(\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}+\left|{g}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)}\right),

(46)

for some constant
c_{2}(n,\Omega,\alpha,\varepsilon)>0
, and we want to prove that we actually have (
45
).
To do this, we mimic an argument in showing inequalities between two equivalent norms which is also similar to a proof of Poincaré’s inequality via a contradiction (see, e.g.,
[
26
, Proof of Theorem 1, pp. 275–276]
or
[
19
, Lem. 49.30, p. 1037]
).
Analogous proof can also be found in
[
64
, Proof of Theorem 3.28, pp. 194–195]
.

The argument to prove (
45
) will proceed by contradiction.
So, let us suppose that inequality (
45
) does not hold.
This means that we can find a sequence
\{U^{0}_{\text{N}k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Omega)
and
\{g_{k}\}\subset{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
such that

\Delta U^{0}_{\text{N}k}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega,\qquad\dfrac{\partial{U^{0}_{\text{N}k}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}={g_{k}}\quad\text{on}\in\Sigma,\qquad U^{0}_{\text{N}k}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$},

and, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
U^{0}_{\text{N}k}
satisfies

\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}k}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}k}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\geqslant k\left|{{g_{k}}}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)}.

Letting
k\to\infty
, we get
{g_{k}}\to 0
in
{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
because
\frac{1}{k}\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}k}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\to 0
.
By the first condition above, we see that for every multi-index
\beta
, with
\left|{\beta}\right|=0,1,2
, the sequence
\{D^{\beta}U^{0}_{\text{N}k}\}
is uniformly bounded in
{{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Consequently, the sequence is equicontinuous because the inequality

\left|{D^{\beta}U^{0}_{\text{N}k}(\xi_{1})-D^{\beta}U^{0}_{\text{N}k}(\xi_{2})}\right|\leqslant\left|{\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}}\right|^{\alpha},\qquad\text{for all}\ \xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Omega,\quad\text{for all}\ \left|{\beta}\right|=2,

actually implies that

\left|{D^{\beta}U^{0}_{\text{N}k}(\xi_{1})-D^{\beta}U^{0}_{\text{N}k}(\xi_{2})}\right|\leqslant m_{0}\left|{\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}}\right|^{\alpha},\qquad\text{for all}\ \xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Omega,\quad\text{for all}\ \left|{\beta}\right|=0,\ 1,

for some constant
m_{0}=m_{0}(\Omega)>0
according to
[
27
, Prop. 1.5.2, p. 34, Thm. 4.4.1, p. 109, or Thm. 4.4.2, p. 120]
.
By iteratively applying the Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem (see, e.g.,
[
26
, Sec. C.7, pp. 634–635]
or
[
18
, Thm. 2.86, p. 48]
), we obtain a subsequence (which we denote with the same notation) such that

U^{0}_{\text{N}k}\to U^{0}_{\text{N}}\quad\text{in ${{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega})$},\qquad\text{and}\qquad D^{\beta}U^{0}_{\text{N}k}\to D^{\beta}U^{0}_{\text{N}}\quad\text{in ${{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega})$},\quad\text{for all}\ \left|{\beta}\right|=1,\ 2,

which implies that
U^{0}_{\text{N}k}\to U^{0}_{\text{N}}
in
{{C}}^{2}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Consequently, we arrive at the following limits

\Delta U^{0}_{\text{N}}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\Delta U^{0}_{\text{N}k}=0,\qquad\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}U^{0}_{\text{N}}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}U^{0}_{\text{N}k}=\lim_{k\to\infty}{g_{k}}=0,

from which we obtain

\Delta U^{0}_{\text{N}}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega,\qquad\dfrac{\partial{U^{0}_{\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}=0\quad\text{on}\in\Sigma,\qquad U^{0}_{\text{N}}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}.

This implies that
U^{0}_{\text{N}}\equiv 0
.
Comparing this with the first estimate (
73
) yields a contradiction because the subsequence
\{U^{0}_{\text{N}k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies

1=\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}k}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\leqslant c_{0}(d,\Omega,\alpha)\left[\left|{U^{0}_{\text{N}k}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}+\left|{{g_{k}}}\right|_{\Sigma}^{(1+\alpha)}\right]\longrightarrow 0.

Thus, inequality (
45
) holds true.
Taking the supremum of
\tau<t\in[0,T]
yields the desired estimate.
∎

6
The Nonlinear Problem

In this section, we distinguish the linear part of the main and dynamic boundary conditions of the nonlinear problem (
47
).
To do this, we compute the variations of the operators given in (
30
) with respect to
\tilde{\rho}
.
We introduce the new unknown functions

{V}_{\text{D}}=U_{\text{D}}-U^{0}_{\text{D}}
and
{V}_{\text{N}}=U_{\text{N}}-U^{0}_{\text{N}}
.

Hence, using the equations in (
31
), we may write (
28
) in the following form

\left\{\begin{aligned} \pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}_{\text{D}}}+(\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}-\Delta)U^{0}_{\text{D}}+(\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}_{\text{D}}}+\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U^{0}_{\text{D}}})\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{\tilde{\rho}}}+(\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}_{\text{D}}}+\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U^{0}_{\text{D}}})&=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
\left.{{V}_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{{V}_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=0,\\[5.0pt]
\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}_{\text{N}}}+(\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}-\Delta)U^{0}_{\text{N}}+(\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}_{\text{N}}}+\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U^{0}_{\text{N}}})\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{\tilde{\rho}}}+(\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}_{\text{N}}}+\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{U^{0}_{\text{N}}})&=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
\left.{\dfrac{\partial{{V}_{\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{{V}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=0,\\[5.0pt]
\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial{t}}+B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}\dfrac{\partial{{{V}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}\dfrac{\partial{{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}^{0}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=0,\\
{\tilde{\rho}}\big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(47)

where
\tilde{\rho}=\tilde{\rho}(y,t)=E\rho(y,t)
,
E
being the operator (
24
), satisfies (
25
).

In order to identify the linear part of the main equations and of the dynamic boundary condition in (
47
), we introduce the variation

\delta\pazocal{{F}}_{0}=\left.\frac{d}{d\lambda}\pazocal{{F}}_{\lambda{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|_{\lambda=0},

of the operator
\pazocal{{F}}\in\{\pazocal{{L}},\pazocal{{K}},\pazocal{{M}}\}
which depends on
{\tilde{\rho}}
.
Note that the map
[\tilde{\rho}\mapsto\pazocal{{F}}_{\tilde{\rho}}]
, where
\pazocal{{F}}_{\tilde{\rho}}\in\{\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}},\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}},\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}\}
, are analytic; see (
32
).
Also, we note of the following identities:

\displaystyle\pazocal{{L}}_{0}{V}_{i}

\displaystyle=\Delta{V}_{i},\qquad\pazocal{{M}}_{0}{V}_{i}=0,\qquad\pazocal{{K}}_{0}{V}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nabla{V}_{i},\qquad B_{0}=\frac{1}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nu_{\circ}},\qquad i=\text{D},\text{N}.

Because
\pazocal{{M}}_{0}\equiv 0
and
\pazocal{{L}}_{0}{{\tilde{\rho}}}\equiv 0
, then for
i=\text{D},\text{N}
, we can write the main equations in (
47
) posed over
\Omega
in the following way:

\displaystyle\pazocal{{L}}_{0}{{V}_{i}}+\delta\pazocal{{L}}_{0}{U_{i}}+\delta\pazocal{{M}}_{0}{U_{i}}+\pazocal{{K}}_{0}{U_{i}}\pazocal{{L}}_{0}{{\tilde{\rho}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=-(\pazocal{{L}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{L}}_{0}){V}_{i}-(\pazocal{{L}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{L}}_{0}-\delta\pazocal{{L}}_{0})U_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-(\pazocal{{M}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{M}}_{0}){V}_{i}-(\pazocal{{M}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{M}}_{0}-\delta\pazocal{{M}}_{0})U_{i},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-(\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}_{i}}+\pazocal{{K}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}{U_{i}})(\pazocal{{L}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{L}}_{0}{{\tilde{\rho}}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=:\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{i},{\tilde{\rho}}].

On the other hand, the dynamic boundary condition on
\Gamma
in (
47
) can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial{t}}+B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{{V}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+\left.\delta B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}^{0}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right|_{\Gamma}

(48)

\displaystyle=-\left[(B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-B_{0}-\delta B_{0})\dfrac{\partial{{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}^{0}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+(B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-B_{0})\dfrac{\partial{{{{V}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}^{0}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right]

\displaystyle=:\pazocal{{B}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}]-B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}^{0}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}.

The computations of the variations
\delta\pazocal{{L}}_{0}
,
\delta\pazocal{{K}}_{0}
,
\delta\pazocal{{M}}_{0}
, and
\delta\pazocal{{B}}_{0}
, which consists of the variations of
\delta B_{0}
and/or
\delta\textsf{A}^{(0)}
, can be done without difficulty (see Appendix
D
). In fact, it can be checked, for instance, that

\displaystyle\delta\pazocal{{L}}_{0}U_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}\delta{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}^{(0)}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{i}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{p}},\qquad(i=\text{D},\text{N}),\qquad\delta{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}^{(0)}=-\sum_{m=1}^{d}\left(\frac{\partial(N_{m}{\tilde{\rho}})}{\partial y_{p}}+\frac{\partial(N_{p}{\tilde{\rho}})}{\partial y_{m}}\right),

\displaystyle\delta B_{0}

\displaystyle=-2\nu_{\circ}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}-\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nu_{\circ}}+h(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}},\partial_{y}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}){\tilde{\rho}},

where

h(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}},\partial_{y}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}):=-\frac{2\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nu_{\circ}}+\frac{1}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nu_{\circ}\right)^{2}}\left(\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\right).

Note that
\delta B_{0}
is well-defined because of Assumption (
A2
).

Observe from the above expressions that the variations
\delta\pazocal{{L}}_{0}
and
\delta B_{0}
only consist of first-order derivatives of
{\tilde{\rho}}
.
Because
{\tilde{\rho}}
is satisfies the Laplace equation (
25
), the linear part of the first and fourth equation in problem (
47
) do not contain the second-order partial derivatives of the function
{\tilde{\rho}}
.
Therefore, we are able to reduce the problem as follows:

{\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta{V}_{\text{D}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}+{Q}_{\text{D}}{\tilde{\rho}}=\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{\tilde{\rho}}],&\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
\left.{{V}_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{{V}_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,&\\[5.0pt]
\Delta{V}_{\text{N}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}+{Q}_{\text{N}}{\tilde{\rho}}=\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}],&\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
\left.{\dfrac{\partial{{V}_{\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{{V}_{\text{N}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,&\\[5.0pt]
\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial{t}}+b_{\perp}\dfrac{\partial{{\tilde{\rho}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}_{\parallel}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}{\tilde{\rho}}+h{\tilde{\rho}}+B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{V}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=\pazocal{{B}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}]-B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{{U}_{\text{D}\text{N}}^{0}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}},\\
{\tilde{\rho}}\big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}=0,&\end{aligned}\right.}

(49)

where
h:=h(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}},\partial_{y}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})
.
Here,
\pazocal{{B}}
and
\pazocal{{N}}
are the nonlinear terms.
The functions
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}
and
{Q}_{\text{D}}
depend on
U^{0}_{\text{D}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
, and their derivatives,
while the functions
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}
and
{Q}_{\text{N}}
depend on
U^{0}_{\text{N}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
, and their derivatives.
The functions
b_{\perp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}_{\parallel}
depend on
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}
and its derivatives.

At this point, it is important to know the sign of the coefficient
b_{\perp}
of
\dfrac{\partial{{\tilde{\rho}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}
appearing in the boundary equation on
\Gamma
(cf. (
48
)).
By examining the explicit form of
\delta B_{0}
, (
A2
), and (
A3
), we deduce that
b_{\perp}<0
.

7
The Linear Problem

Our goal here is to prove the existence of classical solution to a linear problem corresponding to (
49
).
That is, we study the following system of partial differential equations:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta w_{\text{D}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}\cdot\nabla\theta+{Q}_{\text{D}}\theta&=F_{\text{D}}(y,t),\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
\left.{w_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{w_{\text{D}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=0,\\[5.0pt]
\Delta w_{\text{N}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}\cdot\nabla\theta+{Q}_{\text{N}}\theta&=F_{\text{N}}(y,t),\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
\left.{\dfrac{\partial{w_{\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{w_{\text{N}}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=0,\\[5.0pt]
\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial{\theta}}{\partial{t}}+b_{\perp}\dfrac{\partial{\theta}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}_{\parallel}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta+h\theta+B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{w}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=\psi(y,t),\\
{\theta}\big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(50)

where
\theta=\theta(y,t)
, for
t>0
, is harmonic in
y\in\Omega
and vanishes on
\Sigma
(i.e.,
\theta\big|_{\Sigma}=0
).

In connection with the above problem, we will prove the following result
under the crucial condition
b_{\perp}<0
(cf.
[
3
, equ. (3.4)]
).
If this condition is violated, the moving boundary problem (
1
) may become ill-posed; that is, it may fail to admit a classical solution; cf.
[
42
, Thm. 2.1]
for a related issue in Hele-Shaw flows (see also
[
32
]
and
[
25
, Rem. 5.3]
).

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\Sigma,\Gamma\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
suppose that the coefficients in (
50
) satisfy the following conditions

\left\{\begin{aligned} b_{\perp},\ h&\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma)),\\
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}_{\parallel}&\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma)^{d}),\\
{Q}_{\text{D}},\ {Q}_{\text{N}}&\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{0+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})),\\
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}},\ \boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}&\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{0+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}),\\
b_{\perp}&<0.\end{aligned}\right.

For any
F_{\text{D}},F_{\text{N}}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{0+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
and
\psi\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma))
,
system (
50
) has a unique solution

w_{\text{D}},\ w_{\text{N}},\ \theta\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))

with
\theta
having additional smoothness with respect to
t
on the surface
\Gamma
.
That is,
\theta_{t}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma))
.
In addition, the following estimate hold:

\left\|{{w_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left\|{\theta}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c\left(\left\|{{F_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}}\right\|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\psi}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\right),

(51)

for some constant
c>0
.

To solve system (
50
), we will apply the method of successive approximations (see, e.g.,
[
31
, p. 74]
or
[
65
, Sec. 1.1.1, p. 124]
).
At each step, three problems are solved: the first two are elliptic equations whose coefficients and unknown functions depend on time like a parameter (this corresponds to the pure Dirichlet problem and mixed Dirichlet-Neumann problem in (
50
)), and the problem for an elliptic equation with the time-derivative in the boundary condition (this corresponds to the last two equations in (
50
)).
In the next several lines, we focus on the existence of solution to the last mentioned problem.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open, bounded, connected set with boundary
\partial\Omega=\Gamma\cup\Sigma
, where
\Gamma,\Sigma\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
are disjoint surfaces, and
\Gamma
is interior to
\Sigma
.
Let us consider and examine the following system:

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\Theta=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\qquad\left.{\Theta}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=\psi_{2},\qquad{\Theta}\big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}&=0,\\
\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial\Theta}{\partial t}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}\cdot\nabla\Theta+{\kappa}\Theta\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}&=\psi_{1}.\end{aligned}\right.

(52)

Lemma 7.2
.

Let the coefficients in (
52
) satisfy the following conditions

b_{i}\ (i=1,\ldots,d),\ {\kappa}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma)),\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}\cdot\nu<-b_{0}<0,\quad(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{d})^{\top}),

for some constant
b_{0}>0
.
For any given boundary data

\psi_{1}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma))
and
\psi_{2}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\Sigma))
,

there exists a unique solution
\Theta\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))
to problem (
52
), and such that the following estimate hold

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Theta\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\leqslant c\left(\left|{\psi_{1}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\psi_{2}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Sigma;\,[0,t]}}\right),\qquad t\leqslant T,

(53)

where the constant
c>0
depends on the coefficients in the boundary condition.

Proof.

The proof is given in Appendix
E.3
. ∎

We are now in the position to prove Theorem
7.1
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

We first confirm estimate (
51
).
To this end, we write the main equations in (
50
) in the form

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta w_{\text{D}}&={H}_{\text{D}}\equiv F_{\text{D}}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}\cdot\nabla\theta-{Q}_{\text{D}}\theta,\\
\Delta w_{\text{N}}&={H}_{\text{N}}\equiv F_{\text{N}}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}\cdot\nabla\theta-{Q}_{\text{N}}\theta.\end{aligned}\right.

(54)

We consider the Poisson equation above with the homogeneous Dirichlet condition on the whole boundary
\partial\Omega
.
According to a classical result concerning the pure Dirichlet problem (cf.
[
46
, Eq. (1.11), p. 110]
), the following estimate holds:

\left|{w_{\text{D}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c\left(\left|{{H}_{\text{D}}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{w_{\text{D}}}\right|\right)
,
for some constant
c>0
.
We can disregard the term
{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{w_{\text{D}}}\right|
because, as we will verify later in the proof, the Poisson problem has a unique solution (cf.
[
46
, p. 110]
).
Hence, we get the bound

\left|{w_{\text{D}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c_{\text{D}}\left(\left|{\theta}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\theta}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{F_{\text{D}}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right),

(55)

where
c_{\text{D}}>0
is a constant that depends only on
{Q}_{\text{D}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}
.

For the second equation in (
54
), we consider the boundary problem for the Poisson equation with homogenous Neumann condition on
\Sigma
and a homogenous Dirichlet condition on
\Gamma
.
Using the result from
[
64
, Thm. 3.28(ii), p. 194]
, we have the estimate

\left|{w_{\text{N}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c\left|{{H}_{\text{N}}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\\
\leqslant c_{\text{N}}\left(\left|{\theta}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\theta}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{F_{\text{N}}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right),

(56)

for some constant
c,c_{\text{N}}>0
that depends only on
{Q}_{\text{N}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}
.

Next, we consider the last two equations in system (
50
) and apply estimate (
53
) with

\psi_{1}=\psi-B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{w}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}

to obtain

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\theta\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\leqslant c\left|{\psi_{1}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\\
\leqslant c\left(\left|{\psi}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}+\left\|{{w_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\right),

for some constant
c>0
.
Combining this estimate with (
55
) and (
56
), we get a new estimate

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\theta\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\leqslant c\left(\left|{\psi}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\theta}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\theta}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left\|{{F_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}}\right\|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right).

It only remains to estimate the sum
\left|{\theta}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\theta}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}
.
To get rid of these terms on the right hand side of the above inequality, we simply apply the same argument used in the latter part of the proof of Lemma
7.2
.
That is, we utilize the interpolation inequalities (
E.4
), apply the maximum principle (since
\theta
is harmonic), and then use Grönwall lemma, noting that
\theta(y,t)=0
for
y\in\Gamma
at
t=0
, to eventually get the desired estimate (
51
).
This ends the verification of estimate (
51
).

We next establish the solvability of system (
50
).
Our approach is to apply the method of successive approximation (see, e.g.,
[
24
]
).
To this end, the initial approximation
(w_{\text{D}}^{(0)},w_{\text{N}}^{(0)},\theta^{(0)})
is found by solving the problem

\mathscr{Q}(w_{\text{D}}^{(0)},w_{\text{N}}^{(0)},\theta^{(0)})(y,t)=(F_{\text{D}},F_{\text{N}})(y,t),\qquad y\in\overline{\Omega},\quad t>0,

(57)

where, for
n=0,1,2,\ldots
,

\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned} &\mathscr{Q}(w_{\text{D}}^{(n)},w_{\text{N}}^{(n)},\theta^{(n)})(y,t)=(\Psi_{\text{D}},\Psi_{\text{N}})(y,t),\qquad y\in\overline{\Omega},\quad t>0,\\[10.00002pt]
&\qquad\Updownarrow\\[10.00002pt]
&\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta w_{\text{D}}^{(n)}=\Psi_{\text{D}}(y,t),\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\quad\quad\left.{w_{\text{D}}^{(n)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\quad\left.{w_{\text{D}}^{(n)}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,\\[5.0pt]
&\Delta w_{\text{N}}^{(n)}=\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\text{N}}}}(y,t),\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\quad\ \left.{\dfrac{\partial{w_{\text{N}}^{(n)}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\quad\left.{w_{\text{N}}^{(n)}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,\\[5.0pt]
&\Delta\theta^{(n)}=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\quad\left.{\theta^{(n)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\theta^{(n)}\big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}=0,\\
&\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial{{\theta}^{(n)}}}{\partial{t}}+b_{\perp}\dfrac{\partial{\theta^{(n)}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}_{\parallel}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta^{(n)}+h\theta^{(n)}\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=\psi(y,t)-B_{0}\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial{{w}^{(n)}_{\text{D}\text{N}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}},\end{aligned}\right.\end{aligned}\end{gathered}

(58)

for
n=0,1,\ldots
, where
(w_{\text{D}}^{(n)},w_{\text{N}}^{(n)},\theta^{(n)})
are the unknown triplet, and
(\Psi_{\text{D}},\Psi_{\text{N}})
are a pair of given functions.

Then, we define the approximants
(w_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},w_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},\theta^{(m+1)})
, for
m=0,1,\ldots
, as solution to (
58
) with
n=m+1
; that is,
(w_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},w_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},\theta^{(m+1)})
solves the equation

\mathscr{Q}(w_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},w_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},\theta^{(m+1)})(y,t)=({H}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{H}_{\text{N}}^{(m)})(y,t),\qquad y\in\overline{\Omega},\quad t>0,

(59)

where

{H}_{i}^{(m+1)}\equiv{F}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{i}\cdot\nabla\theta^{(m)}-{Q}_{i}\theta^{(m)},\qquad i=\text{D},\text{N}.

The solvability of the Poisson problem with pure Dirichlet boundary condition is well-known (see, e.g.,
[
31
, Thm. 4.3, p. 56]
).
5
5

5

Because
\Omega
is bounded, its closure is compact.

Meanwhile, because
w_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}(y,t)
vanishes for
y\in\Gamma
,
t>0
, for all
m=0,1,\ldots
, then the Poisson problem with mixed Dirichlet-Neumann boundary condition is also guaranteed to be solvable (see
[
64
, Chap. 3]
).
Together with Theorem
7.1
and Lemma
7.2
, these allow us to infer the unique solvability of (
59
) for all
t\in[0,T]
and for all
m=0,1,\ldots
.
In addition, the solutions satisfy the following estimates
6
6

6

Note here that
\left|{\Theta^{(m)}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}={{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{\Theta^{(m)}}\right|+[\Theta^{(m)}]^{(\alpha)}_{{{{\overline{\Omega}}}}}
.

\displaystyle\left|{w_{i}^{(m+1)}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{i}^{m}\left(\left|{F_{i}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\theta^{(m)}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\theta^{(m)}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right),\qquad i=\text{D},\text{N},

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\theta^{(m+1)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}

\displaystyle\leqslant c\left(\left|{\psi}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}+\left\|{{w_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}^{(m+1)}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\right),

for some constants
c_{i}^{m}:=c_{\text{D}}^{m}(Q_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{i})>0
,
i=\text{D},\text{N}
, and
c>0
.

We first estimate the term
\left|{\Theta^{(m)}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{{{\overline{\Omega}}}}}
via the interpolation inequality

[{\Theta^{(m)}}]_{{{{\overline{\Omega}}}}}^{(\alpha)}\leqslant\varepsilon_{4,m}\left|{\Theta^{(m)}}\right|_{{{{\overline{\Omega}}}}}^{(1)}+c_{\varepsilon_{4,m}}\left|{\Theta^{(m)}}\right|_{{{{\overline{\Omega}}}}}^{(0)},

and choose
\varepsilon_{4,m}>0
small enough so that we can bound the terms above only by
{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{\Theta^{(m)}}\right|
.
Then, we argue as in the latter part of the proof of Lemma
7.2
, to write the estimates above as follows:

\displaystyle\left|{w_{i}^{(m+1)}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{i}^{m}\left(\left|{{F}_{i}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\theta^{(m)}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right),\qquad i=\text{D},\text{N},

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\theta^{(m+1)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}\left(K(t)+\left|{\theta^{(m)}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right),

for some constants
\tilde{c}_{i}^{m}:=\tilde{c}_{i}^{m}(Q_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{i})>0
,
i=\text{D},\text{N}
, and
\tilde{c}>0
, where

K(t):=\left\|{{F_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}}\right\|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\psi}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}.

Moreover, the initial approximant
(w_{\text{D}}^{(0)},w_{\text{N}}^{(0)},\theta^{(0)})
satisfies the estimate

\left\|{w_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}^{(0)}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left\|{\theta^{(0)}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\leqslant c_{0}K(t),

(60)

for some constant
c_{0}>0
.

Let us consider the differences

\varpi_{i}^{(m+1)}=w_{i}^{(m+1)}-w_{i}^{(m)},\quad i=\text{D},\text{N},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\vartheta^{(m+1)}=\theta^{(m+1)}-\theta^{(m)},

where
m=0,1,\ldots
.
Clearly, for
m=0,1,\ldots
,
(\varpi_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},\varpi_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},\vartheta^{(m+1)})
satisfies

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta\varpi_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}=-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}\cdot\nabla\vartheta^{(m)}-{Q}_{\text{D}}\vartheta^{(m)},\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\quad\left.{\varpi_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\quad\left.{\varpi_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,\\[5.0pt]
&\Delta\varpi_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}=-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}\cdot\nabla\vartheta^{(m)}-{Q}_{\text{N}}\vartheta^{(m)},\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\quad\left.{\dfrac{\partial{\varpi_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\quad\left.{\varpi_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,\\[5.0pt]
&\Delta\vartheta^{(m+1)}=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\qquad\left.{\vartheta^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad{\vartheta^{(m+1)}}\Big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}=0,\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial{\vartheta}}{\partial{t}}^{(m+1)}+b_{\perp}\dfrac{\partial{\vartheta^{(m+1)}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}_{\parallel}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\vartheta^{(m+1)}+h\vartheta^{(m+1)}\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=-B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{\varpi}_{\text{N}\text{D}}^{(m+1)}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(61)

Following the previous estimations, it can be deduced that
(\varpi_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},\varpi_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},\vartheta^{(m+1)})
satisfy the estimates

\left\|{\varpi_{\text{D},\text{N}}^{(m+1)}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left\|{\vartheta^{(m+1)}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\leqslant c\left|{\vartheta^{(m)}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}},

(62)

for some constants
c>0
.

We estimate in the next few lines the right hand side of (
62
).
For this purpose, we use the following property of norms on
{{C}}^{k+\alpha}
(see, e.g.,
[
46
, Equ. (5.7), p. 403]
):

\left|{u}\right|_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}^{(k-1+\alpha)}\leqslant\varepsilon_{6}\left|{u}\right|_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}^{(k+\alpha)}+c_{\varepsilon_{6}}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{u}\right|,\quad(k\geqslant 2).

Here,
{\varepsilon_{6}}>0
is an arbitrary small number and
c_{\varepsilon_{6}}\to\infty
as
\varepsilon_{6}\to 0
.
This, together with the fact that
\vartheta^{(m)}(y,0)=0
, for
y\in\Gamma
, for each
m=0,1,\ldots
, leads us to the following estimate

\displaystyle\left|{\vartheta^{(m)}}\right|_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}^{(1+\alpha)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\varepsilon_{6}\left|{\vartheta^{(m)}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+c_{\varepsilon_{6}}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{\vartheta^{(m)}}\right|\right)

(63)

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon_{6}\left|{\vartheta^{(m)}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+c_{\varepsilon_{6}}\int_{0}^{t}{{\max_{\Gamma}}}\left|{\frac{\partial}{\partial s}}\vartheta^{(m)}(\cdot,s)\right|{{{d}}s}.

For
m=0
, it is easy to see that

\Delta\varpi_{\text{D}}^{(1)}=-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}\cdot\nabla\vartheta^{(0)}-{Q}_{\text{D}}\vartheta^{(0)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta\varpi_{\text{N}}^{(1)}=-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}\cdot\nabla\vartheta^{(0)}-{Q}_{\text{N}}\vartheta^{(0)}.

Hence, based on (
63
) and (
E.11
), we can get an estimate for
(\varpi_{\text{D}}^{(1)},\varpi_{\text{N}}^{(1)},\vartheta^{(1)})
given by

\left\|{\varpi_{\text{D},\text{N}}^{(1)}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left\|{\vartheta^{(1)}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\leqslant\underaccent{\tilde}{c}\left|{\vartheta^{(0)}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\leqslant\underaccent{\tilde}{c}_{1}K(t),

for some constants
\underaccent{\tilde}{c}_{1}>0
.

Let us write
\sigma_{m}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}(t)
where
a_{m}(t)
is given by

a_{m}(t):=\left\|{\varpi_{\text{D},\text{N}}^{(m)}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left\|{\vartheta^{(m)}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}.

As shown previously, we have
\sigma_{1}(t)=a_{1}(t)\leqslant\underaccent{\tilde}{c}_{1}K(t)
.
So, from (
62
) and (
63
), with
\varepsilon_{6}>0
taken sufficiently small, we can get a bound for the sum
\sigma_{m}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}(t)
given as follows

\sigma_{m}(t)\leqslant c\left(K(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{t}{{\max_{\Gamma}}}\left|{\frac{\partial}{\partial s}}\vartheta^{(j)}(\cdot,s)\right|{{{d}}s}\right),

for some constant
c>0
(independent of
m
), for all
m=2,3,\ldots
.
Applying Grönwall’s lemma, we deduce that the sums
\sigma_{m}(t)
, for
m=2,3,\ldots
, are also uniformly bounded above by
K(t)
.
Thus, the series sums
\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}(t)
actually converges.
As a result, the sequence
\{(\varpi_{\text{D}}^{(m)},\varpi_{\text{N}}^{(m)},\vartheta^{(m)})\}_{m}
converges in the corresponding norm.
Passing to the limit as
m\to\infty
, we finally get the solution to system (
50
).

The Schauder method used above only proves the existence of a solution to system (
50
), which at the same time satisfies the estimate (
51
).
Thus, it remains only to address the issue of uniqueness of the solution.
To do this, we assume that two solutions,
(\varpi_{\text{D}}^{1},\varpi_{\text{N}}^{1},\vartheta^{1})
and
(\varpi_{\text{D}}^{2},\varpi_{\text{N}}^{2},\vartheta^{2})
, to system (
50
) exist. Clearly, their difference is also a solution to (
50
) with
F_{\text{D}}\equiv 0
,
F_{\text{N}}\equiv 0
, and
\psi\equiv 0
. Moreover, the estimate (
51
) remains valid, from which we see that
\varpi_{\text{D}}^{1}-\varpi_{\text{D}}^{2}\equiv 0
,
\varpi_{\text{N}}^{1}-\varpi_{\text{N}}^{2}\equiv 0
, and
\vartheta^{1}-\vartheta^{2}\equiv 0
. This completes the proof of Lemma
7.2
.
∎

8
Proof of the Main Result

We are now in the position to prove Theorem
4.4
by adapting a technique used in
[
10
, Section 5]
.
Also, we will verify at the end of the section our claims in Theorem
3.1
by using Theorem
4.4
and through the change of variables.
Regarding the latter objective, the interpolation inequalities stated in the lemma below will be central to our proof.

Lemma 8.1
.

For some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open, connected, bounded set of class
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
and
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Then, there exist a constant
\varepsilon_{5}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{5})
the following inequalities hold

\displaystyle{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{\nabla u}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{c_{7}}{\varepsilon}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{u}\right|,

(64)

\displaystyle\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{c_{8}}{\varepsilon^{2}}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{u}\right|,

(65)

where
c_{7}:=c_{7}(d,\Omega,\varepsilon)
and
c_{8}:=c_{8}(d,\Omega,\varepsilon)
are positive constants that depend only on the dimension
d
, the number
\varepsilon
, and
\Omega
.

See Appendix
E.4
for the proof.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

Let us now recall the nonlinear problem (
49
).
We will prove its solvability by giving an estimate for the nonlinear terms

\displaystyle\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{i},{\tilde{\rho}}]

\displaystyle=-(\pazocal{{L}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{L}}_{0}){V}_{i}-(\pazocal{{L}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{L}}_{0}-\delta\pazocal{{L}}_{0})U_{i}

\displaystyle-(\pazocal{{M}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{M}}_{0}){V}_{i}-(\pazocal{{M}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{M}}_{0}-\delta\pazocal{{M}}_{0})U_{i},

\displaystyle-(\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}_{i}}+\pazocal{{K}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}{U_{i}})(\pazocal{{L}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{L}}_{0}{{\tilde{\rho}}})

\displaystyle\text{in $\Omega$},

\displaystyle i=\text{D},\text{N},

\displaystyle\pazocal{{B}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}]

\displaystyle=-\left[(B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-B_{0}-\delta B_{0})\dfrac{\partial{{{U}_{\text{N}\text{D}}^{0}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+(B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-B_{0})\dfrac{\partial{{{{V}_{\text{N}\text{D}}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right]

\displaystyle\text{on $\Gamma$}.

We start by noting the following formulae:

\displaystyle(\pazocal{{F}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{F}}_{0}){{V}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{d\lambda}(\pazocal{{F}}_{\lambda{\tilde{\rho}}}{{V}}){{{d}}\lambda},

\displaystyle(\pazocal{{F}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{F}}_{0}-\delta\pazocal{{F}}_{0}){{V}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\left\{\frac{d}{d\lambda}(\pazocal{{F}}_{\lambda{\tilde{\rho}}}{{V}})-\left.\frac{d}{d\mu}(\pazocal{{F}}_{\mu{\tilde{\rho}}}{{V}})\right|_{\mu=0}\right\}{{{d}}\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(1-\mu)\frac{{{d}}^{2}}{{{d}}\mu^{2}}(\pazocal{{F}}_{\mu{\tilde{\rho}}}{{V}}){{{d}}\lambda},

where the operator
\pazocal{{F}}\in\{\pazocal{{L}},\pazocal{{M}},\pazocal{{B}}\}
.
Using these formulae, and by expanding the derivative
\frac{d}{d\lambda}({{F}}(\lambda{\tilde{\rho}},\lambda\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}))
,
it can be verified that
(\pazocal{{F}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{F}}_{0}){{V}}
,
\pazocal{{F}}\equiv\pazocal{{L}}
,
{{V}}\in\{{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}}\}
, can be expressed as a linear combination of terms containing the products
{\tilde{\rho}}{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}
and
{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}
, for
i,j,k=1,2\ldots,d
.
Here, for notational convenience,
{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}
is used to denote the partial derivative of
{\tilde{\rho}}
with respect to the variable
y_{k}
while
{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}
stands for the second-order partial derivative of
W
with respect to the variables
y_{i}
and
y_{j}
. Meanwhile,
(\pazocal{{F}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{F}}_{0}-\delta\pazocal{{F}}_{0}){U}_{0}
,
\pazocal{{F}}\in\{\pazocal{{L}},\pazocal{{M}},\pazocal{{B}}\}
,
{U}_{0}\in\{U^{0}_{\text{D}},U^{0}_{\text{N}}\}
, can be written as a linear combination of terms containing the products
{\tilde{\rho}}^{2}
,
{\tilde{\rho}}{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}
, and
{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}
(cf.
[
10
, p. 131]
).
Note also that the terms involving
\pazocal{{F}}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}-\pazocal{{F}}_{0}
,
\pazocal{{F}}\in\{\pazocal{{L}},\pazocal{{M}},\pazocal{{B}}\}
, share similar structures.
Indeed, the following expansions hold:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\lambda}({{F}}(\lambda{\tilde{\rho}},\lambda\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}))

\displaystyle={{F}}^{\prime}_{{\tilde{\rho}}}(\lambda{\tilde{\rho}},\nabla\lambda{\tilde{\rho}}){\tilde{\rho}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}{{F}}^{\prime}_{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}}(\lambda{\tilde{\rho}},\nabla\lambda{\tilde{\rho}}){\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}},

\displaystyle\frac{{{d}}^{2}}{{{d}}\mu^{2}}({{F}}(\mu{\tilde{\rho}},\mu\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}))

\displaystyle={{F}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\tilde{\rho}}{\tilde{\rho}}}(\mu{\tilde{\rho}},\nabla\mu{\tilde{\rho}}){\tilde{\rho}}^{2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{d}{{F}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\tilde{\rho}}{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}}(\mu{\tilde{\rho}},\nabla\mu{\tilde{\rho}}){\tilde{\rho}}{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k,j=1}^{d}{{F}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}}(\mu{\tilde{\rho}},\nabla\mu{\tilde{\rho}}){\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}.

Hence, all the nonlinear terms of
\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}]
and
\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{\tilde{\rho}}]
consist of a multiplier term
{\tilde{\rho}}
or a first-order partial derivative
{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}
.
Moreover, they are linear with respect to the second-order derivatives of the unknowns
{V}_{\text{N}}
and
{V}_{\text{D}}
.
Thus, we only need to estimate the product of two functions, one of which contains
{\tilde{\rho}}
or
{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{j}}
.

Let us estimate, for instance, the product
{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}
over the domain
\Omega
, for
i,j,k=1,\ldots,d
,
{{V}}\in\{{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}}\}
.

First, let us note that
\tilde{\rho}
(see (
34
)) and
{{V}}
are
C^{2+\alpha}
smooth on
\overline{\Omega}
.

By using the interpolation inequality (
64
) in Lemma
E.3
we can get the estimate

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(\alpha)}\leqslant{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}}\right|\left|{{{V}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}\leqslant\left(\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{c_{7}}{\varepsilon}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|\right)\left|{{{V}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)},}}

for some constant
c_{7}:=c_{7}(d)>0
.

Note that, in the above, we performed the estimate by first taking the max norm of
{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}
on
\overline{\Omega}
in order to apply (
64
). Alternatively, one could obtain the same estimate by first writing

\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(\alpha)}\leqslant\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}\left|{{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(\alpha)}+\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(\alpha)}\left|{{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)},

and then applying the equalities and interpolation inequalities given by Equations (
E.2
), (
E.3
), (
E.7
), and (
E.8
) in Appendices
E.3
and
E.4
.

Because
{\tilde{\rho}}
vanishes on the exterior boundary
\Sigma

(see (
35
)
), the maximum principle (
[
26
, Chap. 6.4, p. 344]
) implies that
{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|\leqslant{{\max_{\Gamma}}}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|
.
Then, from the previous estimate, and in view of (
E.5
), we get

\displaystyle\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\frac{c_{7}}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{{\max_{\Gamma}}}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{s}(\cdot,s)}\right|{{{d}}s}\right)\left|{{{V}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|{{{V}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\left(\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\frac{c_{7}}{\varepsilon}t\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{\tau}}\right|^{\infty}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\right).

Letting
\varepsilon=t^{\frac{1}{2+\alpha}}
, we get the estimate

\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{y_{k}}{{V}}_{y_{j}y_{i}}}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\leqslant\tilde{c}_{0}t^{\eta}\left|{{{V}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\left(\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{\tau}}\right|^{\infty}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\right),

for some constant
\tilde{c}_{0}>0
, where
\eta=\dfrac{1+\alpha}{2+\alpha}\in(0,1)
, for each function
{{V}}\in\{{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}}\}
.

We next estimate the nonlinear terms in the boundary condition.
Because these terms do not contain the second-order derivatives of the unknown functions – in fact, on the boundary
\Gamma
, we only have linear combinations of the products of
{\tilde{\rho}}
and the unknown functions
{V}_{\text{D}}
and
{V}_{\text{N}}
, up to their first-order derivatives – we can estimate them in the
{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma))
-norm via the interpolation inequality (cf.
(
E.8
)
)

\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|_{\Gamma}^{(2)}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|_{\Gamma}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{c_{8}}{\varepsilon^{2}}{{\max_{\Gamma}}}\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|,

(66)

for some constant
c_{8}:=c_{8}(d)>0
.
The above estimates follows from (
64
), the maximum principle, and the fact that
{\tilde{\rho}}
vanishes on the fixed boundary
\Sigma
.
The end estimate in the
{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma))
-norm is achieved through (
66
) in combination with the identities in (
E.3
) and of the first estimate in (
E.4
).
Now, while keeping these informations in mind, we employ the aforementioned estimates to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{\tilde{\rho}}]}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}]}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\pazocal{{B}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}]}\right|_{\Gamma}^{(1+\alpha)}

(67)

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{1}t^{\eta}\left(\left|{{V}_{\text{D}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{V}_{\text{N}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right)\left(\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{\tau}}\right|^{\infty}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{1}t^{\eta}\left({\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}](t)\right)^{2},

where

{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}](t):=\left\|{{V}_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{\tilde{\rho}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]},

for some constant
\tilde{c}_{1}>0
and
\eta>0
.
Here, the notation
{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}](t)
is introduced for convenience of later use.

The rest of the proof applies the method of successive approximation.
To this end, we will need the following estimate (cf.
[
10
, Eq. 5.3, p. 131]
) in our argumentation further below.
Let us consider the functions

{V}_{\text{D}1},{V}_{\text{D}2},{V}_{\text{N}1},{V}_{\text{N}2},{\tilde{\rho}}_{1},{\tilde{\rho}}_{2}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}))

such that

{\tilde{\rho}}_{1t},{\tilde{\rho}}_{2t}\in{{C}}^{0}([0,T];{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\Gamma)).

Then, by estimate (
67
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{D}2},{\tilde{\rho}}_{2}]-\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{D}1},{\tilde{\rho}}_{1}]}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}2},{\tilde{\rho}}_{2}]-\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}1},{\tilde{\rho}}_{1}]}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}

(68)

\displaystyle+\left|{\pazocal{{B}}[{V}_{\text{D}2},{V}_{\text{N}2},{\tilde{\rho}}_{2}]-\pazocal{{B}}[{V}_{\text{D}1},{V}_{\text{N}1},{\tilde{\rho}}_{1}]}\right|_{\Gamma}^{(1+\alpha)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{2}t^{\eta}\left\{\left(|{V}_{\text{D}2}-{V}_{\text{D}1}|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+|{V}_{\text{N}2}-{V}_{\text{N}1}|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{2}-{\tilde{\rho}}_{1}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\times\sum_{i=1,2}\left(|{\tilde{\rho}}_{i}|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{i\tau}}\right|^{\infty}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{2}-{\tilde{\rho}}_{1}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{2\tau}-{\tilde{\rho}}_{1\tau}}\right|^{\infty}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left.\sum_{i=1,2}\left(\left|{{V}_{\text{D}i}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{V}_{\text{N}i}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{{\tilde{\rho}}_{i}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{2}t^{\eta}\left({\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}},{W}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\varrho}}](t)\right)\left\{{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}1},{V}_{\text{N}1},{\tilde{\rho}}_{1}](t)+{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}2},{V}_{\text{N}2},{\tilde{\rho}}_{2}](t)\right\},

where
{W}_{\text{D}}={V}_{\text{D}2}-{V}_{\text{D}1}
,
{W}_{\text{N}}={V}_{\text{N}2}-{V}_{\text{N}1}
, and
{\tilde{\varrho}}={\tilde{\rho}}_{2}-{\tilde{\rho}}_{1}
,
for some constant
\tilde{c}_{2}>0
.

Now, we consider the initial approximation
({V}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)})
, which we assume satisfies the linear system (
50
) with
F_{\text{D}}(y,t)=0
and
F_{\text{N}}(y,t)=0
for
y\in\Omega
,
t>0
, and
\psi(y,t)=-B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{{U}_{\text{N}\text{D}}^{0}}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}
for
y\in\Gamma
,
t>0
.
Meanwhile, the approximants
\{({V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)})\}_{m}
, for
m=0,1,\ldots
, are defined as solutions to the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}+{Q}_{\text{D}}{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}=\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)}],\qquad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
&\qquad\quad\left.{{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,\\[5.0pt]
&\Delta{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}+{Q}_{\text{N}}{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}=\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)}],\qquad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
&\qquad\quad\left.{\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,\\[5.0pt]
&\Delta{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0\qquad\left.{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\\
&\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}}}{\partial{t}}+b_{\perp}\dfrac{\partial{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}_{\parallel}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}+h{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}+B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{V}_{\text{N}\text{D}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ =\pazocal{{B}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)}]-B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{{U}_{\text{N}\text{D}}^{0}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}},\\
&\qquad{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}}\big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(69)

Our goal now is to show that all approximants are defined on some interval
{{I}^{\star}}
and that the sequence of sub-approximants
\{{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m)}\}
,
\{{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m)}\}
, and
\{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)}\}
converge.

By virtue of Theorem
7.1
, the initial approximation
({V}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)})
is defined for all
t\in[0,T]
and satisfies the estimate

{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)}](t)\leqslant\tilde{c}_{3}\left\|{(U^{0}_{\text{D}},U^{0}_{\text{N}})}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}=:\tilde{c}_{3}\left\|{{{U}_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}^{0}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}},\quad\text{for all $t\leqslant T$},

(70)

for some constant
\tilde{c}_{3}>0
.
Meanwhile, to get an estimate for the first approximant
({V}_{\text{D}}^{(1)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(1)})
, we apply Theorem
7.1
to (
69
) with
m=0
, to obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(1)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(1)}](t)

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{3}\left\{\left|{\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)}]}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)}]}\right|^{(\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right.

(71)

\displaystyle+\left.\left|{\pazocal{{B}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)}]}\right|_{\Gamma}^{(1+\alpha)}+\left\|{{{U}_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}^{0}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{3}\left\{\tilde{c}_{1}t^{\eta}\left({\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)}](t)\right)^{2}+\left\|{{{U}_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}^{0}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{3}^{3}\tilde{c}_{1}t^{\eta}\left(\left\|{{{U}_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}^{0}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}\right)^{2}+\tilde{c}_{3}\left\|{{{U}_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}^{0}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}

\displaystyle=:{{L}}(t),

where the second inequality follows from (
67
), while the third one is due to estimate (
70
).

We introduce the following notations:

{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}={V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}-{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},\qquad{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}={V}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}-{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},\qquad{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(m+1)}={\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}-{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)}.

Then, by telescoping sums, we see that

{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}={V}_{\text{D}}^{(1)}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}{W}_{\text{D}}^{(j+1)},\quad{V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}={V}_{\text{D}}^{(1)}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}{W}_{\text{D}}^{(j+1)},\quad{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}={\tilde{\rho}}^{(1)}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(j+1)}.

Moreover, by (
69
),
{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}
,
{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}
, and
{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(m+1)}
satisfy

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{D}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}+{Q}_{\text{D}}{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ =\pazocal{{N}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)}]-\pazocal{{N}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m-1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m-1)}],\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
&\qquad\quad\left.{{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,\\[10.00002pt]
&\Delta{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{q}}_{\text{N}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}+{Q}_{\text{N}}{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ =\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)}]-\pazocal{{N}}[{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m-1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m-1)}],\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0,\\
&\qquad\quad\left.{\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\qquad\left.{{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}=0,\\[10.00002pt]
&\Delta{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}=0,\quad y\in\Omega,\ t>0\qquad\left.{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}}\right|_{y\in\Sigma}=0,\\
&\left.{\left(\dfrac{\partial{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}}}{\partial{t}}+b_{\perp}\dfrac{\partial{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{b}}_{\parallel}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}+h{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}+B_{0}\dfrac{\partial{{V}_{\text{N}\text{D}}}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\right)}\right|_{y\in\Gamma}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ =\pazocal{{B}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)}]-\pazocal{{B}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m-1)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m-1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m-1)}],\\
&\qquad{{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)}}\big|_{y\in\Gamma,\,t=0}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(72)

We again utilize Theorem
7.1
, and make use of the estimate (
68
), to obtain

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(m+1)}](t)

(73)

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{c}_{2}\tilde{c}_{3}t^{\eta}\left(\sum_{j=m,m-1}{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(j)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(j)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(j)}](t)\right){\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(m)}](t).

We are now in the position to determine the maximum time
{{t}^{\star}}
so that the claims we state in Theorem
4.4
are valid.
First, we choose
{{t}^{\star}}
so that our first approximant satisfies

({{t}^{\star}})^{\eta}{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(1)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(1)}](t)\leqslant({{t}^{\star}})^{\eta}{{L}}(t)\leqslant{{M}_{0}}

and such that
{{M}^{\star}}=2\tilde{c}_{2}\tilde{c}_{3}{{M}_{0}}<M_{1}<1
, for some fixed constant
M_{1}>0
.
Clearly, the initial approximant
{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)}](t)
also satisfy the estimate
({{t}^{\star}})^{\eta}{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(0)}](t)\leqslant{{M}_{0}}
.
We then assume that
({V}_{\text{D}}^{(j)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(j)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(j)})
,
j=0,1,\ldots,m
, are defined for
t\in{{I}^{\star}}
and satisfy the estimate

({{t}^{\star}})^{\eta}{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}}^{(j)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(j)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(j)}](t)\leqslant{{M}_{0}},\quad\text{for all $j=0,1,\ldots,m$}.

(74)

By Theorem
7.1
, it follows that the
(m+1)
th approximant given by the triplet
({V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)})
is also defined in
{{I}^{\star}}
.
Combining estimates (
73
) and (
74
), we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(m+1)}]({{t}^{\star}})

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\tilde{c}_{2}\tilde{c}_{3}{{M}_{0}}{\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(m)}]({{t}^{\star}})

\displaystyle\leqslant{{M}^{\star}}{\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(m)}]({{t}^{\star}}).

Taking the sum of above inequality with respect to
j
from
0
to
m
yields

\displaystyle{{S}}_{m+1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=0}^{m}{\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(j+1)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(j+1)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(j+1)}]({{{t}}})

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant{{M}^{\star}}\sum_{j=0}^{m}{\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(j)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(j)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(j)}]({{{t}}})

\displaystyle\quad={{M}^{\star}}{{S}}_{m+1}+{{M}^{\star}}\left({\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(0)}]({{{t}}})-{\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(m+1)}]({{{t}}})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant{{M}^{\star}}{{S}}_{m+1}+{{M}^{\star}}{\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(0)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(0)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(0)}]({{{t}}})

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant{{M}^{\star}}{{S}}_{m+1}+{{L}}(t),

where the last inequality follows from (
71
) and the fact that
{{M}^{\star}}<1
.
The above estimate clearly shows that the sums
{{S}}_{m+1}
,
m=0,1,\ldots
, are uniformly bounded by
{{L}}(t)
.
This information implies that the series sums
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\mathbb{W}}[{W}_{\text{D}}^{(j+1)},{W}_{\text{N}}^{(j+1)},{\tilde{\varrho}}^{(j+1)}](t)
converges for all
t\in{{I}^{\star}}
and that
\{({V}_{\text{D}}^{(m+1)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m+1)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m+1)})\}_{m}
,
m=0,1,\ldots
, also satisfy inequality condition (
74
).

Finally, passing to the limit
m\to\infty
, we conclude that

({V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}})=\lim_{m\to\infty}({V}_{\text{D}}^{(m)},{V}_{\text{N}}^{(m)},{\tilde{\rho}}^{(m)})

is a solution to (
49
) satisfying the estimate

{\mathbb{W}}[{V}_{\text{D}},{V}_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}}](t)\leqslant c{{L}}(t)\leqslant c\left\|{{{U}_{{\text{D},\text{N}}}^{0}}}\right\|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}},\quad\text{for all $t\in{{I}^{\star}}$},

(75)

for some constant
c>0
.

To complete the proof of Theorem
4.4
, we need to revert to the original unknown functions that resolve the transformed problem (
28
).
As stated in Section
6
, we have the relations
U_{\text{D}}={V}_{\text{D}}+U^{0}_{\text{D}}
and
U_{\text{N}}={V}_{\text{N}}+U^{0}_{\text{N}}
.
These functions, along with
{\tilde{\rho}}
, solve (
28
).
Additionally, estimate (
37
) is derived from (
75
), in conjunction with estimates (
43
) and (
45
).
Furthermore, since the difference between two solutions must also satisfy estimate (
37
) with a zero right-hand side, the solution
(U_{\text{D}},U_{\text{N}},{\tilde{\rho}})
is unique.
Finally, to obtain
\rho
, we set
\rho={\tilde{\rho}}\big|_{\Gamma}
and then determine the free boundary using the description given in (
16
).

\Gamma(t):=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x=\xi+\rho(\xi,t)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(\xi),\ \xi\in\Gamma\right\},\qquad\text{for $t\in{{I}^{\star}}$}.

∎

To conclude this section, we remark – as alluded at an earlier part of this note – that the main result given by Theorem
3.1
follows from Theorem
4.4
which asserts the local-in-time solvability of problem (
28
).
Indeed, recalling the change of variables
Y^{-1}(y,t)
from (
26
), we can transform the fixed domain
\Omega
to the moving domain
\Omega(t)
, for
t\in{{I}^{\star}}
, and retrieve the functions
u_{\text{D}}(x,t)=U_{\text{D}}(y,t)=u_{\text{D}}(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)
and
u_{\text{N}}(x,t)=U_{\text{N}}(y,t)=u_{\text{N}}(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)
(cf. (
C.1
)) as the pair of solutions to (
1
).
In addition, estimate (
17
) immediately follows from (
37
), confirming our assertion.
This verifies Theorem
3.1
.

9
Summary and Final Remarks

In this study, we have rigorously established the well-posedness of the moving boundary problem (
1
), proving the local-in-time existence of classical solutions under key assumptions, including the positivity of the data and Assumption
A
. These conditions form the foundation of our theoretical framework and are essential for ensuring the mathematical consistency of the analysis. Furthermore, the results obtained here implicitly support the stability of numerical schemes designed for related problems in shape optimization.

The problem addressed is closely connected to the classical Hele-Shaw problem in the expanding case, whose well-posedness has been widely studied through various analytical approaches, such as variational inequality formulations and other advanced techniques, as detailed in
[
32
,
28
,
43
,
45
]
.
Despite these developments, several important directions remain open for the problem considered here. Extending the analysis to describe the long-time behavior of solutions and to identify possible steady states presents a natural continuation of this work. Additionally, relaxing the assumptions adopted here—for instance, by reducing the regularity of the moving boundary or by reformulating the problem in a generalized setting—would offer valuable insights, although these extensions are expected to involve significant analytical challenges. The established mathematical tools cited above are anticipated to remain central in addressing these issues.
