Lastly, translating this theoretical framework to more physically relevant or applied contexts offers both promising opportunities and technical difficulties, providing a rich avenue for future investigation.

Appendix A
Comparison of normal derivatives

In this appendix, we examine the normal derivatives of two functions that fulfill a pure Dirichlet problem and a mixed Dirichlet-Neumann problem on the boundary of axisymmetric domains.
Throughout the section,
\rho>0
denotes the radius of either a circle or a sphere.
In this section,
f
and
g
are two fixed positive-valued scalar functions defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

A.1
The case of concentric circles

For axisymmetric case in two spatial dimensions, the Laplace equation becomes

\Delta u(\rho)=\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{d}{d\rho}\left(\rho\frac{d{u}}{d\rho}\right)=0\qquad\text{or}\qquad\Delta u(\rho)=\frac{d}{d\rho}\left(\rho\frac{d{u}}{d\rho}\right)=0.

Integrating once, we get
d{u(\rho)}/{d\rho}={a}/{\rho}
, and then again,
u=a\log\rho+b
, for some unknowns
a
and
b
.

Let
B_{r}:=B(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{0}},r)
be a circle with radius
r>0
.
Let
r^{\star}\in(0,R)
and consider the PDE system

\Delta{{u}^{\star}}=0\quad\text{in}\ {\Omega}^{\star}:=B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{{r}^{\star}},\qquad{{u}^{\star}}=f>0\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad{{u}^{\star}}=0\quad\text{on}\ {\Gamma}^{\star}:=\partial{B_{{r}^{\star}}}.

(A.1)

Then, we can compute the exact solution as

{u}^{\star}(\rho)=\frac{f\log{(\rho/{r}^{\star})}}{\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}},\qquad\rho\in({r}^{\star},R).

(A.2)

Note that for any
\rho\in({r}^{\star},R]
,
u(\rho)
is positive.
Now, differentiating this function with respect to
\rho
, we get

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{R}}{u}^{\star}(\rho)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\left|{x}\right|}{u}^{\star}(\left|{x}\right|)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}{u}^{\star}(\rho)=\frac{f}{\rho\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}},

(A.3)

where
\nu_{R}
is outward unit normal vector to
\partial B_{R}
.
At
\rho=R
, we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{R}}{u}^{\star}(R)=\frac{f}{R\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}}.

We define

g:=\frac{f}{R\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}}>0,\qquad 0<{r}^{\star}<R.

Let us consider Equations (
7
) and (
8
) with
\Omega=B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}
,
\Gamma=\partial{B_{R}}
, and
\Sigma=\partial{B_{R}}
, where
0<r<R
.
In view of (
A.2
), it can easily be verified that the exact solutions to these systems of PDEs are respectively given by

u_{\text{D}}(\rho)=\frac{f\log{(\rho/r)}}{\log{(R/r)}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad u_{\text{N}}(\rho)=\frac{f\log{(\rho/r)}}{\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}},\qquad\rho\in[r,R].

Now, on
\Gamma
, straightforward computation of
V_{n}
gives us

\displaystyle V_{n}

\displaystyle=-\left(\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{D}}}}{\partial\nu}-\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}}}{\partial\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left.\left[\frac{f}{\log{(R/r)}}\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\right)-\frac{f}{\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}}\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\right)\right]\right|_{\rho=r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{f}{r{\log{(R/r)\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}}}}\left[\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}-\log{(R/r)}\right]

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{\frac{f}{r{\log{(R/r)\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}}}}}_{>0}\underbrace{\left[\log{\left(\dfrac{r}{{r}^{\star}}\right)}\right]}_{:=L_{1}(r)}.

Observe that the sign of
V_{n}
only depends on the relations between
r
and
{r}^{\star}
.
If we want
V_{n}
to be negative, we need
L_{1}(r)>0
to be positive, and this happens when
r>{r}^{\star}
.

Now, motivated by the previous discussion, we examine the existence and uniqueness of solution to the following initial value problem: for a given
T>0
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} r^{\prime}(t)&=-\dfrac{f\left(\log{r(t)}-\log{{r}^{\star}}\right)}{r(t)\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}\left(\log{R}-\log{r(t)}\right)}=:F_{1}(t,r(t)),&&\text{for }0<t<T,\\
r(0)&=r_{0},&&r_{0}>{r}^{\star},\end{aligned}\right.

(A.4)

for some given
r_{0}\in({r}^{\star},R)
.

Given the techical assumption stated in Remark
1.3
, we show – although it is trivial – that the initial value problem (
A.4
) admits a unique solution.
We prove the existence using Peano’s Theorem
[
35
, Thm. 2.1, p. 10]
and its uniqueness via Picard-Lindelöf Theorem
[
35
, Thm. 1.1, p. 8]
.
First, we observe that for
t>0
,
0<{r}^{\star}\leqslant r(t)<R
(see the proof of Proposition
A.2
), and for
T>0
,
F_{1}
is continuous, for all
t\in[0,T]
and
\left|{r(t)-r_{0}}\right|\leqslant R-{r}^{\star}
.
Moreover,
r
is uniformly bounded in
[0,T]
, and therefore,
F_{1}(t,r(t))
is bounded in
[0,T]\times[{r}^{\star},R_{0}]
for some
{r}^{\star}<r_{0}\leqslant R_{0}<R
.
In fact, we see that

\left|{F_{1}(t,r(t))}\right|\leqslant\dfrac{\|{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}}{{r}^{\star}\log{(R/R_{0})}}=:K_{0},\qquad\text{for all $(t,r(t))\in[0,T]\times[{r}^{\star},R_{0}]$}.

By Peano’s Theorem
[
35
, Thm. 2.1, p. 10]
, (
A.4
) possesses at least one solution
r=r(t)
on
[0,T_{0}]
where
T_{0}=\min\{T,(R-{r}^{\star})/K_{0}\}
.

Remark A.1
.

The choice
T_{0}=\min\{T,(R-{r}^{\star})/K_{0}\}
for the existence of solution to (
A.4
) is natural.
On the one hand, the requirement
T_{0}\leqslant T
is necessary.
On the other hand, the requirement
T_{0}<(R-{r}^{\star})/K_{0}
is due to the fact that if
r=r(t)
is a solution of (
A.4
) on
t\in[0,T_{0}]
, then
\left|{r^{\prime}(t)}\right|\leqslant K_{0}
implies that
\left|{r(t)-r_{0}}\right|\leqslant R-{r}^{\star}\leqslant K_{0}(t-0)=K_{0}t
.
However, we note that
\left|{r(t)-r_{0}}\right|\leqslant R-{r}^{\star}
.
Thus, we require
t\leqslant(R-{r}^{\star})/K_{0}
so that the previous inequality holds.

To prove that the solution to (
A.4
) is unique, we will prove that
F_{1}
is uniformly Lipschitz continuous with respect to
r
.
Let us consider

F_{1}(r_{j}):=F_{1}(t,r_{j}(t))=-\frac{f\left(\log{r_{j}(t)}-\log{{r}^{\star}}\right)}{r_{j}(t)\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{j}(t)}\right)},\quad\text{for $j=1,2$, and $t>0$},

where
{r}^{\star}<r_{j}\leqslant R_{0}
for
j=1,2
, for some
0<R_{0}<R
.
Hence,we have

\displaystyle\left|{F_{1}(r_{1})-F_{1}(r_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\frac{f\left(\log{r_{1}}-\log{{r}^{\star}}\right)}{r_{1}\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{1}}\right)}-\frac{f\left(\log{r_{2}}-\log{{r}^{\star}}\right)}{r_{2}\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{2}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\dfrac{\|{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}}{\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}}\left|{\frac{r_{2}\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{2}}\right)\left(\log{r_{1}}-\log{{r}^{\star}}\right)-r_{1}\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{1}}\right)\left(\log{r_{2}}-\log{{r}^{\star}}\right)}{r_{1}r_{2}\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{1}}\right)\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{2}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle=:\dfrac{\|{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}}{\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}}\left|{\frac{\textsf{n}(r_{1},r_{2})}{\textsf{d}(r_{1},r_{2})}}\right|,

where

\displaystyle\textsf{n}(r_{1},r_{2})

\displaystyle=(\log{R})r_{2}\log{r_{1}}-r_{2}\log{r_{1}}\log{r_{2}}-\log{{r}^{\star}}(\log{R})r_{2}+(\log{{r}^{\star}})r_{2}\log{r_{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad-(\log{R})r_{1}\log{r_{2}}+r_{1}\log{r_{2}}\log{r_{1}}+\log{{r}^{\star}}(\log{R})r_{1}-(\log{{r}^{\star}})r_{1}\log{r_{1}}

\displaystyle=\log{R}(r_{2}\log{r_{1}}-r_{1}\log{r_{2}})-\log{r_{1}}\log{r_{2}}(r_{2}-r_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad+\log{{r}^{\star}}(r_{2}\log{r_{2}}-r_{1}\log{r_{1}})+\log{{r}^{\star}}\log{R}(r_{1}-r_{2}),

\displaystyle\textsf{d}(r_{1},r_{2})

\displaystyle=r_{1}r_{2}\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{1}}\right)\left(\log{R}-\log{r_{2}}\right).

For
r_{j}>0
such that
{r}^{\star}<r_{j}\leqslant R_{0}<R
for
j=1,2
, the denominator
d
is bounded below away from zero; i.e.,

\left|{\textsf{d}(r_{1},r_{2})}\right|\geqslant\left[{r}^{\star}\left(\log{R}-\log{R_{0}}\right)\right]^{2}=:K_{1}.

Now, on the other hand, we claim that
\textsf{n}(r_{1},r_{2})
can be bounded by
\left|{r_{1}-r_{2}}\right|
; that is, there exists a constant
K_{2}>0
such that

\left|{\textsf{n}(r_{1},r_{2})}\right|\leqslant K_{2}\left|{r_{1}-r_{2}}\right|,

where
K_{2}
does not depends on
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
.

Let us note that

\displaystyle\left|{\textsf{n}(r_{1},r_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\log{R}}\right|\left|{r_{2}\log{r_{1}}-r_{1}\log{r_{2}}}\right|+\left|{\log{r_{1}}}\right|\left|{\log{r_{2}}}\right|\left|{r_{2}-r_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left|{\log{{r}^{\star}}}\right|\left|{r_{2}\log{r_{2}}-r_{1}\log{r_{1}}}\right|+\left|{\log{{r}^{\star}}}\right|\left|{\log{R}}\right|\left|{r_{1}-r_{2}}\right|.

We focus on proving that
\left|{r_{2}\log{r_{1}}-r_{1}\log{r_{2}}}\right|
can be bounded by
\left|{r_{1}-r_{2}}\right|
.
The rest can be shown in a similar manner.
So, let us consider the function
\textsf{f}(r)=r\log{r}
.
We note that
f
is continuous on
[r_{1},r_{2}]
and differentiable on
(r_{1},r_{2})
.
Hence, by the mean value theorem, there exists
\bar{r}\in(r_{1},r_{2})
such that

\log{\bar{r}}+1=\textsf{f}^{\prime}(\bar{r})=\dfrac{\textsf{f}(r_{2})-\textsf{f}(r_{1})}{r_{2}-r_{1}}=\dfrac{r_{2}\log{r_{2}}-r_{1}\log{r_{1}}}{r_{2}-r_{1}},\qquad(0<{r}^{\star}<r_{j}\leqslant R_{0}<R,\ j=1,2).

Because
r_{1},r_{2}>0
,
\left|{\log{\bar{r}}+1}\right|
is clearly bounded and does not depend on
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
.
Therefore,
\left|{r_{2}\log{r_{2}}-r_{1}\log{r_{1}}}\right|=c\left|{r_{2}-r_{1}}\right|
, for some constant of
c>0
.

Combining the bounds on the numerator and denominator, we get

\left|{\textsf{f}(r_{2})-\textsf{f}(r_{1})}\right|\leqslant\frac{K_{2}}{K_{1}}\left|{u_{1}-u_{2}}\right|,

which shows that
\textsf{f}(r)
is uniformly Lipschitz continuous.
Finally, applying Picard-Lindelöf Theorem
[
35
, Thm. 1.1, p. 8]
, we deduce that the solution to (
A.4
) is unique.

A.2
The case of concentric spheres

For axisymmetric case in three spatial dimensions, the Laplace equation becomes

\Delta u(\rho)=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\frac{d}{d\rho}\left(\rho^{2}\frac{du}{d\rho}\right)=0\qquad\text{or}\qquad\Delta u(\rho)=\frac{d}{d\rho}\left(\rho^{2}\frac{du}{d\rho}\right)=0.

Integrating once, we get
{du(\rho)}/{d\rho}={a}/{\rho^{2}}
, and then again,
u(\rho)=-{a}/{\rho}+b
, for some unknowns
a
and
b
.

Let
B_{r}:=B(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{0}},r)
be a sphere with radius
r>0
.
Let
r^{\star}\in(0,R)
and consider again the PDE system (
A.1
) but with
{\Omega}^{\star}:=B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{{r}^{\star}}
and
{\Gamma}^{\star}:=\partial{B_{{r}^{\star}}}
described by the given spheres.
Then, we can compute the exact solution as

{u}^{\star}(\rho)=\frac{fR}{R-{r}^{\star}}\left(1-{r}^{\star}\rho^{-1}\right),\qquad\rho\in({r}^{\star},R).

(A.5)

Observe that for any
\rho\in({r}^{\star},R]
,
u(\rho)
is positive.

Now, we differentiate
{u}^{\star}
with respect to
\rho
, to obtain

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{R}}{u}^{\star}(\rho)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\left|{x}\right|}{u}^{\star}(\left|{x}\right|)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}{u}^{\star}(\rho)=\frac{f}{\rho\log{(R/{r}^{\star})}},

(A.6)

where
\nu_{R}
is outward unit normal vector to
\partial B_{R}
.
At
\rho=R
, we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{R}}{u}^{\star}(R)=\frac{fR{r}^{\star}}{R-{r}^{\star}}\rho^{-2}\Big|_{\rho=R}=\frac{f{r}^{\star}}{R(R-{r}^{\star})}.

We define

g:=\frac{f{r}^{\star}}{R(R-{r}^{\star})}>0,\qquad 0<{r}^{\star}<R.

Let us again consider Equations (
7
) and (
8
), but now in three dimensions, with
\Omega=B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}
,
\Gamma=\partial{B_{R}}
, and
\Sigma=\partial{B_{R}}
, where
0<r<R
.
In view of (
A.5
), it can easily be shown that the exact solutions to these systems of PDEs are respectively given by

u_{\text{D}}(\rho)=\frac{fR}{R-r}\left(1-r\rho^{-1}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad u_{\text{N}}(\rho)=\frac{fR{r}^{\star}}{R-{r}^{\star}}\left(r^{-1}-\rho^{-1}\right),\qquad\rho\in[r,R].

Now, computing
V_{n}
on
\Gamma
yields the following

\displaystyle V_{n}

\displaystyle=-\left(\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{D}}}}{\partial\nu}-\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}}}{\partial\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left.\left[\frac{fR}{R-r}\left(\frac{r}{\rho^{2}}\right)-\frac{fR{r}^{\star}}{R-{r}^{\star}}\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\right)\right]\right|_{\rho=r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{fR}{r}\left[\frac{1}{R-r}-\frac{{r}^{\star}}{(R-{r}^{\star})r}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{fR}{r}\left[\frac{(R-{r}^{\star})r-(R-r){r}^{\star}}{r(R-r)(R-{r}^{\star})}\right]

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{\frac{fR^{2}}{r^{2}(R-r)(R-{r}^{\star})}}_{>0}\underbrace{(r-{r}^{\star})}_{=:L_{2}(r)}.

Notice that, as expected, the sign of
V_{n}
only depends on the relations between
r
and
{r}^{\star}
.
If we want
V_{n}
to be negative, we obviously need
L_{2}(r)>0
to be positive, and this occurs when
r>{r}^{\star}
.

Similar to the previous subsection, one can prove the existence and uniqueness of solution to the following initial value problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} r^{\prime}(t)&=-\frac{fR^{2}(r(t)-{r}^{\star})}{r(t)^{2}(R-r(t))(R-{r}^{\star})}=:F_{2}(t,r(t)),&&\text{for }0<t<T,\\
r(0)&=r_{0},&&r_{0}>{r}^{\star},\end{aligned}\right.

(A.7)

for some given
r_{0}\in({r}^{\star},R)
.
To prove that the right-hand side of (
A.7
) is uniformly Lipshitz continuous with respect to
r
, we can again apply the mean value theorem.
For this purpose, we can utilize the derivative of
G
with respect to
r
given by

F_{2}^{\prime}(\cdot,r)=-\dfrac{fR^{2}(2r^{2}+{r}^{\star}(2R-3r)-Rr)}{r^{3}(R-{r}^{\star})(R-r)^{2}},

and show that this expression is bounded within the interval
[{r}^{\star},R)
.

In view of the existence of unique solution to (
A.4
) and (
A.7
), we formally have the following proposition.

Proposition A.2
.

The unique solutions to (
A.4
) and (
A.7
) satisfy
r^{\prime}(t)<0
, for all
t>0
.

Proof.

From previous discussion, we have deduced that
V_{n}
remains negative for
r>{r}^{\star}
.
We want to prove then that
r(t)\geqslant{r}^{\star}
, for all
t>0
, and we claim that
r(t)\to{r}^{\star}
as
t\to T
where
T=\infty
.
In other words,
r(t)
will not reach
{r}^{\star}
in finite time.
We will prove the latter claim via a contradiction.
That is, we first suppose that there exists a
T<\infty
such that
r(T)={r}^{\star}
and show that this will lead to a contradiction.

To do this, we first comment that
F_{i}(\cdot,r)\in{{C}}^{\infty}(0,R)
, where
F_{i}
,
i=1,2
, are the rational functions given in (
A.4
) and (
A.7
), respectively.
Hence, via Taylor expansion, we can write
r^{\prime}
(after some transformation normalizing the coefficient) as follows

r^{\prime}(t)=({r}^{\star}-r(t))s_{i}(r(t)),

where
s_{i}
are some functions over
r
such that
s_{i}({r}^{\star})>0
for
i=1,2
.
The functions
s_{i}
,
i=1,2
, can be expressed in the form

s_{i}(r)=\frac{r^{\prime}(t)}{{r}^{\star}-r(t)}=\log\left(\frac{1}{r(t)-{r}^{\star}}\right).

Now, if
r\in{{C}}^{1}([0,T))\cap{{C}}^{0}([0,T])
and
r(T)={r}^{\star}
for
0\leqslant a<T-\varepsilon<T<\infty
fo some constant
a
and
\varepsilon>0
then

\int_{a}^{T-\varepsilon}s_{i}(r(t))\ {d}{t}=\left.\log\left(\frac{1}{r(t)-{r}^{\star}}\right)\right|_{a}^{T-\varepsilon}=\log\left(\frac{r(a)-{r}^{\star}}{r(T-\varepsilon)-{r}^{\star}}\right).

Notice that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{a}^{T-\varepsilon}s_{i}(r(t))\ {d}{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\log\left(\frac{r(a)-{r}^{\star}}{r(T-\varepsilon)-{r}^{\star}}\right)=\infty,

which is a contradiction.
∎

Appendix B
Mullins-Sekerka analysis for 2D axisymmetric domains

In this appendix, we examine the main system (
1
) on axisymmetric case in two dimension through Mullins-Sekerka analysis given that
f
and
g
are positive-valued functions.
Let
r_{\Sigma}>0
and
\rho^{0}>0
be given.
We let the exterior (fixed) and the interior (free) boundary be defined by two concentric circles
\Sigma=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\left|{x}\right|=r_{\Sigma}\}
and
\Gamma=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\left|{x}\right|=\rho^{0}\}
.
We define
\Gamma^{0}(t)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\left|{x}\right|=\rho^{0}(t)\}
, and consider, in polar coordinate
(r,\theta)
, its perturbation given by

\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(t)=\{(r,\theta)\mid r=\rho^{\varepsilon}(\theta,t)\},

where we suppose that
\rho^{\varepsilon}
formally has the expansion

\rho^{\varepsilon}(\theta,t)=\rho^{0}(t)+\varepsilon\rho^{1}(\theta,t)+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

\Sigma
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(t)
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(t)
\nu^{\varepsilon}
\nu

Figure 4:
The axisymmetric annular domain

Here, in particular, we shall consider perturbation of the type
\rho^{1}(\theta,t)=R(t)\cos k\theta
or
R(t)\sin k\theta
.
Our aim is to derive a necessary condition of
R(t)
for
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(t)
satisfying our main system (
1
) with suitable
u_{\text{D}}^{\varepsilon}(x,t)
and
u_{\text{N}}^{\varepsilon}(x,t)
, except for the initial condition.
For simplicity, let us suppose that
f
and
g
are positive constants.
We define

\Omega^{\varepsilon}(t)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\rho^{\varepsilon}(t)<\left|{x}\right|<r_{\Sigma}\}.

Of course, we set
\Omega^{0}(t)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\rho^{0}(t)<\left|{x}\right|<r_{\Sigma}\}
.

We consider the following systems of PDEs:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u_{\text{D}}^{\varepsilon}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega^{\varepsilon}(t),\qquad u_{\text{D}}^{\varepsilon}=f>0\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad u_{\text{D}}^{\varepsilon}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}^{\varepsilon}(t),\\
\Delta u_{\text{N}}^{\varepsilon}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega^{\varepsilon}(t),\qquad\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}^{\varepsilon}}}{\partial\nu}=g>0\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad u_{\text{N}}^{\varepsilon}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}^{\varepsilon}(t),\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(B.1)

where
\nu
is the outward unit normal vector to
\Sigma
.
Accordingly, the normal velocity of the moving boundary
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
is given by

v^{\varepsilon}(\theta,t)=-\left(\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{D}}^{\varepsilon}}}{\partial\nu^{\varepsilon}}-\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}^{\varepsilon}}}{\partial\nu^{\varepsilon}}\right),\qquad y\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(t),

where
\nu^{\varepsilon}
is the
inward
unit normal to
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(t)
(see Figure
4
for illustration) given by

\nu^{\varepsilon}(\theta,t)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta\\
\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}+\varepsilon\frac{\rho^{1}_{\theta}(\theta,t)}{\rho^{0}(t)}\begin{pmatrix}\sin\theta\\
-\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

Here, the initial geometric profile of the perturbed boundary is
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(0)=\{(r,\theta)\mid r=\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\theta)\}
where
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\theta)=\rho^{0}_{0}+\varepsilon\rho^{1}_{0}(\theta)+O(\varepsilon^{2})
.
Now, we assume that

u_{\text{p}}^{\varepsilon}(x,t)=u_{\text{p}}^{0}(x,t)+\varepsilon u_{\text{p}}^{1}(x,t)+O(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad\text{p}=\text{D},\text{N},

in a neighborhood of
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(t)
.
So, for
x=\rho^{0}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta\\
\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{0}(t)
and
y=\rho^{\varepsilon}(\theta,t)\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta\\
\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(t)
we have, after applying Taylor’s expansion and combining terms with respect to
\varepsilon
, the following identities

\displaystyle u_{\text{p}}^{\varepsilon}(y,t)

\displaystyle=u_{\text{p}}^{0}(x,t)+\varepsilon\left(u_{\text{p}}^{1}(x,t)+\rho^{1}(\theta,t)\frac{\partial u_{\text{p}}^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)\right)+O(\varepsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle=u_{\text{p}}^{0}(x,t)+\varepsilon\left(u_{\text{p}}^{1}(x,t)+\rho^{1}(\theta,t)\frac{\partial u_{\text{p}}^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)\right)+O(\varepsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u_{\text{p}}^{\varepsilon}}{\partial\nu^{\varepsilon}}(y,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial u_{\text{p}}^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)+\varepsilon\left(\frac{\partial u_{\text{p}}^{1}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)+\rho^{1}(\theta,t)\frac{\partial^{2}u_{\text{p}}^{0}}{\partial{r}^{2}}(x,t)\right)+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

The equations and terms of order
O(\varepsilon^{0})
are as follows:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u_{\text{D}}^{0}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega^{0}(0),\qquad u_{\text{D}}^{0}=f\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad u_{\text{D}}^{0}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}^{0}(0),\\
\Delta u_{\text{N}}^{0}=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega^{0}(0),\qquad\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}^{0}}}{\partial\nu}=g\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\qquad u_{\text{N}}^{0}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}^{0}(0),\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(B.2)

with

\rho_{t}^{0}(t)=-\left(\frac{\partial u_{\text{D}}^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)-\frac{\partial u_{\text{N}}^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)\right),\quad x\in\Gamma^{0}(t),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho^{0}(0)=\rho_{0}^{0}.

(B.3)

Meanwhile, the equations and terms of order
O(\varepsilon^{1})
are as follows:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u_{\text{D}}^{1}&=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega^{0}(t),\\
u_{\text{D}}^{1}&=f\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
u_{\text{D}}^{1}(x,t)+\rho^{1}(\theta,t)\frac{\partial u_{\text{D}}^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)&=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}^{0}(t),\\
\Delta u_{\text{N}}^{1}&=0\quad\text{in}\ \Omega^{0}(t),\\
\dfrac{\partial{u_{\text{N}}^{1}}}{\partial\nu}&=g\quad\text{on $\Sigma$},\\
u_{\text{N}}^{1}(x,t)+\rho^{1}(\theta,t)\frac{\partial u_{\text{N}}^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)&=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma$}^{0}(t),\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(B.4)

with

\rho_{t}^{1}(\theta,t)=-\left[\left(\frac{\partial u_{\text{D}}^{1}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)-\frac{\partial u_{\text{N}}^{1}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)\right)+\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u_{\text{D}}^{0}}{\partial{r}^{2}}(x,t)-\frac{\partial^{2}u_{\text{N}}^{0}}{\partial{r}^{2}}(x,t)\right)\rho^{1}(\theta,t)\right]

(B.5)

and

\rho^{1}(\theta,0)=\rho_{0}^{1}(\theta).

Next, we compute the exact solutions to (
B.2
).
We let

u_{\text{p}}^{0}=C_{\text{p}}\log\dfrac{\left|{x}\right|}{\rho^{0}(t)},\quad\text{for \text{p} = \text{D}, \text{N}, \quad where $\left|{x}\right|=r$}.

Then, for
f(t)=C_{\text{D}}\log\dfrac{r_{\Sigma}}{\rho^{0}(t)}
, we get
C_{\text{D}}(t)=\dfrac{f(t)}{\log(r_{\Sigma}/\rho^{0}(t))}
.
Meanwhile, for
\dfrac{\partial u_{\text{p}}^{0}}{\partial\nu}\Big|_{\Sigma}=\dfrac{C_{\text{p}}}{r_{\Sigma}}=g(t)
at
\text{p}=\text{N}
(note here that
\nu
is the outward unit normal to
\Sigma
), we get
C_{\text{N}}(t)=g(t)r_{\Sigma}
.
Therefore, we obtain the following exact solutions to (
B.2
):

\displaystyle u_{\text{D}}^{0}

\displaystyle=C_{\text{D}}(t)\log\left(\frac{\left|{x}\right|}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)=\frac{f(t)}{\log\left(\frac{r_{\Sigma}}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)}\log\left(\frac{\left|{x}\right|}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right),

\displaystyle u_{\text{N}}^{0}

\displaystyle=C_{\text{N}}(t)\log\left(\frac{\left|{x}\right|}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)=g(t)r_{\Sigma}\log\left(\frac{\left|{x}\right|}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right),

which gives us

w^{0}:=u_{\text{N}}^{0}-u_{\text{D}}^{0}=(C_{\text{N}}(t)-C_{\text{D}}(t))\log\left(\frac{\left|{x}\right|}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)=\left(g(t)r_{\Sigma}-\frac{f(t)}{\log\left(\frac{r_{\Sigma}}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)}\right)\log\left(\frac{\left|{x}\right|}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right).

Using the above notation, and from (
B.3
) and (
B.5
), we get

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon^{0}):\ \left\{\begin{aligned} \rho_{t}^{0}(t)&=\frac{\partial w^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t),\\
\frac{\partial w^{0}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)\Big|_{x\in\Gamma^{0}(t)}&=\frac{C_{\text{N}}(t)-C_{\text{D}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)},\qquad x\in\Gamma^{0}(t),\end{aligned}\right.

(B.6)

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon^{1}):\ \left\{\begin{aligned} \rho_{t}^{1}(\theta,t)&=\frac{\partial w^{1}}{\partial{r}}(x,t)+\frac{\partial^{2}w^{1}}{\partial{r}^{2}}(x,t)\rho^{1}(\theta,t),\\
\frac{\partial^{2}w^{0}}{\partial{r}^{2}}(x,t)\Big|_{x\in\Gamma^{0}(t)}&=-\frac{C_{\text{N}}(t)-C_{\text{D}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)^{2}},\quad x=\rho^{0}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta\\
\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{0}(t),\end{aligned}\right.

(B.7)

where

C_{\text{D}}(t)=\frac{f(t)}{\log\left(\frac{r_{\Sigma}}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)}>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad C_{\text{N}}(t)=g(t)r_{\Sigma}>0.

(B.8)

Let us now suppose, as mentioned earlier, that
\rho^{1}(\theta,t)=R(t)\cos k\theta
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, we consider the ansatz

u_{\text{p}}^{1}=a_{\text{p}}(t)r^{k}\cos k\theta+b_{\text{p}}(t)r^{-k}\cos k\theta,\qquad r=\left|{x}\right|,\qquad\text{p}=\text{D},\text{N},

which leads to the equivalence

u_{\text{p}}^{1}+\rho^{1}\frac{\partial u_{\text{p}}^{0}}{\partial{r}}=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad a_{\text{p}}(t)\rho^{0}(t)^{k}+b_{\text{p}}(t)\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}+R(t)\frac{C_{\text{p}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)}=0.

(B.9)

So, for the first-order term, we have

\displaystyle w^{1}

\displaystyle:=u_{\text{N}}^{1}-u_{\text{D}}^{1}=\left\{(a_{\text{N}}(t)-a_{\text{D}}(t))r^{k}+(b_{\text{N}}(t)-b_{\text{D}}(t))r^{-k}\right\}\cos k\theta,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial w^{1}}{\partial{r}}\Big|_{\Gamma^{0}(t)}

\displaystyle=k\left\{(a_{\text{N}}(t)-a_{\text{D}}(t))\rho^{0}(t)^{k-1}-(b_{\text{N}}(t)-b_{\text{D}}(t))\rho^{0}(t)^{-(k+1)}\right\}\cos k\theta.

From (
B.7
)
1
and the fact that
\rho^{1}(\theta,t)=R(t)\cos k\theta
, we get

R^{\prime}(t)=\lambda_{k}(t)R(t),

where

\lambda_{k}(t)=k\left\{(\tilde{a}_{\text{N}}(t)-\tilde{a}_{\text{D}}(t))\rho^{0}(t)^{k-1}-(\tilde{b}_{\text{N}}(t)-\tilde{b}_{\text{D}}(t))\rho^{0}(t)^{-(k+1)}\right\}-\frac{C_{\text{N}}(t)-C_{\text{D}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)^{2}},

where
\tilde{a}_{\text{p}}=a_{\text{p}}/R(t)
and
\tilde{b}_{\text{p}}=b_{\text{p}}/R(t)
.

To get the exact form of
\lambda_{k}(t)
, we need to compute for the coefficients
a_{\text{D}}(t)
,
b_{\text{D}}(t)
,
a_{\text{N}}(t)
, and
b_{\text{N}}(t)
.
To this end, we note the following implications of the boundary conditions

\displaystyle u_{\text{D}}^{1}=0\quad\text{on $\Sigma$}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\qquad a_{\text{D}}(t)r_{\Sigma}^{k}+b_{\text{D}}(t)r_{\Sigma}^{-k}=0,

(B.10)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u_{\text{N}}^{1}}{\partial\nu}=0\quad\text{on $\Sigma$}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\qquad a_{\text{D}}(t)r_{\Sigma}^{k-1}-b_{\text{D}}(t)r_{\Sigma}^{-(k+1)}=0.

Equations (
B.9
) and (
B.10
) respectively lead to a system of equations which, upon solving, provides the forms of
a_{\text{D}}
and
b_{\text{D}}
as well as
a_{\text{N}}
and
b_{\text{N}}
.
That is, we have

\begin{pmatrix}\rho^{0}(t)^{k}&\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}\\
r_{\Sigma}^{k}&r_{\Sigma}^{-k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{\text{D}}(t)\\
\tilde{b}_{\text{D}}(t)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{C_{\text{D}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)}\\
0\end{pmatrix}

and

\begin{pmatrix}\rho^{0}(t)^{k}&\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}\\
r_{\Sigma}^{k-1}&-r_{\Sigma}^{-(k+1)}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{\text{N}}(t)\\
\tilde{b}_{\text{N}}(t)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{C_{\text{N}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)}\\
0\end{pmatrix},

which would give us

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{\text{D}}(t)\\
\tilde{b}_{\text{D}}(t)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho^{0}(t)^{k}r_{\Sigma}^{-k}-\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}r_{\Sigma}^{k}}\begin{pmatrix}r_{\Sigma}^{-k}&-\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}\\
-r_{\Sigma}^{k}&\rho^{0}(t)^{k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{C_{\text{D}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)}\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{D}}(t)}\left(\frac{C_{\text{D}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)\begin{pmatrix}r_{\Sigma}^{-k}\\[5.0pt]
-r_{\Sigma}^{k}\\
\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{\text{N}}(t)\\
\tilde{b}_{\text{N}}(t)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{-\rho^{0}(t)^{k}r_{\Sigma}^{-(k+1)}-\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}r_{\Sigma}^{k-1}}\begin{pmatrix}-r_{\Sigma}^{-(k+1)}&-\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}\\
-r_{\Sigma}^{k-1}&\rho^{0}(t)^{k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{C_{\text{N}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)}\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{N}}(t)}\left(\frac{g(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)\begin{pmatrix}r_{\Sigma}^{-k}\\[5.0pt]
r_{\Sigma}^{k}\\
\end{pmatrix},

where

\displaystyle\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{D}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\rho^{0}(t)^{k}r_{\Sigma}^{-k}+\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}r_{\Sigma}^{k}=\left(\frac{r_{\Sigma}}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)^{k}-\left(\frac{\rho^{0}(t)}{r_{\Sigma}}\right)^{k},

\displaystyle\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=\rho^{0}(t)^{k}r_{\Sigma}^{-k}+\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}r_{\Sigma}^{k}=\left(\frac{\rho^{0}(t)}{r_{\Sigma}}\right)^{k}+\left(\frac{r_{\Sigma}}{\rho^{0}(t)}\right)^{k}.

Note that both of these determinants are positive because
\rho^{0}(t)<r_{\Sigma}
for all
t>0
.
Moreover, we observe that

0<\frac{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{N}}(t)}{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{D}}(t)}\longrightarrow 1,\qquad 0<\frac{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{D}}(t)}{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{N}}(t)}\longrightarrow 1,\qquad\text{as $k\to\infty$}.

Then, finally, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{\rho^{0}(t)}\left\{\left[-\tilde{a}_{\text{D}}(t)\rho^{0}(t)^{k}+\tilde{b}_{\text{D}}(t)\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}\right]+\left[\tilde{a}_{\text{N}}(t)\rho^{0}(t)^{k}-\tilde{b}_{\text{N}}(t)\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C_{\text{D}}(t)-C_{\text{N}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{\rho^{0}(t)^{2}}\left\{\frac{-C_{\text{D}}(t)}{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{D}}(t)}\left(r_{\Sigma}^{-k}\rho^{0}(t)^{k}+r_{\Sigma}^{k}\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}\right)+\frac{g(t)}{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{N}}(t)}\left(r_{\Sigma}^{-k}\rho^{0}(t)^{k}-r_{\Sigma}^{k}\rho^{0}(t)^{-k}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C_{\text{D}}(t)-C_{\text{N}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{\frac{k}{\rho^{0}(t)^{2}}\left(C_{\text{D}}(t)\frac{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{N}}(t)}{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{D}}(t)}+g(t)\frac{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{D}}(t)}{\operatorname{{det}}^{k}_{\text{N}}(t)}\right)}_{>0}+\frac{C_{\text{D}}(t)-C_{\text{N}}(t)}{\rho^{0}(t)^{2}}.

Evidently, for large enough
k
,
\lambda_{k}(t)
is negative, for any
t\geqslant 0
.
This implies, formally, that the main system (
1
) is
well-posed
in the case of annular domains formed by concentric radially symmetric shapes.

Appendix C
Change of variables

For the benefit of the reader, we provide here the computation of the transformed domain.
We focus on rewriting the main equations for the variable
u_{\text{N}}
(the fourth to sixth equations in (
1
)).
For the case
u_{\text{D}}
, the computations are similar.
So, consider a smooth function
u_{\text{N}}(x,t)
,
x\in\Omega(t)
, and
U_{\text{N}}(y,t)
,
y\in\Omega
, where
y=Y(x,t)
.
Then,
x=Y^{-1}(y,t)
), and we have

\displaystyle u_{\text{N}}(x,t)=U_{\text{N}}(y,t)=u_{\text{N}}(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)=:u_{\text{N}}\circ Y^{-1}(y,t).

(C.1)

Let us introduce the notation
x:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
y:=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so that
Y^{-1}(y,t):=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})^{\top}(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For clarity, we shortly verify here identity (
27
).

By generalized chain rule, the
m
th (
m=1,\ldots,n
) column entry of
\nabla_{y}^{\top}
is computed as follows (dropping
t
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{m}}u_{\text{N}}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{k}}(y)\frac{\partial Y_{k}}{\partial x_{m}}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{k}}(y)\textsf{J}^{km}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{km}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{k}}(y).

We next focus on the transformation of the Laplace equation.
We recall that

\nabla_{x}\cdot\nabla_{x}u=\Delta_{x}u=\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{1}^{2}}+\cdots+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{d}^{2}}\right)u.

From the relation
u_{\text{N}}(x,t)=U_{\text{N}}(y,t)
, we get
\Delta_{x}u_{\text{N}}(x,t)=\Delta_{y}U_{\text{N}}(y,t)=\nabla_{y}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{y}^{\top}U_{\text{N}}(y,t)
.
Each summand
\partial^{2}u/\partial x_{k}^{2}
,
k=1,\ldots,n
, is computed by performing the following calculation of partial derivatives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}u_{\text{N}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{d}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y)\frac{\partial Y_{p}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{d}\left\{\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y)\right)}_{=:D_{1}}\frac{\partial Y_{p}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)+\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y)\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\frac{\partial Y_{p}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)\right)}_{=:D_{2}}\right\}.

The partial derivative
D_{1}
is computed as follows:

D_{1}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y)\right)=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{m}y_{p}}(y)\frac{\partial Y_{m}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{m}y_{p}}(y)\textsf{J}^{mk}.

Hence, the first summand where
D_{1}
appears can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{d}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y)\right)\frac{\partial Y_{p}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{d}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{m}y_{p}}(y)\textsf{J}^{mk}\right)\textsf{J}^{pk}.

Now, summing
k
from
1
to
d
, and after a few rearrangements, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{p=1}^{d}\sum_{m=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\textsf{J}^{pk}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\textsf{J}^{pk}\right)\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{p}},

where
{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\textsf{J}^{pk}
are the entries of the matrix
A=\textsf{J}^{-1}\textsf{J}^{-\top}
.
For the partial derivative
D_{2}
, we have the form

\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mp}\frac{\partial\textsf{J}^{pk}}{\partial y_{m}}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}=\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\frac{\partial Y_{m}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{m}}\left(\frac{\partial Y_{p}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)\right)\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y).

The following computations yield the above expression:

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\frac{\partial Y_{p}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\varphi\circ Y(x)\right)\quad\left(\varphi(y):=\left(\frac{\partial Y^{-1}_{p}}{\partial y_{k}}(y)\right)^{-1},\ \Omega\ni y=Y(x,t),\ x\in\Omega(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y_{m}}(Y(x))\frac{\partial Y_{m}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y_{m}}(y)\quad(\text{$m$-$k$: row-column entry})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{m}}\textsf{J}^{pk}.

Inserting the above expression for
D_{2}
in the second summand earlier above, and then summing
k
from
1
to
d
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{p=1}^{d}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left(\frac{\partial Y_{p}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{m=1}^{d}\sum_{p=1}^{d}\frac{\partial Y_{m}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y_{m}}(y)\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\frac{\partial\textsf{J}^{pk}}{\partial y_{m}}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}},

where
\varphi(y)
is again defined as before.
In summary, the Laplace equation
\Delta_{x}u_{\text{N}}(x,t)=0
,
x\in\Omega(t)
,
t>0
, when expressed over the fixed domain
\Omega
, has the following form:

\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{p}}+\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\frac{\partial\textsf{J}^{pk}}{\partial y_{m}}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}=0,

where
{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}
is as mentioned earlier.
For the Laplace equation
\Delta_{x}u_{\text{D}}(x,t)=0
,
x\in\Omega(t)
,
t>0
, simply replace
U_{\text{N}}
by
U_{\text{D}}
above.

We next perform the transformations of equations on the boundary.
First, it is easy to check that
U_{\text{N}}(y,t)=u_{\text{N}}(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)=0
,
y\in\Gamma
,
t>0
.
Similarly, on
\Sigma
, we can write
g(x,t)
(
x\in\Sigma
) as
g(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)
.
Since
{\tilde{\rho}}(x,t)\equiv 0
on
\Sigma
, we simply write the function
g(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)
as
g(y,t)
.
Now, let us rewrite the expression
\nabla_{x}u_{\text{N}}(x,t)\cdot\nu(x,t)
, where
x\in\partial\Omega(t)
, in terms of
U_{\text{N}}(y,t)
on the fixed boundary
\partial\Omega
.
This is done as follows (hereafter, we occasionally drop
t
and write
\nu_{\circ}:=\nu_{\circ}(y)
for convenience):

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}u_{\text{N}}(x)\cdot\nu(x)

\displaystyle=\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nabla_{y}^{\top}U_{\text{N}}(y)\cdot\frac{\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}{|\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}|}(y).

Clearly, on the fixed (interior) boundary
\Sigma
, we have

\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}U_{\text{N}}(y,t)=g(y,t),\qquad y\in\Sigma,\ t>0.

In the same manner, we obtain the following transformation of boundary conditions for the variable
u_{\text{D}}

U_{\text{D}}(y,t)=u_{\text{D}}(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)=0,\qquad y\in\Gamma,\ t>0,

and, on
\Sigma
, we can write
f(x,t)
(
x\in\Sigma
) as
f(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)
.
Again, because
{\tilde{\rho}}(x,t)\equiv 0
on
\Sigma
, we simply write the function
f(Y^{-1}(y,t),t)
as
f(y,t)
.
Moreover, from
\Gamma(t)
to
\Gamma
, we have the following sequence of computations and transformations with regards to the equation for the normal velocity

\displaystyle V_{n}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}(x,t)\cdot\nu(x,t)=\frac{d}{dt}x(t)\cdot\nu(x,t),

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left(y+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y){\tilde{\rho}}(y,t)\right)\cdot\nu(x,t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}{\tilde{\rho}}(y,t)\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)\cdot\frac{\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}{|\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}|}(y),\quad\left({\tilde{\rho}_{t}}(y,t):=\frac{d}{dt}{\tilde{\rho}}(y,t),\ y\in\Gamma,\ t>0\right).

and

\displaystyle\left(\nabla u_{\text{D}}(x,t)-\nabla u_{\text{N}}(x,t)\right)\cdot\nu(x,t)=\textsf{J}^{-\top}\left(\nabla_{y}^{\top}U_{\text{D}}(y)-\nabla_{y}^{\top}U_{\text{N}}(y)\right)\cdot\frac{\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}{|\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}|}(y),

for
x=x(t)\in\Gamma(t)
,
y\in\Gamma
, and
t>0
.
These identities, with
{\textsf{A}}:=\textsf{J}^{-1}\textsf{J}^{-\top}
, gives us

\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}{\tilde{\rho}}(y,t)=-\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)\cdot\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}(y)\right)^{-1}\left(\nabla_{y}^{\top}U_{\text{D}}(y)-\nabla_{y}^{\top}U_{\text{N}}(y)\right)\cdot{{\textsf{A}}}\nu_{\circ},

for
y\in\Gamma
,
t>0
.
Taking into account the fact that
U_{\text{N}}(y,t)=U_{\text{D}}(y,t)=0
on
\Gamma
,
t>0
,
we can equivalently write the above equation as follows:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}{\tilde{\rho}}(y,t)+B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\nu_{\circ}}}\left(U_{\text{D}}(y,t)-U_{\text{N}}(y,t)\right)=0,\qquad(y\in\Gamma,\ t>0),

where
B_{\tilde{\rho}}:=B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}(y)=\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)\cdot\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}(y)\right)^{-1}\left(\nu_{\circ}\cdot{{\textsf{A}}}\nu_{\circ}\right)
.
We remind that throughout the paper we shall assume that
{\tilde{\rho}}(y,t)
,
y\in\Gamma
,
t\in[0,T]
, is small enough so that
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)\cdot\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}(y)
and
|\textsf{J}^{-1}\nu_{\circ}(y)|
are strictly positive for
y\in\Gamma
, and that the map
Y^{-1}(y,t)
is invertible.

In the rest of the section, we look at how the transformed Laplace equation splits into the operators given in (
30
). The form of
\pazocal{{L}}_{\tilde{\rho}}
is already clear, so we focus on rewriting the expression

\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\frac{\partial\textsf{J}^{pk}}{\partial y_{m}}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}(y),\qquad(y\in\Omega),

(C.2)

in terms of
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)
and
{\tilde{\rho}}(y)
(again, we drop the dependence of
{\tilde{\rho}}
to
t
for convenience) to confirm the forms of
\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}
and
\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}
.
From the identity
\textsf{J}\textsf{J}^{-1}=\textsf{I}
, we have
\partial_{y}\textsf{J}\textsf{J}^{-1}+\textsf{J}\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}=0
, or equivalently,
\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}=-\textsf{J}^{-1}\left(\partial_{y}\textsf{J}\right)\textsf{J}^{-1}
.
We compute
\partial_{y}\textsf{J}
by differentiating the equation
\textsf{J}=\textsf{I}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}+(\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}){\tilde{\rho}}
with respect to
y
:

\partial_{y}\textsf{J}=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\operatorname{Hess}_{y}{\!({\tilde{\rho}})}+\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\left(\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}\right)+\operatorname{Hess}_{y}{\!(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})}{\tilde{\rho}}+(\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}},

where
\operatorname{Hess}_{y}{\!(\cdot)}
denotes the bilinear form associated with the Hessian matrix, i.e.,
\operatorname{Hess}_{y}{\!({\tilde{\rho}})}:=\operatorname{Hess}_{y}{\!({\tilde{\rho}}(y))}=\partial_{y}(\nabla^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}(y))
,
y\in\Omega
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\textsf{J}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\operatorname{Hess}_{y}{\!({\tilde{\rho}})}\textsf{J}^{-1}-\textsf{J}^{-1}\left[\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\left(\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}\right)+(\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}+\operatorname{Hess}_{y}{\!(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})}{\tilde{\rho}}\right]\textsf{J}^{-1}.

The entries of the above matrix can be computed without difficulty.
For instance, the matrix
-\textsf{J}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\operatorname{Hess}_{y}{\!({\tilde{\rho}})}\textsf{J}^{-1}

has entries (in Einstein’s notation)

-\textsf{J}^{pj}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\partial_{y_{m}}{(\nabla^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}})}\right)_{jq}\textsf{J}^{qk}\\
=-\textsf{J}^{pj}\left(N_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{m}}\left(\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{q}}\right)\right)\textsf{J}^{qk}.

For the sake of clarity, we provide below a detailed computation of the matrix
\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}
in terms of its entries.
To start, we apply the matrix inversion lemma or the so-called Sherman–Morrison–Woodbury formula
[
34
,
60
,
67
]
to obtain the following identity

\displaystyle D_{x}Y(x)

\displaystyle=\left[D_{y}Y^{-1}(y)\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left[\textsf{I}+(\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}){\tilde{\rho}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}\right]^{-1},\qquad(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}:=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y),\ {\tilde{\rho}}:={\tilde{\rho}}(y),\ y\in\Omega),

\displaystyle=\textsf{I}-\left[I+(\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}){\tilde{\rho}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}\right]^{-1}\left((\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}){\tilde{\rho}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}\right),

\displaystyle=\textsf{I}-\left[D_{y}Y^{-1}(y)\right]^{-1}\left((\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}){\tilde{\rho}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}\right),

where
\textsf{I}:=(\delta_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
(
i,j=1,\ldots,d
) denotes the
d\times d
identity matrix.
The above equations mean that

\textsf{J}^{-1}=\textsf{I}-\textsf{J}^{-1}\left((\nabla_{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}){\tilde{\rho}}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\nabla_{y}^{\top}{\tilde{\rho}}\right)=:\textsf{I}-\textsf{J}^{-1}\textsf{M},

where, of course,

\textsf{J}^{-1}=\left(\textsf{J}^{ij}\right)=(\textsf{J}_{ij})^{-1}=\left(\delta_{ij}+N_{i}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{j}}+\frac{\partial N_{i}}{\partial y_{j}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right)^{-1}.

Differentiating the above equation with respect to
y
, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}=-\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}\textsf{M}-\textsf{J}^{-1}\partial_{y}\textsf{M}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}(\textsf{I}+\textsf{M})

\displaystyle=-\textsf{J}^{-1}\partial_{y}\textsf{M}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\textsf{J}^{-1}(\partial_{y}\textsf{M})(\textsf{I}+\textsf{M})^{-1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\textsf{J}^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\textsf{J}^{-1}(\partial_{y}\textsf{M})\textsf{J}^{-1}.

We recall that the product of three matrices, say
\textsf{A}=(\textsf{A}_{ij})
,
\textsf{B}=(\textsf{B}_{ij})
, and
\textsf{C}=(\textsf{C}_{ij})
, in terms of its
jm
-th entry, is given by (using Einstein’s notation)
(\textsf{A}\textsf{B}\textsf{C})_{ij}=\textsf{A}_{ik}\textsf{B}_{kl}\textsf{C}_{lj}
.
Therefore,
\partial_{y_{i}}\textsf{J}^{-1}=-\textsf{J}^{-1}(\partial_{y_{i}}\textsf{M})\textsf{J}^{-1}
,
i=1,\ldots,d
, in terms of its entries, is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{m}}\textsf{J}^{pk}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j,q=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{pj}(\partial_{y_{m}}\textsf{M})_{jq}\textsf{J}^{qk},\qquad(\text{$p$-$k$: row-column entry}),

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j,q=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{pj}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{m}}\left(\frac{\partial{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}+{N}_{j}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{q}}\right)\textsf{J}^{qk}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j,q=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{pj}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}+\frac{\partial{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{q}}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{m}}+\frac{\partial{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{m}}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{q}}+{N}_{j}\frac{\partial^{2}{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{q}}\right)\textsf{J}^{qk}.

Inserting the above expression to (
C.2
), we get the equivalent sum

\displaystyle-\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\left\{\sum_{j,q=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{pj}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}+\frac{\partial{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{q}}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{m}}+\frac{\partial{N}_{j}}{\partial y_{m}}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{q}}\right)\textsf{J}^{qk}\right\}\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{k,m,p=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\left(\sum_{j,q=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{pj}{N}_{j}\frac{\partial^{2}{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{q}}\textsf{J}^{qk}\right)\frac{\partial U_{\text{N}}}{\partial y_{p}}\ (=:S_{1}+S_{2}).

To get the desired expression (
C.2
) (with the same notation on indices), we simply interchange the indices
k
and
m
in
S_{1}
, and for
S_{2}
, we apply the change of notations on indices:
k\leftarrow j
,
j\leftarrow p
,
p\leftarrow q
, and note that
{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\textsf{J}^{pk}
.
The resulting expression with these changes in notations finally provides the desired expansion of (
C.2
) given in (
30
) from which the forms of the operators
\pazocal{{K}}_{\tilde{\rho}}
and
\pazocal{{M}}_{\tilde{\rho}}
are made clear.

Appendix D
Computations of
\delta\textsf{A}^{(0)}
and
\delta B_{0}

In this appendix, we compute the variations
\delta\textsf{A}^{(0)}
and
\delta B_{0}
for the more general variation

\delta\pazocal{{F}}_{\rho_{0}}=\left.\frac{d}{d\lambda}\pazocal{{F}}_{\rho_{0}+\lambda({\tilde{\rho}}-\rho_{0})}\right|_{\lambda=0},

where, basically,
\rho_{0}(y,0)=0
,
y\in\Sigma
, and
\rho_{0}(y,0)=0
,
y\in\Gamma
(see
[
10
, Sec. 2]
).
In our case,
\rho_{0}\equiv 0
.
For convenience, let us first recall that the Jacobi matrix
J
has entries given by

\textsf{J}_{km}=\delta_{km}+N_{k}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{m}}+\frac{\partial N_{k}}{\partial y_{m}}{\tilde{\rho}},

and that
\textsf{J}^{km}
are, on the other hand, the entries of the inverse matrix
\textsf{J}^{-1}
which is the Jacobi matrix of the transform
Y(x,t)
, and that
\textsf{J}^{-\top}=(\textsf{J}^{-1})^{\top}
.
Here,
\delta\textsf{A}^{(0)}=\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}
,
\textsf{J}_{0}=\textsf{J}\big|_{{\tilde{\rho}}={\tilde{\rho}}_{0}}
, where
{\tilde{\rho}}_{0}
satisfies the PDE system given by (
25
) with
{\tilde{\rho}}_{0}(y,0)=0
.
Moreover, we recall the entries
{{\textsf{A}}}_{mp}:=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\textsf{J}^{mk}\textsf{J}^{pk}
of the matrix
{{\textsf{A}}}:=\textsf{J}^{-1}\textsf{J}^{-\top}
.
We shall express
\delta A
in terms of
\delta\textsf{J}
, and the computation of
\delta\textsf{A}^{(0)}
goes as follows.
First, we note that, from the identity
\textsf{J}_{0}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}=\textsf{I}
, we get

\delta(\textsf{J}_{0}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1})=\textsf{J}_{0}(\delta\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1})+(\delta\textsf{J}_{0})\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{0}}
.
So,
\delta\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}=-\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}(\delta\textsf{J}_{0})\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}
.
Hence, we have the following sequence of identities (the index
0
is dropped)

\displaystyle\delta{\textsf{A}}=\delta(\textsf{J}^{-1}\textsf{J}^{-\top})

\displaystyle=\textsf{J}^{-1}(\delta\textsf{J}^{-1})^{\top}+(\delta\textsf{J}^{-1})\textsf{J}^{-\top}

\displaystyle=\textsf{J}^{-1}[-\textsf{J}^{-\top}(\delta\textsf{J})^{\top}\textsf{J}^{-\top}]+[-\textsf{J}^{-1}(\delta\textsf{J})\textsf{J}^{-1}]\textsf{J}^{-\top}

\displaystyle=-{\textsf{A}}(\delta\textsf{J})^{\top}\textsf{J}^{-\top}-\textsf{J}^{-1}(\delta\textsf{J}){\textsf{A}}.

Here,

\delta\textsf{J}=\left.\frac{d}{d\lambda}\left\{\delta_{km}+{N}_{k}\frac{\partial(\lambda{\tilde{\rho}})}{\partial y_{m}}+\frac{\partial{N}_{k}}{\partial y_{m}}(\lambda{\tilde{\rho}})\right\}\right|_{\lambda=0}={N}_{k}\frac{\partial{\tilde{\rho}}}{\partial y_{m}}+\frac{\partial{N}_{k}}{\partial y_{m}}{\tilde{\rho}}.

So,

\delta{\textsf{A}}=-{\textsf{A}}(\nabla\otimes({\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}{\tilde{\rho}}))\textsf{J}^{-\top}-\textsf{J}^{-1}(\nabla\otimes({\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}{\tilde{\rho}}))^{\top}{\textsf{A}},

or, in terms of its entries,

\delta{\textsf{A}}_{ij}^{(0)}=-\sum_{k,m=1}\left({\textsf{A}}_{ik}^{(0)}\frac{\partial({N}_{m}{\tilde{\rho}})}{\partial y_{k}}\textsf{J}_{0}^{jm}+\textsf{J}_{0}^{im}\frac{\partial({N}_{m}{\tilde{\rho}})}{\partial y_{k}}{\textsf{A}}_{mj}^{(0)}\right).

Next, let us compute the variation
\delta B_{0}
.
To do so, let us first compute the variations
\delta(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ})
and
\delta\left(\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}/(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ})\right)
, and note that
\delta\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\perp\nu_{\circ}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\perp\delta\nu_{\circ}
:

\displaystyle\delta(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ})

\displaystyle=\delta\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\left(\delta\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}+\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\delta\nu_{\circ}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}(\delta\textsf{J})\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\right)\nu_{\circ}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i,k,m,j=1}\textsf{J}_{0}^{ki}\nu_{0k}\frac{\partial(N_{i}{\tilde{\rho}})}{\partial y_{j}}\textsf{J}_{0}^{jm}N_{m}

\displaystyle=-\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes({\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}{\tilde{\rho}}))^{\top}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}.

Therefore, we have the following computations

\displaystyle\delta\left(\frac{\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}-\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\frac{\delta(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ})}{(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left[-\textsf{A}^{(0)}(\nabla\otimes({\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}{\tilde{\rho}}))\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}-\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}(\nabla\otimes({\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}{\tilde{\rho}}))^{\top}\textsf{A}^{(0)}\right]\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\frac{\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes({\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}{\tilde{\rho}}))^{\top}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ})^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}-\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\frac{\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}+\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\frac{\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left[-\frac{\textsf{A}^{(0)}(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}-\frac{\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left.\frac{\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\right)^{2}}\left(\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\right)\right]{\tilde{\rho}}.

Now, from (
29
), we recall that

B_{\tilde{\rho}}:=B_{{\tilde{\rho}}}(y)=\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}(y)\right)^{-1}\left(\nu_{\circ}(y)\cdot{{\textsf{A}}}\nu_{\circ}(y)\right),\qquad y\in\Gamma.

So, we have the following calculations

\displaystyle\delta\left(\frac{\nu_{\circ}\cdot\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\nu_{\circ}\cdot\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}-\nu_{\circ}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\frac{\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}+\nu_{\circ}\cdot\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\frac{\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left[-\frac{\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}-\frac{\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left.\frac{\nu_{\circ}\cdot\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\right)^{2}}\left(\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\right)\right]{\tilde{\rho}}

\displaystyle=-2\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}-\frac{(\nu_{\circ}\cdot\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ})}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left[-\frac{\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}-\frac{\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left.\frac{\nu_{\circ}\cdot\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\right)^{2}}\left(\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}\cdot(\nabla\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}})^{\top}\textsf{J}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\right)\right]{\tilde{\rho}}

\displaystyle=:-2\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ}\cdot\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}-\frac{(\nu_{\circ}\cdot\textsf{A}^{(0)}\nu_{\circ})}{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nu_{\circ}}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}\cdot\textsf{J}_{0}^{-\top}\nabla{\tilde{\rho}}+h(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}},\partial_{y}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}){\tilde{\rho}}.

Appendix E
Lemmata Proofs

For the benefit of the reader, we will prove some of the lemmas used in the paper here.

E.1
Proof of Lemma
2.5

In the proof we require a simple matrix inequality.
For
\textsf{A}=(a_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we define
\left\|{\textsf{A}}\right\|=\sqrt{\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}{a_{ij}^{2}}}
(i.e.,
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
denotes the usual entry-wise matrix norm).
Then, for
\textsf{A},\textsf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we have

\left\|{\textsf{A}\textsf{B}}\right\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d}a_{ik}b_{kj}\right)^{2}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d}a_{ik}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d}b_{kj}^{2}\right)=\left\|{\textsf{A}}\right\|^{2}\left\|{\textsf{B}}\right\|^{2}.

So,
\left\|{\textsf{A}\textsf{B}}\right\|\leqslant\left\|{\textsf{A}}\right\|\left\|{\textsf{B}}\right\|
.

Proof of Lemma
2.5
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in[0,1)
, and
\phi\in{{C}}_{0}^{k+\alpha}(\Omega)
.
Since
\phi\in{{C}}_{0}^{k+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we consider the case
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
without loss of generality.
To show that
\varphi\in\operatorname{Diffeo}^{k+\alpha}(\Omega,\Omega)
, we need to prove the following:

(i)

\varphi
is injective;

(ii)

\varphi
is surjective;

(iii)

\operatorname{{det}}(\nabla^{\top}\varphi)\neq 0
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.

Let us prove that
\varphi
is injective.
That is, for every
x,y\in\Omega
such that
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)
, we have
x=y
.
So, let us suppose that
x+\phi(x)=y+\phi(y)
.
Then, we have the following sequence of computations:

\displaystyle\left|{x-y}\right|=\left|{\phi(y)-\phi(x)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}\left[\phi(x+t(y-x))\right]\,dt}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\int_{0}^{1}\left[\nabla^{\top}\phi(x+t(y-x))\right](y-x)\,dt}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\left\|{\nabla^{\top}\phi(x+t(y-x))}\right\|\left|{y-x}\right|\,dt.

Because
\max_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}\left\|{\nabla^{\top}\phi(x)}\right\|<1
, then there exists
{\varepsilon_{\star}}>0
such that
\left\|{\nabla^{\top}\phi(x+t(y-x))}\right\|\leqslant{\varepsilon_{\star}}<1
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\left|{x-y}\right|=\left|{\phi(y)-\phi(x)}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant{\varepsilon_{\star}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|{y-x}\right|\,dt={\varepsilon_{\star}}\left|{y-x}\right|.

This implies that
(1-{\varepsilon_{\star}})\left|{y-x}\right|=0
, or equivalently,
y=x
.
This proves the injectivity of
\varphi
.

Next let us prove that
\varphi
is surjective.
That is, for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\varphi(x)=y
.
Let us define the map
T_{y}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
T_{y}(x):=y-\phi(x)
.
We want to solve the equation
T_{y}(x)=x
.
Note that

\left|{T_{y}(x)-T_{y}(z)}\right|=\left|{\phi(x)-\phi(z)}\right|\leqslant{\varepsilon_{\star}}\left|{x-z}\right|.

Because
{\varepsilon_{\star}}<1
, then by Contraction Mapping Theorem, there exists an
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
x=T_{y}(x)
.
This holds for arbitrary
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, proving the surjectivity of
\varphi
.

Lastly, let us show that
\operatorname{{det}}(\nabla^{\top}\varphi)\neq 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Note that
\nabla^{\top}\varphi=\textsf{I}+\nabla^{\top}\phi
, where
\textsf{I}=\textsf{I}(x)=x
is the identity matrix/operator.
If
\left\|{\nabla^{\top}\phi(x)}\right\|<1
, then
\operatorname{{det}}(\textsf{I}+\nabla^{\top}\phi)\neq 0
.
Let
\textsf{A}(x):=\nabla^{\top}\phi(x)
,
\textsf{A}\in{{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\left\|{\textsf{A}(x)}\right\|\leqslant{\varepsilon_{\star}}<1
, for all
x\in\overline{\Omega}
, and
\textsf{B}_{n}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-\textsf{A}(x))^{k}
,
\textsf{B}_{n}\in{{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
and
\textsf{B}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-\textsf{A}(x))^{k}
.
Hence,

\left\|{\textsf{B}_{n}(x)}\right\|\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left\|{\textsf{A}(x)}\right\|^{k}\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n}\varepsilon_{\star}^{k}=\frac{1-\varepsilon_{\star}^{n+1}}{1-{\varepsilon_{\star}}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-{\varepsilon_{\star}}},

and
\{\textsf{B}_{n}\}
is a Cauchy sequence in
{{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
and
\textsf{B}(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\textsf{B}_{n}(x)
.
Therefore,
\textsf{B}\in{{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\textsf{B}_{n}=\textsf{B}
in
{{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
.
These arguments show that
\textsf{B}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-\textsf{A}(x))^{n}
uniformly converges in
\overline{\Omega}
where
\textsf{B}\in{{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
and
\textsf{B}(x)=(\textsf{I}+\textsf{A}(x))^{-1}
.

Now, it follows that,

(\textsf{I}+\textsf{A}(x))\textsf{B}(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\textsf{I}+\textsf{A}(x))\textsf{B}_{n}(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\textsf{I}-(-\textsf{A}(x))^{n+1})=\textsf{I},

or equivalently,
\textsf{I}+\textsf{A}(x)=\textsf{B}^{-1}(x)
, for all
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.
Taking the determinant of both sides of this equation, together with the matrix inequality, we deduce that
\operatorname{{det}}(\nabla^{\top}\varphi)\neq 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

To conclude that
\operatorname{{det}}(\nabla^{\top}\varphi)>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We argue as follows.
Consider the set
\mathscr{O}:=\{\textsf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\mid\left\|{\textsf{A}}\right\|<1\}
.
Clearly,
\mathscr{O}
is a connected convex open set.
Let us define
F(\textsf{A}):=\operatorname{{det}}(\textsf{I}+\textsf{A})
.
We already prove that
F(\textsf{A})\neq 0
for all
\textsf{A}\in\mathscr{O}
and
F\in{{C}}^{0}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R})
.
Evidently, the zero matrix
\textsf{O}\in\mathscr{O}
and
F(\textsf{O})=\operatorname{{det}}\textsf{I}=1
.
Hence, it follows that
F(\textsf{A})>0
, for all
\textsf{A}\in\mathscr{O}
, as desired.

To finish the proof, let us note that, for every
x\in\Omega
, we have
\varphi(x)\in\Omega
.
Indeed, if
y=\varphi(x)\in\Omega^{c}
, we have
\Omega\ni x=\varphi^{-1}(y)=y\in\Omega^{c}
, which is a contradiction.
This concludes the proof.
∎

E.2
Proof of Lemma
4.2

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

We first show that for any
y\in\overline{\Omega}
, we have that
Z(y)\in\overline{\Omega(\rho)}
.
We prove this by contradiction.
So, suppose the statement is not true, then there is a point
y_{a}\in\Omega
such that
Z(y_{a})\in\Gamma(\rho)\cup\Sigma
,
\Gamma(\rho)={\mathscr{S}}(\rho)
for sufficiently small
\rho>0
(i.e.,
\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{C^{2+\alpha}(\Gamma)}<\varepsilon
where
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
).
We have the following situations:

(i)

there is a point
z_{a}\in\Omega
such that
Z(z_{a})\in\Omega(\rho)
;

(ii)

there is a point
z_{b}\in\overline{\Omega}
such that
Z(z_{b})\not\in\overline{\Omega(\rho)}
;

(iii)

there exists a curve
\mathscr{C}(z_{a},z_{b})
such that
\mathscr{C}(z_{a},z_{b})\setminus\{z_{b}\}\subset\Omega
;

(iv)

there is a point
y_{a}\in\Omega\cap\mathscr{C}(z_{a},z_{b})
such that
Z(y_{a})\in\Gamma(\rho)\cup\Sigma
.

However, we can find a point
y_{b}\in\Gamma\cup\Sigma
such that
Z(y_{b})=Z(y_{a})
because
Z
is a
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
-diffeomorphism from
\partial\Omega
onto
\partial\Omega(\rho)
.
But, as we shall show next, the map is injective, i.e., we have that
y_{b}=y_{a}
, a contradiction.

Now, let us verify that the map is one-to-one.
We need to show that for any point
y_{a},y_{b}\in\overline{\Omega}
such that
Z(y_{b})=Z(y_{a})
, it must be that
y_{b}=y_{a}
.
Let us note that for any pair of points
y_{a},y_{b}\in\overline{\Omega}
, there exists a curve
\mathscr{C}(y_{a},y_{b})\subset\overline{\Omega}
of class
{{C}}^{1}
such that
\left|{\mathscr{C}(y_{a},y_{b})}\right|\leqslant c(\Omega)|y_{b}-y_{a}|
, for some constant
c(\Omega)>0
.
Hence, the equation
Z(y_{b})=Z(y_{a})
implies that
y_{b}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y_{b})\tilde{\rho}(y_{b})=y_{a}+\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y_{a})\tilde{\rho}(y_{a})
, and we get the inequality

\left|{y_{b}-y_{a}}\right|=\left|{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y_{b})\tilde{\rho}(y_{b})-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y_{a})\tilde{\rho}(y_{a})}\right|\leqslant c(\Omega)\max_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\left|{\nabla(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{N}}(y)\tilde{\rho}(y))}\right|\left|{y_{b}-y_{a}}\right|.

By successively applying the inequality, we will get
\left|{y_{b}-y_{a}}\right|=0
, or equivalently,
y_{b}=y_{a}
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

E.3
Proof of Lemma
7.2

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

The existence result follows from
[
3
, Sec. 3, Thm. 4, p. 1270]
, where the general linear problem with the time-derivative in the boundary condition was studied.

We emphasize that the assumption of the negativity of
b_{\perp}
is a technical condition in the existence proof presented in
[
3
]
(see Equation (3.4)).
This same assumption also serves as a technical requirement in the proofs of Theorems 4.2 and 5.2 in
[
25
]
(see Remark 4.3).

For the proof of the
a priori
estimate (
53
) we apply Schauder’s method.
That is, using the estimate for the solution to the corresponding model problem in a half-space
[
46
, Chap. 3, Sec. 2]
, one first obtain an estimate of the form

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Theta\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\leqslant c\left(\left|{\Theta}\right|^{(2)}_{{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\psi_{2}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Sigma;\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\Theta}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\psi_{1}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}\right),

for some constant
c>0
.
Here we apply the interpolation inequality (see, e.g.,
[
46
, Equ. (2.1), p. 117]
or
[
31
, Chap. 6, Sec. 8]
)

\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2)}\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}\sum_{\left|{k}\right|=2}|{{D}}^{k}u|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(\alpha)}+c_{\varepsilon_{1}}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{u}\right|,

(E.1)

where
\varepsilon_{1}>0
is an arbitrary small number and
c_{\varepsilon_{1}}\to\infty
as
\varepsilon_{1}\to 0
, to
\left|{\Theta}\right|^{(2)}_{{{{\overline{\Omega}}}}}
.
We take
\varepsilon_{1}
small enough so that we will get an estimate of the form

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Theta\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\leqslant c\left(\left|{\Theta}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\psi_{1}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\psi_{2}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Sigma;\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\Theta}\right|^{\infty}_{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}\right).

(E.2)

Now, to get further with our computation, we note the following equalities for
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \left|{u}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega}}&=\sum_{\left|{j}\right|<1+\alpha}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{{D}^{j}u(x)}\right|+[u]^{(1+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega}}\\
&={{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{u}}}\right|+\sum_{\left|{j}\right|=1}{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}\left|{{D}^{j}u(x)}\right|+\sum_{\left|{j}\right|=1}\max_{x,{{\hat{x}}}\in\overline{\Omega}}\frac{\left|{{D}^{j}u(x)-{D}^{j}u(y)}\right|}{\left|{x-y}\right|^{\alpha}},\\
[{D}u]^{(\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega}}&=\sum_{\left|{j}\right|=[\alpha]}\max_{x,{{\hat{x}}}\in\overline{\Omega}}\frac{\left|{{D}^{j+1}u(x)-{D}^{j+1}u(y)}\right|}{\left|{x-y}\right|^{\alpha-[\alpha]}}\\
&=\sum_{\left|{j}\right|=[1+\alpha]}\max_{x,{{\hat{x}}}\in\overline{\Omega}}\frac{\left|{{D}^{j}u(x)-{D}^{j}u(y)}\right|}{\left|{x-y}\right|^{1+\alpha-[1+\alpha]}}\\
&\equiv[u]^{(1+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(E.3)

In above equalities, we apply the interpolation inequalities (see
[
46
, Equ. (2.1), p. 117]
)

[{D}{u}]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(\alpha)}\leqslant\varepsilon_{2}\left|{{u}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2)}+c_{\varepsilon_{2}}\left|{{u}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad[{u}]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(\alpha)}\leqslant\varepsilon_{3}\left|{{u}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(1)}+c_{\varepsilon_{3}}\left|{{u}}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)},

(E.4)

together with (
E.1
) to get an estimate for
\left|{\Theta}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}
.
We choose small enough values for
\varepsilon_{2}>0
and
\varepsilon_{3}>0
such that the right-hand side of the inequality (
E.2
) contains only the norms of known functions and the quantity
{{\max_{\overline{\Omega}}}}|\Theta(\cdot,\tau)|
.
Since
\Theta
is harmonic in
\Omega
, we can then apply the maximum principle (see, e.g.,
[
47
, Chap. I.3, III, p. 7]
) on the aforesaid norm to obtain

\left|{\Theta}\right|^{\infty}_{\overline{\Omega};\,[0,t]}\leqslant\left|{\psi_{2}}\right|^{\infty}_{\Sigma;\,[0,t]}+\left|{\Theta}\right|^{\infty}_{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}.

We next estimate
\left|{\Theta}\right|^{\infty}_{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}
.
Applying the fundamental theorem of calculus, together with the homogenous initial condition for the function
\Theta\big|_{\Gamma}
, we get

\left|{\Theta}\right|^{\infty}_{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}=\max_{0\leqslant\tau\leqslant t}\max_{\Gamma}\left|{\int_{0}^{\tau}{\frac{\partial}{\partial s}}\Theta(\cdot,s){{{d}}s}}\right|\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}{{\max_{\Gamma}}}\left|{\frac{\partial}{\partial s}}\Theta(\cdot,s)\right|{{{d}}s}.

(E.5)

Inserting this estimate to (
E.2
), and letting

{{{\Psi}}(t)}=\left|{\psi_{1}}\right|^{(1+\alpha)}_{{\Gamma;\,[0,t]}}+\left|{\psi_{2}}\right|^{(2+\alpha)}_{{\Sigma;\,[0,t]}}
, leads to

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Theta\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{(2+\alpha)}_{\overline{\Omega},\,\Gamma;\,[0,t]}\leqslant c\left({{{\Psi}}(t)}+\int_{0}^{t}{{\max_{\Gamma}}}\left|{{\frac{\partial}{\partial s}}\Theta(\cdot,s)}\right|{{{d}}s}\right).

(E.6)

Because
{{{\Psi}}(t)}
is non-negative, then we may apply Grönwall’s inequality (see, e.g.,
[
22
, Appx. 5.1, p. 498]
) to (
E.6
) deducing the desired estimate (
53
).
∎

E.4
Proof of Lemma
8.1

We provide here the proof of Lemma
8.1
.

Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

For some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open, connected, bounded set of class
{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
and
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Then,
\Omega
satisfies the
cone
condition and, in particular, the
(interior/exterior) ball
condition.
Let us suppose that, in specific,
\Omega
has cone property with the cone having the opening angle
\hat{\theta}:=\hat{\theta}(\Omega)
and height
h
.
To prove estimates (
64
) and (
65
), it is enough to show that the following inequalities are true:

\displaystyle[u]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}[u]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{c_{7}}{\varepsilon}\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)},

(E.7)

\displaystyle[u]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}[u]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{c_{8}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}.

(E.8)

We will only verify in detail inequality (
E.8
), but (
E.7
) can be shown in a similar fashion.
To this end, let us consider another cone
{\pazocal{{C}}}_{1}:={\pazocal{{C}}}_{1}(\hat{\theta},1)
with the same opening angle, but with cone height equal to
1
.
Using the interpolation inequality (
E.15
) (with
\varepsilon=1
) in Lemma
E.1
, we get

[u]_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{1}}^{(2)}\leqslant[u]_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{1}}^{(2+\alpha)}+c(d,\hat{\theta})\left|{u}\right|_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{1}}^{(0)},

(E.9)

for any function
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{{\pazocal{{C}}}}_{1})
, where
c(d,\hat{\theta})>0
is a constant that depends on
d
and
\hat{\theta}
.
To get the desired coefficients in the inequality condition, we observe that for any constant
\tilde{\mu}>0
and function
\tilde{u}(\tilde{x})=u(\tilde{\mu}x)
we have the equality

[\tilde{u}]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(l)}=\tilde{\mu}^{l}[u]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(l)},

(E.10)

for all
0\leqslant l\leqslant 2+\alpha
.
So, for
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}({\overline{\pazocal{{C}}}}_{\varepsilon})
, where the cone
{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}:={{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}(\hat{\theta},\varepsilon)
with its vertex at the origin, we apply the change of variables
\tilde{x}=x/\varepsilon
,
0<\varepsilon\leqslant h
, and consider the function
\tilde{u}(\tilde{x})=u(\varepsilon\tilde{x})=u(x)
.
Obviously,
\tilde{u}\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{{\pazocal{{C}}}}_{1})
, and (
E.9
) also holds for
\tilde{u}
.
Applying identity (
E.10
) to our case, we get

[\tilde{u}]_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}^{(2)}\leqslant[\tilde{u}]_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\tilde{c}(d,\hat{\theta})|\tilde{u}|_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}^{(0)}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad[u]_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}^{(2)}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}[u]_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{\tilde{c}(d,\hat{\theta})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left|{u}\right|_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}^{(0)},

(E.11)

for some constant
\tilde{c}(d,\hat{\theta})>0
.
Now, note that for any
x\in\Omega\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}
, we can construct a cone
{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}
with vertex
x
and such that
{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}\subset\Omega
.
Hence, using (
E.11
), we can further get the estimate

[u]_{{\pazocal{{C}}}_{\varepsilon}}^{(2)}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}[u]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{\tilde{c}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)}.

Because
x\in\Omega
is taken arbitrarily, we conclude that

[u]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2)}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}[u]_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{c_{7}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left|{u}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(0)},

for some constant
c_{7}>0
, proving (
E.8
).
Using the definition of the norm
\left|{\cdot}\right|_{\overline{\Omega}}^{(l)}
, we finally recover inequality (
65
).
The other interpolation inequality (
E.7
) from which (
64
) relies can be shown in a similar manner as above with the help of the interpolation inequality (
E.14
) in Lemma
E.1
(stated in the subsection
E.5
below).
∎

E.5
Interpolation inequalities

We justify in this subsection the interpolation inequalities used to proved Lemma
8.1
.
Similar and more general interpolation inequalities are given and proved in
[
31
, Sec. 6.8, pp. 130–136]
(see also
[
27
, Cor. 5.3.4, p. 144]
for analogous results).

Lemma E.1
.

Let
B_{r}
be a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with radius
r
and consider a function
u\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{B}_{r})
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
For any real number
\varepsilon\in(0,r]
, the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1+\alpha)}+\frac{{{c}_{d}}}{\varepsilon}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)},

(E.12)

\displaystyle[u]_{B_{r}}^{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1+\alpha)}+\frac{{{c}_{d}}}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)},

(E.13)

where
{{c}_{d}}
is a positive constant that depends only on the dimension
d
.
Moreover, if
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{B}_{r})
, then we further have

\displaystyle[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{(1+\alpha)}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{{{c}_{d}}}{\varepsilon}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)},

(E.14)

\displaystyle[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{{{c}_{d}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}.

(E.15)

Proof.

Consider a ball
B_{r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with radius
r
and let
x\in B_{r}
.
Let us choose a point in
B_{r}
, say
x_{0}
, such that
x\in B_{\varepsilon/{2}}(x_{0})\subset B_{r}
.
Over
B_{\varepsilon/{2}}(x_{0})
, we integrate
\partial_{x_{i}}u
and apply Green’s formula to obtain

\int_{B_{\varepsilon/{2}}(x_{0})}{\partial_{x_{i}}u}{\,dx}=\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon/{2}}(x_{0})}{u\cos(\nu_{\partial B_{\varepsilon/{2}}},x_{i})}{\,ds},

(E.16)

where
\nu_{\partial B_{\varepsilon/{2}}}
is the outward unit normal vector to the boundary
\partial B_{\varepsilon/{2}}
.
Because
u\in{{C}}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{B}_{r})
, then the Mean Value Theorem can be applied and it tells us that there is a point
\bar{x}\in B_{\varepsilon/{2}}
such that

\partial_{x_{i}}u(\bar{x})=\frac{1}{\left|{B_{\varepsilon/{2}}}\right|}\int_{B_{\varepsilon/{2}}(x_{0})}{\partial_{x_{i}}u}{\,dx},

from which, together with (
E.16
), we can obtain the estimate (cf.
[
31
, Equ. (2.1), p. 22]
)

\displaystyle\left|{\partial_{x_{i}}u(\bar{x})}\right|=\dfrac{1}{\left|{B_{\varepsilon/{2}}}\right|}\left|{\int_{B_{\varepsilon/{2}}(x_{0})}{\partial_{x_{i}}u}{\,dx}}\right|\leqslant\dfrac{\left|{\partial B_{\varepsilon/{2}}}\right|}{\left|{B_{\varepsilon/{2}}}\right|}\leqslant\dfrac{\dfrac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma_{f}({d}/{2})}\left(\dfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{d-1}}{\dfrac{\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma_{f}({d}/{2}+1)}\left(\dfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{d}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}=\frac{2d}{\varepsilon}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)},

where
\Gamma_{f}
denotes the gamma-function.
For
\bar{x}\neq x
, we can then make the following estimation of
\left|{\partial_{x_{i}}u(x)}\right|
:

\displaystyle\left|{\partial_{x_{i}}u(x)}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|{\partial_{x_{i}}u(x)-\partial_{x_{i}}u(\bar{x})}\right|+\left|{\partial_{x_{i}}u(\bar{x})}\right|\leqslant\frac{|\partial_{x_{i}}u(x)-\partial_{x_{i}}u(\bar{x})|}{\left|{x-\bar{x}}\right|^{\alpha}}\left|{x-\bar{x}}\right|^{\alpha}+\frac{2d}{\varepsilon}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{\alpha}[\partial_{x_{i}}u]_{B_{r}}^{(\alpha)}+\frac{2d}{\varepsilon}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}.

This verifies estimate (
E.12
).

Next, we validate (
E.13
) using (
E.12
).
For any
x,{{\hat{x}}}\in\overline{B}_{r}
,
x\neq{{\hat{x}}}
, we have either
\left|{x-{{\hat{x}}}}\right|<\varepsilon
or
\left|{x-{{\hat{x}}}}\right|\geqslant\varepsilon
which would give us the inequalities

\frac{|u(x)-u({{\hat{x}}})|}{\left|{x-{{\hat{x}}}}\right|^{\alpha}}=\left|{x-{{\hat{x}}}}\right|^{1-\alpha}\frac{|u(x)-u({{\hat{x}}})|}{\left|{x-{{\hat{x}}}}\right|}<\varepsilon^{1-\alpha}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(1)},

and

\frac{|u(x)-u({{\hat{x}}})|}{\left|{x-{{\hat{x}}}}\right|^{\alpha}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\left(\max_{x\in\Omega}|u(x)|+\max_{{{\hat{x}}}\in\Omega}|u({{\hat{x}}})|\right)=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)},

respectively.
In either case, we get the estimate

\frac{|u(x)-u({{\hat{x}}})|}{\left|{x-{{\hat{x}}}}\right|^{\alpha}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{1-\alpha}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(1)}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}.

Combining the above estimate with (
E.12
), we obtain (
E.13
).

We next prove (
E.14
) and (
E.15
) using (
E.12
) and (
E.13
), respectively.
To this end, let us assume that
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{B}_{r})
.
Then, (
E.13
) implies that

[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1+\alpha)}\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{{{c}_{d}}}{\varepsilon_{1}^{\alpha}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(1)},

(E.17)

for any
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,r]
.
Let us denote
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{2}
in (
E.12
) and use the resulting inequality to estimate the quantity
\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(1)}
in (
E.17
).
These lead us to

\left(1-{{c}_{d}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)^{\alpha}\right)[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1+\alpha)}\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{{{c}_{d}}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}^{\alpha}\varepsilon_{2}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(1)}.

(E.18)

To force a single parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,r]
in the above inequality, we apply a scaling technique.
To this end, we introduce the scaling
\varepsilon_{1}=t_{1}\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon_{2}=t_{2}\varepsilon
, where
t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,1)
and substitute these quantities to (
E.18
) to obtain

\frac{1}{t_{1}}\left(1-{{c}_{d}}\left(\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\right)^{\alpha}\right)[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1+\alpha)}\leqslant\varepsilon[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{{{c}_{d}}^{2}}{t_{1}^{1+\alpha}t_{2}\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}.

(E.19)

Now, we only need to choose the values of
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
such that
t_{1}^{-1}\left(1-{{c}_{d}}\left(t_{2}t_{1}^{-1}\right)^{\alpha}\right)=1
and denote by
{{\tilde{c}}_{d}}:={{c}_{d}}^{2}({t_{1}^{1+\alpha}t_{2}})^{-1}>0
to obtain

\varepsilon^{\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1+\alpha)}\leqslant\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+{{\tilde{c}}_{d}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}.

Using the above estimate for the quantity
\varepsilon^{\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(1+\alpha)}
in (
E.12
), and after some change of notations, we finally get (
E.14
).
Of course, the inequality holds for any
\varepsilon\in(0,r]
since we choose
t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,1)
.

We apply the same technique to prove (
E.15
) using (
E.12
) and (
E.14
).
That is, using (
E.12
) with
u\in{{C}}^{2+\alpha}(\overline{B}_{r})
, and then applying (
E.14
) to estimate
\varepsilon\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(1)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2)}\leqslant\varepsilon^{2+\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+{{c}_{d}}\varepsilon\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(1)}\leqslant(1+{{c}_{d}})\varepsilon^{2+\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+{{c}_{d}}^{2}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}.

Again, to force the constant, which is dependent only on the dimension
d
, to appear only as a coefficient of
\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}
, we consider the scaled free parameter
t\tilde{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\in(0,r]
, where
t\in(0,1)
, to obtain

\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2)}\leqslant(1+{{c}_{d}})t^{\alpha}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2+\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\left(\frac{{{c}_{d}}}{t}\right)^{2}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)}.

We choose
t\in(0,1)
such that
(1+{{c}_{d}})t^{\alpha}=1
and let
{{\tilde{c}}_{d}}:=\left({{c}_{d}}t^{-1}\right)^{2}
in order to finally get

[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2)}\leqslant\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}[u]_{B_{r}}^{(2+\alpha)}+\frac{{{\tilde{c}}_{d}}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}}\left|{u}\right|_{B_{r}}^{(0)},

which holds for all
\tilde{\varepsilon}\in(0,r]
.
This verifies the last estimate (
E.15
), completing the proof of the lemma.
∎
